## 121 (results). The one-call shell finds binary-search peak in 9 of 10 seeds; the loop-free arm finds nothing

`data/dc_search_ab_20261018/` (`scripts/run_dc_search_ab.sh`, any_peak, seeds
59000-59009, `--start-level 2`). Triage: `scripts/triage_open_spec.py ... --seed-rec`
(new flag: the engine seeds v1/v2 = 0/n when `--recursion-call-bias > 0`; without
it the Python port rejects every recursive program, which is what the first
triage pass did before the flag existed).

| arm | engine-verified | verified `call(` without `while` | fitted exponent of those |
|---|---|---|---|
| base | 9/10 | **0** | -- |
| search (`--dc-shell-search 0.15`) | 9/10 | **9 seeds, 10 programs** | 0.19 (n = 32 -> 1024, ascending and descending) |
| search_noloop | **0/10** | 0 | -- |

Smallest program, size 32, found in 6 of the 9 seeds in the same form (the other
hits are variants of the comparison and offsets):

    if ((v2 - v1) > 1) { v3 = v1 + (v2 - v1)/2;
        if (arr[v3] >= arr[v3 - 1]) call(v3, v2); else call(v1, v3); }
    else arr[0] = v1;

It is correct: invariant a[lo-1] < a[lo] (or lo = 0) and a[hi-1] >= a[hi] (or
hi = n), kept by both branches; passes the engine's adversarial check and 300
outside inputs up to n = 256 with duplicates and negatives.

**Prediction 1 (>= 1 hit in search, 0 in base): held** (9 against 0).
**Prediction 2 (noloop >= 1 hit and more than search): falsified**: 0/10, the
opposite order. Without the scalar-loop skeleton and the combinator, any_peak
stays at N = 2: the generic grammar does not reach even the linear scans in
the budget, so the loop-free arm is starved, not recursion-rich.
**Prediction 3 (no loss, tolerance -1): held** (9 against 9).

**Reading, without inflating it.** This is the first logarithmic-time program in
the project and the first time a recursive program passes where no loop does.
It is also the shell with one lucky draw: the program is the shell, minus
the randomization of four small choices (operator, neighbour side, two
offsets). The honest statement is "a generic interval-halving piece plus a
property checker lets the search find a known O(log n) algorithm in 90% of the
runs"; it is not "the search invented binary search". Binary-search peak is
textbook (LeetCode's Find Peak Element).

**Decision rule.** Prediction 1 held, so the shell stays opt-in and is
reported. Promotion needs the second problem registered above: a different
search shape on which the same piece, unchanged, finds something. Next
candidate: a sorted-array search (`contains_ordered` / lower bound) where the
halving comparison is against a key, not a neighbour. Not promoted, not
paper-ready alone.

## 121b. Second problem for the one-call shell -- predictions registered before running

The decision rule of section 121 asks for a second problem on which the same,
unchanged piece finds something. Checking the piece against the candidate I
proposed (`contains_ordered`: arr[0] = key, arr[1..] sorted ascending or
descending, output 1/0) before running shows it can not express it, which I
had not checked when proposing it: the shell compares arr[mid] only with a
NEIGHBOUR (arr[mid +- 1]), never with the key arr[0]; its base case writes an
index, not a 0/1 membership flag; and the seeded interval [0, n) contains the
key cell itself. So the test below is run as asked, but its expected outcome is
negative, and a mirror problem is added as a sanity check, not as a second
shape.

New problem `any_valley` (any index with a[i] <= its neighbours; mirror of
any_peak; `make test` 236/236). Runner: `ARMS="base search" PROBLEM=...
scripts/run_dc_search_ab.sh`, seeds 59000-59009, same settings as 121.
Hit criterion as in 121 (verified, `call(`, no `while`, exponent < 0.5).

**Predictions.**
1. contains_ordered: 0 hits in both arms (shape inexpressible; honest
   negative), search solved count not worse than base by more than 1.
2. any_valley: >= 7 of 10 hits in search, 0 in base (mirror of 121: it only
   shows the piece is not tuned to `>=`; the operator draw covers it).

**Decision rule.** any_valley does not count as a second shape. If
contains_ordered gives 0 hits, as predicted, the piece is NOT promoted; it is
reported as a peak-search piece. The registered next lever, not run here:
let the shell's comparison operand be drawn from {neighbour, arr[0]} with a
membership base case. That widens the piece toward a skeleton of binary search
and needs its own registration.

## 121b (results). contains_ordered 0 hits as predicted; any_valley 9/10 hits

`data/dc_search_ab_cord_20261018/`, `data/dc_search_ab_valley_20261018/`
(engine records: SUCCESS, adversarial all passed, `call(` and no `while`;
the fitted exponent was not re-measured for any_valley).

| problem | arm | solved | hits |
|---|---|---|---|
| contains_ordered | base | 1/10 | 0 |
| contains_ordered | search | 1/10 | 0 |
| any_valley | base | 9/10 | 0 |
| any_valley | search | 9/10 | 9 |

Prediction 1 (contains_ordered 0 hits, no loss): **held**. Prediction 2
(any_valley >= 7 hits, base 0): **held**. any_valley is the mirror of any_peak
and not a second shape: by the rule fixed above the piece is NOT promoted.

## 121c. Key-based interval shell (`--dc-shell-search-key`) -- design and predictions registered before running

The lever registered in 121b, built now. `--dc-shell-search-key P` makes a
share P of the one-call shells use "key mode": the comparison is
arr[mid] OP arr[0] (the key), the body starts with `if (v1 < 1) v1 = 1`
(the seeded interval [0, n) contains the key cell itself), and the base case
writes the membership flag `arr[0] = (arr[X] == arr[0])`, X in {v1, v2 - 1}
(the one of the five draws, operator, two offsets, base index, is the rest of
the piece, still random). Caveat, stated before the result: this puts the
key, the clamp and the flag into the piece, i.e. much of a binary-search
skeleton; a hit says the search can assemble the remaining five choices, not
that it discovered the key comparison. A correct assembly exists: op `<=`, right half
[mid, hi), left half [lo, mid), base arr[lo].

New problem `contains_ascending` (contains_ordered restricted to ascending
haystacks, because no single comparison covers both directions; `make test`
238/238). Runner arm `searchkey` = `--dc-shell-search 0.15
--dc-shell-search-key 0.5`; arms base / search / searchkey on
contains_ascending, and base / searchkey on contains_ordered as the negative
control; seeds 59000-59009, settings of 121. Hit = engine SUCCESS, adversarial
all passed, `call(` and no `while`, plus step exponent < 0.5 re-measured
on an ascending array of n = 32..1024.

**Predictions.**
1. contains_ascending: searchkey >= 1 hit of 10 (my uncertainty is large, about
   even odds: only about 1 of 32 key-mode draws is correct); base and search
   (key share 0): 0 hits.
2. contains_ordered (both directions): 0 hits in searchkey (no direction test).
3. No loss in solved count for searchkey versus base (tolerance -1 of 10).

**Decision rule.** A hit on contains_ascending, a different search shape from
the neighbour comparison, makes the piece a candidate for promotion as the
generic interval-search piece, reported with the caveat above. 0 hits closes
the interval-shell line as a peak-search piece.

## 121c (results). Key mode assembles an O(log n) membership search in 5 of 10 seeds (8 by the engine's check)

`data/dc_search_ab_casc_20261018/`, `data/dc_search_ab_cord2_20261018/`
(`ARMS` runner, seeds 59000-59009). Hit exponent measured on an ascending
array, n = 32 -> 1024, key at the end.

| problem | arm | engine-verified | seeds with a `call(`, no-`while` program | of those, fully correct outside the engine |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| contains_ascending | base | 6/10 | 0 | -- |
| contains_ascending | search (key share 0) | **2/10** | 0 | -- |
| contains_ascending | searchkey | 8/10 | **8** | **5** |
| contains_ordered | base | 0/10 | 0 | -- |
| contains_ordered | searchkey | 0/10 | 0 | -- |

Smallest program (size 40, fully correct in 5 seeds: 59001-59003, 59006, 59007; exponent 0.19):

    if ((v2 - v1) > 1) { if (v1 < 1) v1 = 1;
        v3 = v1 + (v2 - v1)/2;
        if (arr[v3] <= arr[0]) call(v3, v2); else call(v1, v3); }
    else arr[0] = (arr[v1] == arr[0]);

It is the correct assembly named in the registration (op `<=`, right half
[mid, hi), left half [lo, mid), base arr[lo]).

**Outside check** (400 ascending inputs, n up to 256, key present and absent,
duplicates, negatives): the 3 other seeds (59004, 59008, 59009) return a size-42
variant with base `arr[v2 - 1]` that is wrong on 13 of 400 inputs although it
passed the engine's adversarial set and every confirmed level. So the engine
count (8) overstates the verified count (5): a generalization gap of the
checker, same family as section 38's `cycle` false positive. That is a
finding in itself: a halving search needs boundary-key instances (key below
the minimum, above the maximum, at both ends) in the adversarial set.

**Prediction 1 (>= 1 hit in searchkey, 0 in base/search): held** (8 by the engine,
5 verified outside). **Prediction 2 (contains_ordered 0): held** (0 solved in both
arms, as the missing direction test implies). **Prediction 3 (searchkey not worse
than base by more than 1): held** (8 against 6), but the `search` arm without
key mode solved only **2 against 6**: shells that cannot express the problem
cost budget there, a loss that the merge policy would have to weigh.

**Reading.** The same shell, with the comparison operand and base-case type
drawn at random, finds a known O(log n) algorithm on a second problem with a
different search shape (against a key, not a neighbour). The caveat registered
before the run stands in full: the key, the clamp and the flag are in the
piece. The honest sentence: "a small generic interval-halving piece with random
operator, offsets and base index lets the search assemble binary search for two
different specifications". Not "discovered binary search".

**Decision.** By the rule of 121c the piece is a promotion candidate, but not
promoted: (1) the `search` arm lost 4 of 10 on contains_ascending, so a
default would need a battery run; (2) the engine's adversarial set missed a
wrong variant. Next steps, none run: add boundary-key instances to the
adversarial set of contains_ordered/ascending; battery A/B of the piece with
the key share on for the whole existing set.

## 122. Step 1 of 3 toward a readable result: adversarial haystacks of every size -- registered before running

Diagnosis of the three wrong "hits" of section 121c (seeds 59004, 59008,
59009): every failing input has a ONE-element haystack with the key absent.
`--start-level 2` means the run never trains on a one-element haystack, and
`ContainsOrderedProblem::generateAdversarialInstances` only used structural
arrays of the level's size, so the checker never saw it. It is a hole of the
start-level 2 convention, not of boundary keys in general. Fix
(`make test` 239/239): the adversarial set now also holds, for every haystack
size 1..level, the key at every position and in every gap (the existing
`addEveryPositionCases`, both directions, filtered to ascending for
contains_ascending).

Adversarial instances are diagnostic only (never used for scoring or
promotion), so the search trajectory is unchanged; only `final_status` can
differ. Protocol: rerun arm searchkey on contains_ascending, same seeds
59000-59009 and settings (`ARMS=searchkey`).

**Predictions.**
1. The three size-42 variants of 121c are now rejected by the engine
   (final_status not SUCCESS or adversarial failing), and the engine-verified
   hit seeds equal the outside-verified ones: exactly 5 (59001-59003, 59006,
   59007), assuming the trajectory is identical.
2. If the trajectory is not identical (non-determinism in threads), the
   engine hit count equals the outside-check count in the new run, whatever it is.
Falsified if the engine still accepts a program the outside check rejects.

## 122 (results). Prediction held: the engine now agrees with the outside check

`data/dc_search_ab_casc2_20261018/` (arm searchkey, contains_ascending, seeds
59000-59009): engine-verified hit seeds = 59001, 59002, 59003, 59006, 59007;
outside-verified (400 inputs) = the same five; programs the engine accepts
and the outside check rejects: **0** (121c: 3 seeds). The trajectory was
identical, as predicted. The three variants are rejected by the every-size
adversarial set. Step 1 done; the correct count for 121c is 5 of 10.

## 123. Step 2 of 3: a third search shape, the minimum of a rotated sorted array -- design registered before running

Why this problem: it is the Tutorialspoint "Easy" list item skipped in section 114
(on rotated input it is not the existing `min`: a halving search applies and
costs O(log n)). The comparison is neither a neighbour (any_peak) nor the key
(contains_ascending): it is arr[mid] against the interval's last cell
arr[hi - 1], and the answer is a value, not an index or a flag.

New problem `rotated_min`: every instance is a rotation of a strictly
increasing sequence (distinct values), output[0] = the minimum; adversarial
set: every size 1..level, negatives, scaled and uneven gaps, `make test` 241/241.
New opt-in `--dc-shell-search-end P`: a share P of the one-call shells (those
not in key mode) compares arr[mid] OP arr[hi - 1] and ends with
`arr[0] = arr[X]`, X in {v1, v2 - 1}; operator, offsets and X stay random. A
correct assembly: op `>`, right half [mid + 1, hi), left half [lo, mid + 1),
X = v1. Same caveat as 121c, now stronger: three modes mean the piece carries
a menu {neighbour, key, end} x {index, flag, value} chosen to match the
problems it is tested on. A hit says the search assembles the remaining
choices, not that it found the comparison.

**Protocol**: rotated_min, seeds 59000-59009, settings of 121,
`--start-level 2`, arms base and searchend (`--dc-shell-search 0.15
--dc-shell-search-end 0.5`). Hit = engine SUCCESS, adversarial all passed,
`call(` and no `while`, AND correct on 400 outside rotated arrays (sizes 1-256,
negatives, uneven gaps), AND step exponent < 0.5 (n = 32 -> 1024).

**Predictions.**
1. searchend: >= 1 verified hit of 10 (about even odds: 1 of 32 end-mode
   draws is right); base: 0 hits (linear min scans solve it, no `call`).
2. base is solved in >= 8/10 by linear scans, so the shell is not needed for
   correctness, only for the exponent.
3. searchend does not lose more than 1 of 10 solved versus base.

**Decision rule.** A hit means the one-call piece has assembled binary-search
shapes on three different specifications (neighbour, key, end). 0 hits would
say the piece is tuned to the first two and the claim stays at two problems.
Either way, step 3 (battery A/B with the piece on) follows.

## 123 (results). Negative: no hit on rotated_min; the claim stays at two specifications

`data/dc_search_ab_rmin_20261018/` (rotated_min, seeds 59000-59009,
`--start-level 2`, arms base and searchend).

| arm | engine-verified (SUCCESS + adversarial) | verified hits (`call(`, no `while`, correct on 400 outside rotations, exponent < 0.5) | records containing the end-shell |
|---|---|---|---|
| base | 3/10 | 0 | 0 |
| searchend (`--dc-shell-search-end 0.5`) | 2/10 | 0 | 0 |

**Prediction 1 (>= 1 verified hit): falsified.** **Prediction 2 (base solved in
>= 8/10): falsified** (3/10). **Prediction 3 (no loss beyond -1): held** (2 against 3).
No archived program of any seed contains the end-shell at all: the shell was
drawn, but no candidate with it survived to a solution, so this is a
reachability negative, not a wrong-answer one.

What the data shows about why base is so low (observed, not tested): the
SUCCESS records in base are the merge-sort skeleton (sorting puts the minimum
at arr[0]) and 7 further records have >= 5 confirmed levels but fail the
adversarial set; the 7 seeds without a SUCCESS record sit at LEVEL 2 for
90-150 size rounds. A linear min scan, the obvious solution, is not what the
search reaches on these instances. I did not diagnose this further.

**Decision.** Per the rule of section 123, the piece is tuned to the first two
specifications (neighbour, key) and the claim stays at two problems, with the
three-mode menu as the stated caveat. Step 3 (battery A/B with the piece on)
follows, run on the existing joint-battery problems.

## 124. Step 3 of 3: joint battery with the interval piece on -- design registered before running

`scripts/run_interval_piece_joint_battery.sh`: the 73 problems of the joint
battery plus the four interval-search problems (any_peak, any_valley,
contains_ascending, rotated_min, run with `--start-level 2`), seeds
60100-60109, section-103 settings per problem, `--benchmark`. Arms: base
(current default) and PIECE (`--recursion-call-bias 0.1 --dc-shell-search
0.15 --dc-shell-search-key 0.3 --dc-shell-search-end 0.3`). Solved = the
benchmark's "genuinely generalizing solution(s) kept" is >= 1.

Known confound, stated before the run: `--recursion-call-bias` itself changes
the generator (seeds v1/v2, draws `call`). If PIECE loses somewhere, a
base_rec arm (recursion seeding only) is run on those problems to split the
effect.

**Predictions.**
1. No problem of the 73 loses more than 3 of 10 (section 99 tolerance), at
   most 3 problems lose more than 1, and the aggregate solved count of PIECE
   is within 5% of base.
2. The only gains are on the interval-search problems: contains_ascending
   PIECE >= base + 1; any_peak, any_valley and rotated_min within +-1 (their
   difference is the log-time hit, not the solved count). No gain on the 73.

**Decision rule.** Default promotion needs: no problem beyond the tolerance,
aggregate not worse, and a measured reachability gain (here only
contains_ascending can supply it). Anything else stays opt-in. The merge
policy asks for a measured improvement; a log-time exponent on a subset is
evidence for the paper, not for a default.

## 124 (results). The piece costs nothing measurable and changes the selected solution on three problems

`data/interval_piece_joint_battery_20261018/` (77 problems x 2 arms x 10
seeds = 1540 runs, seeds 60100-60109, `--benchmark`; solved = benchmark keeps
>= 1 generalizing solution). `rec/` holds the confound arm (recursion seeding
only, `--recursion-call-bias 0.1`) on the six problems whose counts differ.

Totals: base 755 / 770, PIECE 757 / 770.

| problem | base | rec-only | PIECE |
|---|---|---|---|
| majority_value | 2 | 4 | 6 |
| mode | 9 | 10 | 10 |
| least_frequent_value | 9 | 10 | 10 |
| count_leaders | 10 | 10 | 8 |
| count_dominated | 10 | 10 | 9 |
| min_gap | 10 | 8 | 9 |
| any_peak, any_valley, contains_ascending, rotated_min | 10, 10, 10, 10 | -- | 10, 10, 10, 10 |

**Prediction 1 (no loss beyond tolerance): held**: the largest loss is
count_leaders -2, only one problem loses more than 1, and the aggregate is
+2. **Prediction 2 (gains only on the interval problems, contains_ascending
>= base + 1, none on the 73): falsified in both parts.** contains_ascending is
10/10 in base already, and majority_value gains +4 (2 -> 6), mode and
least_frequent_value +1. The rec-only arm splits the effect: of
majority_value's +4, +2 comes from the recursion seeding alone and +2 from
the shells; with 10 seeds none of these differences is distinguishable from
noise, and I do not claim an effect. The mechanism, if any, is untested (the
v1/v2 = 0/n seeding gives free initialised variables, as in the gnome-sort
find of the early sections).

**What did change, and is the useful result.** In the battery's own
configuration (no post-solve window, no profile ban) the fastest solution the
benchmark reports is the recursive halving program in **10/10 seeds for
any_peak, 10/10 for any_valley and 7/10 for contains_ascending** (it has
`call(`, no `while`; the benchmark labels it "O(1)" with fitted exponent
0.24, its classifier has no logarithmic class), and in 0/10 for the same
problems in base. rotated_min stays 0/10: the third-mode negative of section 123
holds under this configuration too.

**Correction to section 123.** There base solved rotated_min in 3/10, here 10/10. The
difference between the two configurations is `--post-solve-sizes 150
--profile-novelty 0.5 --profile-ban` (section 123) against none. The ban
penalises programs whose cost profile is already known, which includes the
linear min scan; this is the likely cause of the low base count, not the
problem's difficulty. Not isolated by its own arm. The same applies to
contains_ascending (base 6/10 in 121c, 10/10 here).

**Decision.** By the rule of section 124 the piece is NOT promoted to default:
there is no measured reachability gain in solved counts (the only candidate,
contains_ascending, was already 10/10). It stays opt-in
(`--recursion-call-bias 0.1 --dc-shell-search 0.15 --dc-shell-search-key 0.3
--dc-shell-search-end 0.3`) with this honest summary: no cost on the 73, and
on three interval-search specifications the reported solution becomes an
O(log n) program. Steps 1-3 are done.

## 125. Cleanup, group 1: redundant positive flags removed

Seventeen CLI flags that only set to `true` what has been the default since
sections 53-113 are gone (`--combinator-state/-nested/-frequency/-orthogonal/
-argbest/-distinct/-relax/-triple/-grid/-transform/-step`, `--follow-extended`,
`--follow-pointer-inputs`, `--distinct-aggregate`, `--arg-sentinel`,
`--scalar-loop`, `--scalar-loop-index-range`). Every `--no-...` form stays for
ablations; `--combinator-follow` stays (it also lifts the pointer-inputs gate).
The 34 scripts that passed one of them moved to `scripts/archive/`; the
commit before this cleanup is the tag `pre-cleanup`, on which they run
unchanged, so sections 47-117 remain reproducible with
`git checkout pre-cleanup`. Check: same seed, same flags, `max_subarray_sum`,
output md5 identical before and after; `make test` 241/241.

## 125b. Cleanup, group 2: six dead levers removed (and a correction to the proposal)

The proposal listed 13 selection and generation levers as dead. A usage check
before deleting showed that five of them are NOT: `--diversity-rate`,
`--crossover-rate`, `--shape-niche-rate`, `--behavior-niche-rate` and
`--speed-bonus-weight` are the diversity configuration of every long run
since section 92 (`run_profile_ban_ab.sh` still passes them), and
`--prune-generation` and `--role-typed-vars` each have an A/B of their own
whose outcome I did not re-read. They stay.

Removed (no script outside the early experiments, no adoption in
FINDINGS.md): `--annealing-temperature` (walker, pool, acceptance and stall
reset in `BeamSearch`), `--score-band-rate` (niching pass),
`--index-increment-bias`, `--bare-swap-penalty` (the swap draw is now a
fixed 0.5), `--control-flow-bias` and `--expr-mutation-rate` (generator and
mutation), plus the `index_increment_bias` test. Scripts that passed them moved
to `scripts/archive/`; `pre-cleanup2` is the tag before this change. All six
were `rng.chance()`-guarded at 0, so the default random stream is untouched:
three runs (max_subarray_sum; sorting with the five diversity flags and
`--post-solve-sizes`, `--composite-bias`; contains with `--restart-on-stall`)
give identical output on both binaries. `make test` 240/240.

## 125c. Cleanup, groups 3 and 4: four closed experiments removed, the rest kept on evidence

Group 3, removed (each closed as a negative in its own section, none
adopted): `--nested-place` and `--nested-place-option` (sections 102 and 102b,
`selectionsort` closed as indispensable; the `Place` relation and its two
draws are gone), `--nested-canonical-tests` (section 73, failed the
no-regression rule) and `--dc-shell` (section 119, the two-call shell; the
one-call `--dc-shell-search` family is separate code and stays). Four tests
went with them. Scripts that passed these flags moved to `scripts/archive/`;
`pre-cleanup3` is the tag before the change. All four draws were guarded at
their default, so the random stream is untouched: min_gap, count_unique,
sorting with the three interval-shell modes and any_peak give identical
output on both binaries. `make test` 235/235.

Kept, after reading what each section concluded instead of assuming:
- `--fill-bsearch-hole`, `--bsearch-stage`: section 69 deliberately kept them
  as research modes.
- `--fill-tsp-guard`, `--soundness-labels`, `--metamorphic`,
  `--rank-worst-scale` (group 4): sections 43-44 are a positive result, the
  guard of the TSP branch-and-bound selected from the problem's own oracle,
  and the pipeline is the only code that reproduces it.
- `--first-index`, `--prefix-split`: section 117 closed two gaps in the
  screen; their regressions are documented and attributed in 117b, so they
  stay opt-in.
- `--forbid-stmt`, `--profile-ban`, `--prune-generation`, `--role-typed-vars`:
  used by current scripts or with an A/B of their own.

## 126. PSB2, the comparable baseline -- design and predictions registered before running

Why: section 16 compared us to PSB2 only by order of magnitude (our evaluation counts against its 60M cap), on
different problems. `docs/literature.md` names PSB2 (Helmuth and Kelly, GECCO 2021) as the nearest published
non-neural benchmark: PushGP with lexicase selection, 100 runs per problem, 200 training cases (every hand-chosen
edge case, then random ones), 2,000 unseen test cases, at most 60,000,000 program executions, success = every
training and test case passes. Owner's rule: distance from LLM-guided systems is intended; closeness to comparable
non-neural work is what must be measured.

Adapter (`Psb2Problem`, `scripts/psb2_prepare.sh`, `scripts/run_psb2.sh`, `--max-executions N`, `make test`
238/238 plus three PSB2 tests): the official datasets are downloaded (edge files whole, the first ~6 MB of each
random file) and sampled by the run's seed, as PSB2 does. The training set is returned for every level, so
`--max-n 1` is one PSB2 run; the 2,000 test cases are the adversarial set, so final_status SUCCESS with all
adversarial cases passed is PSB2's definition. The reference function of each problem was checked against 70,000 to
160,000 official cases per problem, 0 mismatches. Seven of the 25 problems fit the integer-array interface without
changing their meaning (Fuel Cost, Basement, GCD, Find Pair, Coin Sums, Luhn, Paired Digits); the other 18 need
strings, floats or vector outputs and are not run. Encodings: see `Psb2Problem.hpp`.

Differences from PSB2, stated so nobody over-reads the comparison: the instruction set is Mini-C, not Push (so
no per-type stacks, but also no lists or strings); the budget is counted as candidates scored x training cases, an
upper bound on executions (failing candidates stop early); our code was designed over sections 1-125 and GCD is one
of the problems it was tuned on, so GCD is contaminated and the other six are the held-out comparison; 30 runs per
problem here, not 100 (a first pass; the confidence interval is wide, about +-18 points at 50%).

Protocol: seeds 70000-70029, current default configuration, `--max-n 1 --num-vars 7 --threads 1 --beam-width 500
--candidates 1500 --max-program-size 150 --step-limit 20000 --max-executions 60000000`, wall cap 900 s as a safety.

PushGP + lexicase success out of 100 (PSB2 table): Fuel Cost 50, Find Pair 4, GCD 8, Basement 1, Coin Sums 2, Paired
Digits 8, Luhn 0.

**Predictions.**
1. GCD >= 25/30 (the contaminated problem; PushGP 8/100).
2. Fuel Cost and Paired Digits >= 12/30 each (the per-term transform and adjacent-pair pieces cover them; PushGP 50 and 8).
3. Find Pair, Coin Sums and Basement <= 6/30 each (nested pair search, a four-way remainder chain and a first-index
   on a running sum are the shapes the pieces reach least).
4. Luhn 0/30 (as PushGP).
5. At least 4 of the 7 problems have >= 1 success.
**Closeness measure.** A problem is "comparable or better" when our rate >= PushGP's rate minus 20 points. We are
"close" to this line of work if that holds on at least 5 of the 7.

**Decision rule.** Report all rates with the 95% interval; a failed prediction stands as written. No tuning of the
configuration on these problems before the first full run: they are the held-out set.

## 126 (results). PSB2, first full run (30 seeds x 7 problems, default configuration)

Run: `scripts/run_psb2.sh data/psb2_run_20261018` (seeds 70000-70029, finished 23:43). Success = all 200 training and all
2,000 unseen test cases pass (the engine reports UNCONFIRMED with `--max-n 1`, so the criterion is `adversarial_passed ==
adversarial_total`). Only solved runs write a JSON record.

| problem | solved | IC95 (Wilson) | PushGP | registered prediction | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| gcd (contaminated) | 30/30 | 89-100% | 8% | >= 25/30 | held |
| paired_digits | 4/30 | 5-30% | 8% | >= 12/30 | missed |
| fuel_cost | 0/30 | 0-11% | 50% | >= 12/30 | missed |
| basement | 0/30 | 0-11% | 1% | <= 6/30 | held |
| find_pair | 0/30 | 0-11% | 4% | <= 6/30 | held |
| coin_sums | 0/30 | 0-11% | 2% | <= 6/30 | held |
| luhn | 0/30 | 0-11% | 0% | 0/30 | held |

- Prediction 5 (>= 4 of 7 with a success): missed, 2 of 7 (gcd, paired_digits).
- Closeness (rate >= PushGP - 20 points on >= 5 of 7): numerically 6 of 7 (all but fuel_cost), but the test is weak:
  PushGP is near zero on five of the seven, so it is passed by almost any method. Do not cite it as evidence of parity.
- Held-out evidence is 6 problems: 4/180 solved (paired_digits only). PushGP's published total on the same six is
  roughly 17% per problem on average, driven by fuel_cost; excluding fuel_cost we are at the same order as PushGP.
- Fuel cost is the clear gap: 0/30 against 50%. The smoke test stalled at 12/200 training cases.
- Budget caveat: runs ended at the 900 s wall limit having scored 220k-300k programs, i.e. about 44-60M executions of
  the 60M cap (70-100%), at 240-350 programs/s with scoring taking ~97% of the time. The result is therefore a little
  wall-limited, not purely execution-limited.
- Not done: one configuration only, 30 runs, no tuned arm.

### 126b. Fuel cost diagnosis (why 0/30)

- A hand-written loop `v6 = 0; v4 = 0; while (v4 < v0) { v6 = v6 + (arr[v4] / 3 - 2); v4 = v4 + 1 } arr[0] = v6` passes all
  2,000 test cases (new test `psb2_fuel_cost_known_loop_solution_passes_the_official_cases`): the language and the
  encoding are not the obstacle. (Mind `--num-vars`: v7 needs 8 variables; the engine raises 7 to 8 for this problem.)
- The one-line rule `arr[0] = arr[0] / 3 - 2` (7 nodes) already passes 50/200 training cases (46 are singletons), yet
  the search never exceeds 15/200 in any run, in the first levels (size 4: 7/200) or later.
- Tested and refuted, 4 seeds x 150 s each, all unsolved and all in 10-15/200: division drawn at 30% instead of 8%;
  a numeric-proximity partial score (1/(1+|error|)); combinator bias 0 / 0.9; step limit 2,000; beam 2,000; max size 60.
- Reading: the combinator already supplies the loop and the sum; what is missing is the element expression
  `x / 3 - 2`, a composite of two constants and two operators that the random grammar draws with probability ~1e-6.
  Not yet tested: systematic enumeration of small `x op c1 op c2` expressions as the transform piece's body.

## 127. Term-expression piece (`--term-expr P`), opt-in

Design: in a loop Sum, with probability P the term `arr[i]` is replaced by a small arithmetic expression of it drawn
uniformly from an enumerated set (x/c, x%c, x*c, x+-c, x/c1+-c2, x*c1+-c2, constants 1..maxConst; 102 forms with the
default maxConst 3). P=0 consumes no randomness: the programs found are identical to before (checked on `sum`, seed 7).
Screen (not registered, exploratory): fuel_cost, 8 seeds, 150 s, `--term-expr 0.3`: 4/8 solved, every one passing all
2,000 test cases, program size 28 (the expected loop). The default arm was 0/30 (section 126) and 0/4 in 150 s.
Registered before the full run (fuel_cost, paired_digits, luhn; matched seeds 70000-70029, default config +
`--term-expr 0.3`, 900 s): fuel_cost >= 12/30; paired_digits not below its 4/30 minus 3 (a drop of more than 3 would
mean the piece costs); luhn stays 0/30 (the piece cannot express the digit rule). Promotion to default needs the joint
battery (no per-problem drop of 2 or more, aggregate not worse) in addition to the gain; this run is only the gain.

## 128. The other unsolved PSB2 problems: which ones does a term piece reach? (diagnosis)

- Known solutions written by hand and passing all official cases (new tests): Basement (running sum, `if (sum < 0)
  { idx = i; break; }`, sentinel -1) and Find Pair (nested pair loop, target = last element, two outputs, `v1 = v0` to
  leave the outer loop). Both are expressible in Mini-C; the language and the encoding are not the obstacle.
- Best training scores of the first run (seed 70000, /200): fuel_cost 15, paired_digits 88, basement 120, find_pair 108,
  luhn 28, coin_sums 6. The high ones are not close: with a 50 s single run, a size-2 program already scores 77 on
  basement (and 51 on find_pair) because the encoding rewards leaving `arr[1..]` untouched, and the best program rises
  only to 105 by size 30 without containing the prefix-sum-and-exit structure (inspected with the new
  `ALGOSEARCH_DEBUG_BEST=1`, which prints the best program at each improvement). Reading: a plateau, not proximity.
- `--first-index` and `--prefix-split` do not move basement (105/200 at 50 s, same as default); `--combinator-follow`
  gives 115. One seed each, so this is a hint, not a measurement.
- Classification: fuel_cost = missing term (`--term-expr` reaches it). Basement = missing state-and-exit pattern (a
  `Sum` with a break on a threshold is the generic candidate). Find Pair = nested pair loop with two outputs.
  Luhn = positional conditional term. Coin Sums = four chained div/mod outputs. `--term-expr` is not expected to help
  the last four. Not tested here: anything beyond the single-seed hints above.
- Caveat: these diagnostic runs overlapped the term-expr confirmation run (load 11 on 8 cores), which may slow it a bit.

### 128b. Prefix-crossing piece (`--prefix-cross P`), opt-in

- Design: on a single combinator loop, with probability P the relation is `acc = acc + elem; if (r < 0) { if (acc CMP t)
  r = i; }`, r initialised to -1 and written to arr[0]; CMP in {<, >}, t = 0 (75%) or the key. Generic: the first index
  where a running aggregate crosses a threshold. No RNG draw when P = 0 (identical programs verified against the
  previous commit's binary on `sum`, `max_subarray_sum`, `count_distinct`, seed 11, 6 programs each).
- Exploratory screen, no prediction registered (4 seeds, P=0.3, basement): 4/4 solved in 1-3 s, 2000/2000, size 36-38.
- Full run (30 seeds 70000-70029, 300 s wall, `data/psb2_prefix_cross_20261019`), prediction registered before it:
  basement >= 20/30. Result: **30/30**, all 2000/2000 (baseline 0/30; PushGP 1%). Prediction held.
- New test `prefix_cross_bias_draws_the_first_index_where_the_running_sum_crosses_a_threshold`; the two hand-written
  PSB2 solutions are tests too. 243 tests pass.

predictions of section 127:

|---|---|---|---|---|

| paired_digits | 4/30 | 2/30 | not below 1/30 | held, but a drop of 2 |
| luhn | 0/30 | 0/30 | 0/30 | held |

  both pieces on is the follow-up for paired_digits, gcd, find_pair, coin_sums and luhn.

## 130. Promotion of the term and prefix pieces; two new generic pieces for Luhn and Find Pair

- Promotion: `--term-expr` and `--prefix-cross` are now on by default at 0.3 (`--term-expr 0`, `--prefix-cross 0` restore the
  old grammar). Evidence: section 129 (joint battery 755/770 in both arms, no drop of 2 or more; PSB2 fuel_cost 0 -> 29/30,
  basement 0 -> 30/30). Open cost: paired_digits 4 -> 2/30 (not distinguishable from noise at n = 30).
- New piece `--pair-find P`: a combinator block "first pair (i < j) whose sum (75%) or difference (25%) equals a target,
  output as two cells". Target = last element (pairs over arr[0..n-2]) or the first (pairs over arr[1..n-1]); output the two
  values or (25%) the two indices into arr[0], arr[1]; a flag keeps the first pair. Generic: pair that hits a target.
- New piece `--parity-term P`: a block "acc = sum of elem(i), where on positions i % 2 == p the element goes through a
  term from the section 127 set and optionally a fold `if (t > K) t = t - K` (K in 5..9)". Generic: positional digit-checksum
  sums. Its first version, a positional variant inside the existing `Sum` relation, was too rare (about 1/720 per draw
  within a block that is itself rare): luhn stayed at 22-26/200 in 4 seeds. As a dedicated block it is drawn directly.
- Exploratory screens (no predictions registered; 150 s, default term/prefix on): find_pair with `--pair-find 0.2`: 4/4 solved,
  2000/2000, size 63. luhn with `--parity-term 0.1`: 5/8 solved, all 2000/2000, size 42. (Both were 0/30 in section 126.)
- Both flags are opt-in (0 by default, no RNG draw when 0). New test `parity_term_and_pair_find_biases_draw_their_blocks_only_when_on`; 244 tests pass.
- Registered before running: joint battery `scripts/run_parity_pair_joint_battery.sh` (base = current default vs
  `--parity-term 0.1 --pair-find 0.2`, 10 matched seeds 60100-60109, 77 problems): prediction no problem drops by 2 or more
  and the aggregate is not lower. Then a 30-seed PSB2 run of all seven problems with both flags on: prediction luhn >= 12/30,
  find_pair >= 20/30, gcd >= 28/30, fuel_cost >= 25/30, basement >= 25/30, paired_digits in 1-7, coin_sums 0/30.

### 130 (results). Joint battery and PSB2 run with `--parity-term 0.1 --pair-find 0.2`

Battery (`data/parity_pair_joint_battery_20261020`, 77 problems x 2 arms x 10 matched seeds, finished 19:33): aggregate 755 vs
755 solved (prediction "not lower": held). Differences: count_leaders 10 -> 9, **elements_twice 10 -> 8**, least_frequent_value
9 -> 10, min_gap 9 -> 10, mode 9 -> 10. Prediction "no problem drops by 2 or more": **missed** (elements_twice, drop of 2).
Reading: elements_twice swung 9 -> 10 in the section 129 battery and 10 -> 8 here, so one drop of 2 at n = 10 is within what the
seed noise produced already; but both blocks take combinator draws (pair-find 20%, positional sum 10% of the blocks), so a
small real cost on nested-loop problems cannot be excluded. By the registered rule these two pieces are NOT promoted to default.

PSB2 (`data/psb2_all_pieces_20261020`, seeds 70000-70029, 900 s, term/prefix default + the two new flags), predictions
registered before the run:

| problem | baseline (126) | this run | prediction | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| gcd (contaminated) | 30/30 | 30/30 | >= 28 | held |
| fuel_cost | 0/30 | 28/30 | >= 25 | held |
| basement | 0/30 | 30/30 | >= 25 | held |
| paired_digits | 4/30 | 2/30 | 1-7 | held |
| find_pair | 0/30 | **30/30** | >= 20 | held |
| coin_sums | 0/30 | 0/30 | 0 | held |
| luhn | 0/30 | **29/30** | >= 12 | held |

Total 119 of 210 runs solved against 34 of 210 in the baseline; 6 of 7 problems at >= 28/30 except paired_digits (2/30) and
coin_sums (0/30). Caveats: pieces designed looking at these very problems; 30 seeds, not 100; one configuration, no tuned arm.

## 131. Two more generic pieces (`--sum-if`, `--radix`), the cost of the pieces on elements_twice, and low-probability pieces

- `--sum-if P`: on a single combinator loop or an adjacent pass, relation `if (a CMP rhs) acc = acc + a` (30%: `+ b` on an
  adjacent pass); rhs = the key or the neighbour. Generic conditional sum. Screen (4 seeds, 120 s, P = 0.2): paired_digits 4/4
  solved, 2000/2000 (baseline 4/30, 2/30 with the term piece alone).
- `--radix P`: a block `v = arr[0]; cell(k) = v / ck; v = v % ck; ...; cell(d) = v` with d = 1..3 decreasing divisors in 2..30 and
  the d + 1 cells in ascending or descending order. Generic mixed-radix decomposition (greedy change-making, unit
  conversion). **Design caveat: the cap of 30 on the divisors was chosen knowing that the PSB2 coins reach 25**; the piece is
  generic in form but its range is tuned to this problem. Screen (4 seeds, 120 s, P = 0.1): coin_sums 1/4 solved (2000/2000,
  the greedy 25/10/5 decomposition), the other three at 53-90/200 (from 6/200).
- Cost of the section 130 pieces on elements_twice, 30 matched seeds 60100-60129 (`data/elements_twice_30_20261020`):
  base 29/30, with `--parity-term 0.1 --pair-find 0.2` 24/30. The drop of 10 -> 8 in the battery was therefore not just noise
  (Fisher exact on 29/30 vs 24/30, p about 0.09, an indication and not a proof). Reading: the new blocks take combinator draws.
- With all four pieces at low probability (`--pair-find 0.05 --parity-term 0.03 --radix 0.03 --sum-if 0.05`,
  `data/elements_twice_low_20261020`) elements_twice returns to **29/30**, equal to base.
- Registered before running: joint battery `scripts/run_four_pieces_joint_battery.sh` (base vs the four low-probability
  pieces, 10 matched seeds 60100-60109, 77 problems): no problem drops by 2 or more, aggregate not lower. Then PSB2, 30 seeds,
  same four low values: gcd >= 28, fuel_cost >= 25, basement >= 25, find_pair >= 15, luhn >= 10, paired_digits >= 20,
  coin_sums >= 3 (and a miss would be recorded as such).

### 131 (results). Joint battery and PSB2 with the four low-probability pieces; promotion

Battery (`data/four_pieces_joint_battery_20261020`, 77 problems x 2 arms x 10 matched seeds, finished 23:44): aggregate **759 vs
755** solved (prediction "not lower": held). Differences: elements_twice 10 -> 9, least_frequent_value 9 -> 10, mode 9 -> 10,
**majority_value 2 -> 5**. Largest drop 1 (prediction "no drop of 2 or more": held). The majority_value gain was not predicted
and is not explained; at n = 10 it may be noise, so it is not claimed as a gain.

PSB2 (`data/psb2_four_low_20261020`, 30 seeds 70000-70029, 900 s, term/prefix default + the four pieces at low P):

| problem | baseline (126) | with 6 pieces | prediction | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| gcd (contaminated) | 30 | 30 | >= 28 | held |
| fuel_cost | 0 | 28 | >= 25 | held |
| basement | 0 | 30 | >= 25 | held |
| find_pair | 0 | 30 | >= 15 | held |
| luhn | 0 | 28 | >= 10 | held |
| paired_digits | 4 | 30 | >= 20 | held |
| coin_sums | 0 | 2 | >= 3 | **missed by one** |

Total 178 of 210 runs against 34 of 210 in the baseline; 6 of 7 problems at >= 28/30; coin_sums 2/30 (PushGP 2%, so at parity).

- Promotion: the registered rule is met (battery: aggregate not lower, no drop of 2 or more; elements_twice back to 29/30 at
  these probabilities), so `--pair-find 0.05`, `--parity-term 0.03`, `--sum-if 0.05`, `--radix 0.03` are now the defaults
  (set any to 0 to restore the old grammar). 245 tests pass with the new defaults.
- Caveats that matter for any paper: the pieces were designed after looking at these very seven problems (and `--radix`'s
  divisor cap of 30 knowing the coins reach 25); 30 seeds not 100; one configuration, no tuned arm; PSB2 gcd is contaminated;
  the suite battery shows the pieces cost nothing there but does not show they help outside PSB2.

## 132. PSB2 problems not looked at: do the pieces help on unseen problems? (adapter, protocol and predictions, registered before running)

Why: sections 126-131 tuned pieces on the same seven problems they are then reported on. The honest test is on problems chosen
**after** the pieces were frozen. From the 25 PSB2 problems, those expressible without floats or free strings were screened by
reading their datasets: bouncing-balls, dice-game, snow-day, vector-distance and shopping-list need floats; bowling, camel-case,
twitter, spin-words, substitution-cipher, solve-boolean, indices-of-substring, middle-character and square-digits need strings
or variable-length outputs (leaders: a variable-length output vector, with no lossless fixed encoding). Three remain, each with a
lossless encoding (see `Psb2Problem.hpp`):

- `psb2_cut_vector`: arr = the vector; arr[0] = the length of output1 (output2 is the rest). The reference (left part
  1..n, minimum |left - right|, smallest cut on ties) agrees with all 125,491 loaded official cases.
- `psb2_fizz_buzz`: arr = {n}; arr[0] = n, or -1 / -2 / -3 for "Fizz" / "Buzz" / "FizzBuzz". Agrees with all official cases.
- `psb2_mastermind`: arr = guess ++ code (4 + 4 letters as ASCII codes); arr[0] = right colour in the wrong place,
  arr[1] = right colour in the right place. Agrees with all official cases.
Caveat: the encodings are mine (the official outputs are strings and vectors); they carry the same information, but they are not
the official format. Known-solution programs were not hand-written for these three (only the references were checked against the
official data).

Protocol: arm ON = current defaults (all six pieces: term-expr 0.3, prefix-cross 0.3, pair-find 0.05, parity-term 0.03, sum-if
0.05, radix 0.03); arm OFF = the same binary with every piece at 0. Matched seeds 70000-70029, 900 s, 60M executions, 200 training
and 2,000 unseen cases, the criterion of section 126. Runs: `scripts/run_psb2.sh` with `PROBLEMS="psb2_cut_vector psb2_fizz_buzz
psb2_mastermind"` and `EXTRA="--term-expr 0 --prefix-cross 0 --pair-find 0 --parity-term 0 --sum-if 0 --radix 0"` for OFF.

Predictions (my honest priors; the pieces were designed for other structures):
1. The pieces give no measurable gain on these three: |ON - OFF| <= 2 solved runs on each problem.
2. Absolute rates stay low in both arms: fizz_buzz <= 12/30, cut_vector <= 3/30, mastermind 0/30.
3. No harm: ON >= OFF - 2 on each problem.
If ON - OFF >= 5 on a problem, the solved programs are inspected to see which piece produced it before any claim is made. If
fizz_buzz is solved at a high rate in both arms, that says the base grammar already covers it and is reported as such.

### 132 (results). Unseen PSB2 problems: ON = OFF everywhere, and cut_vector turned out degenerate

Runs: seeds 70000-70029, 900 s, `data/psb2_unseen_{off,on}_20261021` (OFF = all six pieces at 0, same binary). Solved = every
adversarial case passed.

| problem | OFF | ON |
|---|---|---|
| psb2_cut_vector | 30/30 | 30/30 |
| psb2_fizz_buzz | 0/30 | 0/30 |
| psb2_mastermind | 0/30 | 0/30 |

Check of the cut_vector result (done because 30/30 in both arms contradicted prediction 2): every one of the 55 logged solved
programs is `arr[0] = 1;`. In the loaded official data, output1 has length 1 in **all** 125,491 random and 10 edge cases (counted
directly), so the constant program is a correct solution of the data as given. The problem is degenerate for this dataset (the
generator evidently produces vectors whose optimal cut is always after the first element); the encoding is not the cause, and my
reference agrees with the data. cut_vector therefore says nothing about the pieces or about the search, and is excluded from the
evidence. My prior "<= 3/30" was wrong because I had not looked at the output distribution.

Against the predictions: (1) |ON - OFF| <= 2 holds (0 on all three), (3) no harm holds, (4) not triggered. (2) fails only on the
degenerate cut_vector. Net: on the two non-degenerate unseen problems both arms are at 0/30, so this round gives **no evidence
either way** that the pieces generalize beyond the problems they were designed for; fizz_buzz and mastermind are simply out of reach
for both arms. For the paper this stays a limitation. A real test needs unseen problems that are solvable at a non-trivial rate.

## 133. Second unseen-problem test: four more PSB2 problems, screened for reachability (adapter, screen, protocol and predictions, registered before the main run)

Why: section 132 ended with no evidence (cut_vector degenerate, fizz_buzz and mastermind 0/30 in both arms). Four more PSB2
problems were added with lossless encodings (see `Psb2Problem.hpp`): `psb2_twitter` (arr[0] = length, -2 over 140; the empty tweet
has no cell and is dropped), `psb2_middle_character`, `psb2_solve_boolean` (strictly left to right, as the data shows: my first
reading, `&` before `|`, disagreed with 32% of the cases and was corrected), `psb2_leaders` (flags; the official data keeps equal
values, so "at least everything to its right"; my first strict reading disagreed with 360 cases). References agree with every
loaded official case. Not added: square-digits (outputs up to 10 digits overflow the 32-bit cells) and camel-case, bowling,
spin-words, substitution-cipher, indices-of-substring (variable-length string outputs or too far from this encoding); floats still
excluded. No known-solution programs were hand-written.

Screen (OFF arm only, seeds 71000-71004, 300 s; different seeds from the main run): twitter 0/5 (best partial 146/200),
middle_character 1/5, solve_boolean 0/5 (best 165/200), leaders 0/5 (best 53/200). So only middle_character is solved at a visible
rate; the others are near or beyond the edge. The screen used the OFF arm so the selection does not favour the pieces.

Protocol of the main run: all four problems, ON (defaults) vs OFF (all six pieces 0), matched seeds 72000-72019, 600 s, 60M
executions, 200 training and 2,000 unseen cases, success as in section 126.

Predictions:
1. middle_character is the only problem with a meaningful rate: OFF between 1/20 and 8/20.
2. The others stay <= 3/20 in both arms.
3. |ON - OFF| <= 2 on every problem (the pieces were not designed for these structures).
4. No harm: ON >= OFF - 2 on every problem.
If ON - OFF >= 4 anywhere, the solved programs are inspected for the responsible piece before any claim.

### 133 (results). Second unseen test: ON 2/20 vs OFF 0/20 on middle_character, everything else 0 in both arms

Runs: seeds 72000-72019, 600 s, `data/psb2_unseen2_{off,on}_20261021`; solved = all 2,000 unseen cases passed.

| problem | OFF | ON |
|---|---|---|
| middle_character | 0/20 | 2/20 |
| twitter | 0/20 | 0/20 |
| solve_boolean | 0/20 | 0/20 |
| leaders | 0/20 | 0/20 |

Against the predictions: (1) failed: OFF was 0/20 for middle_character (predicted 1-8; the 1/5 of the screen was luck or the
screen's 300 s/seed set). (2) held. (3) held (|ON - OFF| = 2 at most). (4) held. The rule "ON - OFF >= 4" was not triggered, but the
two ON solutions were read anyway: both start with the base-grammar core `arr[0] = arr[((v0 - 1) / 2)];` followed by dead or
bloated code (loops over unrelated variables that happen not to disturb the answer, and for even lengths the second cell passes
by the leftover `swap`/`arr[1]` writes); none uses a term-expr, prefix-cross, pair-find, parity-term, sum-if or radix block. A 2/20
vs 0/20 difference has Fisher p about 0.24, so it is noise at this size.

Net: the second round also gives **no evidence that the pieces generalize**: three of four problems are out of reach for both arms
and the fourth is at a 0-10% rate. The reachable unseen-problem pool in PSB2 is exhausted for this encoding style (floats and
free strings are excluded). Any real test needs new unseen problems that are solvable, e.g. PSB1-style problems with
self-written references.

## 134. Third unseen-problem test: ten PSB1-style problems with my own generators (adapter, protocol and predictions, registered before running)

Why: sections 132-133 found no reachable unseen PSB2 problem, so the pieces' generalization is still untested. PSB1 (Helmuth and
Spector 2015) has many integer problems, but its datasets are generated by code, so here the instance generators and references are
**mine**, written from the PSB1 descriptions (input ranges as I remember them, not checked against the original generator): a
weaker claim than the official PSB2 data, said plainly. `Psb1Problem` (see its header) has ten problems, chosen in one go before any
run and without a screen: last_index_of_zero, count_odds, negative_to_zero, vectors_summed, sum_of_squares, collatz_steps,
small_or_large, mirror_image, digits, grade. 200 training and 2,000 unseen cases per seed, success as in section 126. The references
are unit-tested by hand-computed values.

Protocol: ON (defaults, all six pieces) vs OFF (all six at 0), matched seeds 73000-73019, 300 s, 60M executions, 20 seeds per
problem per arm (400 runs). No screen: whatever rates come out are reported, including 0/20 and 20/20 problems.

Predictions (priors; the pieces were not designed for these):
1. Some problems are at ceiling or floor in both arms; the aggregate (sum over the ten problems) satisfies |ON - OFF| <= 6 of 200.
2. No harm: ON >= OFF - 2 on every problem.
3. The two arms differ by >= 4 on at most one problem. Any problem where ON - OFF >= 4 is inspected for the responsible piece
   before any claim.
4. Problems with a digit loop or a conditional sum (digits, count_odds, sum_of_squares) are where a piece could plausibly matter;
   I expect at most a small effect even there.

### 134 (results). PSB1-style test: ON = OFF = 40/200; two problems at ceiling, eight at floor

Runs: seeds 73000-73019, 300 s, `data/psb1_unseen_{off,on}_20261021`; solved = all 2,000 unseen cases passed.

| problem | OFF | ON |
|---|---|---|
| mirror_image | 20/20 | 20/20 |
| grade | 20/20 | 20/20 |
| last_index_of_zero, count_odds, negative_to_zero, vectors_summed, sum_of_squares, collatz_steps, small_or_large, digits | 0/20 each | 0/20 each |

Against the predictions: (1) held (difference 0 in total, 40 vs 40), (2) held, (3) held (no difference anywhere, so no
inspection). Prediction 4 (small effect on digits/count_odds/sum_of_squares) held trivially: all three are 0/20 in both arms.

What it shows: nothing about the pieces. Two problems are trivial for the base grammar (mirror_image, grade) and eight are out of
reach in 300 s with the PSB1 ranges: the training vectors have up to 50 items in [-1000, 1000] (the suite's own problems use
small n and small values), the best partial scores are far from 200 (last_index_of_zero 29, count_odds 49, vectors_summed 4,
sum_of_squares 8, digits 1; small_or_large 179, the closest). Those are my generators, so the floor may partly be my choice of
ranges, not an intrinsic limit; I did not tune them after seeing this (that would be fitting the test).

Net across sections 132-134: 17 problems the pieces were not designed for, none shows a difference between ON and OFF beyond
noise (largest: middle_character 2/20 vs 0/20, p about 0.24) and none shows harm. The pieces' large PSB2 gain (178/210 vs 34/210) is
specific to the seven problems they were designed for. Paper-worthiness: unchanged, still not there; the missing evidence is a set of
unseen problems that sit in the middle of the difficulty range for the base search, which neither PSB2 nor these generators supplied.

## 135. Fourth unseen-problem test: the medium version of the ten PSB1-style problems (definition and predictions, registered before running)

Why: section 134 had eight of ten problems at 0/20 in both arms, so it carried no information. The cause visible in the logs is the
size of the PSB1 ranges (vectors of up to 50 items in [-1000, 1000]). The medium version changes **only the ranges**, fixed here
before any run and without a screen of the new version: vectors of 1..8 items; last_index_of_zero in [-5, 5]; count_odds,
negative_to_zero, vectors_summed and mirror_image in [-20, 20]; sum_of_squares n in 1..10; collatz_steps n in 1..30;
small_or_large n in [-100, 100] with thresholds 10 and 20; digits n in 1..9999; grade unchanged (so it is a repeat of a ceiling
problem). Same ten problems, same references otherwise; `psb1m_<name>`; unit-tested.

Caveat: this is the second look at the same ten problems, and the ranges were adjusted after seeing the first result. That is a
choice made from the floor/ceiling pattern of the OFF arm of section 134 (not from any ON/OFF difference, which was zero), but it
is still a forking path and is reported as such. The ten problems are not new in the sense of section 132: only their size is.

Protocol: ON (defaults) vs OFF (all six pieces 0), matched seeds 74000-74019, 600 s, 60M executions, 200 training and 2,000
unseen cases, success as in section 126. No screen.

Predictions:
1. At least four of the ten problems are solved at a rate strictly between 0 and 20 in the OFF arm (the version is no longer
   at floor/ceiling). If fewer, the medium version failed its purpose and that is the result.
2. |ON - OFF| <= 2 on each problem; aggregate |ON - OFF| <= 8 of 200.
3. No harm: ON >= OFF - 2 on every problem.
4. If ON - OFF >= 4 on a problem, its solved programs are read to see which piece produced it before any claim. The most likely
   places: digits, count_odds, sum_of_squares (digit loop, conditional sum, running sum).

### 135 (results). Medium PSB1-style test: OFF 65/200, ON 62/200; only two problems in the middle range

Runs: seeds 74000-74019, 600 s, `data/psb1m_unseen_{off,on}_20261022`; solved = all 2,000 unseen cases passed.

| problem | OFF | ON |
|---|---|---|
| mirror_image | 20/20 | 20/20 |
| grade | 20/20 | 20/20 |
| last_index_of_zero | 15/20 | 14/20 |
| negative_to_zero | 10/20 | 8/20 |
| count_odds, vectors_summed, sum_of_squares, collatz_steps, small_or_large, digits | 0/20 each | 0/20 each |

Against the predictions: (1) **failed**: only two problems (last_index_of_zero, negative_to_zero) are strictly between 0 and 20
in OFF, not the four predicted; the medium version still leaves six at the floor, so it did not fully fix the problem of section
134. (2) held: the largest per-problem difference is 2 (negative_to_zero), the aggregate is 3 of 200 (65 vs 62). (3) held at its
limit: negative_to_zero ON 8 = OFF 10 - 2. (4) not triggered (no ON - OFF >= 4), so no program was read.

Reading: on the two problems where a difference could show, ON is slightly *below* OFF (1 and 2 runs), within noise (Fisher p
about 0.7 and 0.5), consistent with the small cost one expects from spending 6% to 30% of some draws on pieces that do not apply.
It is not a claim of harm. Sections 132-135 together: 27 problems the pieces were not designed for (counting the repeated ten), none with
a gain beyond noise, and the only non-zero differences point the wrong way by one or two runs. The pieces' benefit is confined to the
seven PSB2 problems they were designed for; the paper cannot claim generality from this evidence. The six medium problems still at
0/20 (count_odds, vectors_summed, sum_of_squares, collatz_steps, small_or_large, digits) are the honest next targets for the search
itself (for instance count_odds is a one-line conditional count that the base search does not reach in 600 s with 8-item vectors),
and a different kind of work from these pieces.

## 136. Why count_odds and sum_of_squares stay at 0/20 in the medium PSB1-style test (diagnosis; no code change)

Question from section 135: the two simplest unsolved problems. Findings, from the logs of seed 74000 (60 s with `ALGOSEARCH_DEBUG_BEST=1`) and from the generator source:

- **Both are expressible** (new test `psb1_medium_count_odds_and_sum_of_squares_are_expressible_in_the_grammar`: hand-written programs of
  about 15 statements pass all 2,000 unseen cases). So the failure is search reach, not the language or the encoding.
- **count_odds** (best 87/200, size 85): the beam sits on a plateau of nonsense (count of elements below the median through a double
  loop). The needed core is `if (arr[i] % 2 != 0) acc = acc + 1;`. In the combinator, `CountIf` builds the test as
  `cmp(elem, rhs)` with `rhs` the key or another element (`ProgramGenerator.cpp`, `case Relation::CountIf`): the element is
  compared raw, never under `% c`. A `% 2` test appears only inside the distinct-aggregate variant (`t % 2 == p` on a
  frequency count) and in parity-term (an `i % 2` position test). So "count the items satisfying a predicate of the item" is
  outside the combinator's CountIf family; it can only come from free mutation.
- **sum_of_squares** (best 47/200, size 37): the best program is a divisor-sum (`while (v4 <= arr[0]) if (arr[0] % v4 == 0)`), i.e. the
  scalar-loop `Test` body. The scalar-loop `Fold` body is `a = a {+, -, *} {i, X, Y, arr[i]}`: it has the loop bound `i * i <= B` but no
  `i * i` **term** in the body, so `a = a + i * i` is not in the piece. (The input is a single cell, so the combinator's v0-bounded
  loops do not apply either; the scalar loop is the only structured route and it lacks the term.)
- Both gaps are structural and generic ("a predicate or a product of the loop variable inside an existing accumulator"), not
  specific to these two names.

What is not claimed: that adding the missing forms would solve them (not run), or that it would help anywhere else. Any such change
would be designed after seeing these two problems, so it needs the same unseen-problem protocol as sections 132-135 (and those
showed that the earlier pieces did not transfer); registered as an option, not a result.

## 137. Closing the two gaps of section 136: `--count-term` and `--square-term` (opt-in), reach result, and the registered tests

Two options, both 0 by default (no RNG draw, byte-identical): `--count-term P` makes a single-loop combinator `CountIf` test a term
of the element against the key (`drawTermExpr`: x % c, x / c, x * c, x +- c, ...) instead of the raw element; `--square-term P`
lets the scalar-loop `Fold` accumulate `i * i` as its term. They were designed after seeing count_odds and sum_of_squares, so the
reach result below proves only that the gap is closed, not that the options help in general.

Reach (not a test of generality): matched seeds 74000-74009 of section 135, 300 s, defaults plus `--count-term 0.3 --square-term
0.3` (`data/psb1m_reach_20261022`), against 0/20 in section 135: sum_of_squares **10/10**, count_odds **1/10**. The solved programs
are the clean ones (`while (v1 <= arr[0]) { v2 = v2 + v1 * v1; ... }`; `if (arr[v4] % 2 != 0) v5 = v5 + 1`). sum_of_squares is
fixed; count_odds is reachable but rare (the draw needs layout Whole, a single loop, CountIf, the option, the form x % 2, the
comparator != and the key 0, a product of small probabilities).

The probability used from here on is **0.1** for both, not the 0.3 of the reach run (lesson of section 131: high P cost
elements_twice); reach at 0.1 has not been measured.

New problems, written and fixed before running anything with the options (`psb1n_<name>`, medium style): count_multiples_of_three,
even_squares, sum_of_cubes, for_loop_index, smallest4 (definitions in `Psb1Problem.hpp`; unit-tested). They are 5 problems the
options were not designed for; count_multiples_of_three shares count_odds's mechanism on purpose, so it is the one place where a
gain is expected.

Two tests, run one after the other (`scripts/run_count_square_joint_battery.sh`, then the chain below):
1. **Regression battery**, the 77 suite problems, 10 matched seeds 60100-60109, base (current defaults) vs `--count-term 0.1
   --square-term 0.1`. Rule (sections 99/131): no per-problem drop of 2 or more, aggregate not worse.
2. **Unseen set**: the 5 new problems, base vs the two options at 0.1, matched seeds 75000-75019, 300 s, 60M executions, 200
   training and 2,000 unseen cases.

Predictions (written before the runs):
- Battery: passes the rule (aggregate within +-3 of base, no drop of 2). If a drop of 2 or more appears it is reported and the
  options stay opt-in.
- Unseen: count_multiples_of_three NEW >= BASE + 2 (but at most 8/20: count_odds was only 1/10 even at P = 0.3); sum_of_cubes,
  even_squares, for_loop_index and smallest4: |NEW - BASE| <= 2 (the options do not touch them); no harm anywhere, NEW >= BASE - 2.
- If the only difference is on count_multiples_of_three, the claim is limited to "one extra predicate form closes one gap", not
  generality.

### 137 (results). Battery: 760 vs 758 with one drop of 2; unseen set: no gain on count_multiples_of_three, nothing else moves

**Battery** (`data/count_square_joint_battery_20261022`, 77 problems, seeds 60100-60109; "solved" = at least one genuinely
generalizing solution kept): base 760, `--count-term 0.1 --square-term 0.1` 758. The only problem that changed is majority_value, 5 -> 3
(a drop of 2). The prediction "aggregate within +-3" holds; "no drop of 2" **fails**, so the options stay opt-in under the rule of
sections 99/131. majority_value is noisy on this suite (section 131 had it 2 -> 5 with no explanation); a 30-seed repeat to separate
noise from cost was not run.

**Unseen set** (`data/psb1n_{base,new}_20261022`, seeds 75000-75019, 300 s; solved = 2,000/2,000):

| problem | BASE | NEW (0.1 / 0.1) |
|---|---|---|
| smallest4 | 20/20 | 20/20 |
| even_squares | 1/20 | 2/20 |
| sum_of_cubes | 1/20 | 2/20 |
| count_multiples_of_three | 0/20 | 0/20 |
| for_loop_index | 0/20 | 0/20 |

Against the predictions: the main one **failed**: count_multiples_of_three is 0/20 in both arms (predicted NEW >= BASE + 2); its best
partial scores in the NEW arm are 104-105/200, nowhere near solved. The other four satisfy |NEW - BASE| <= 2, and there is no harm
(NEW >= BASE - 2 everywhere). The few solved runs of even_squares and sum_of_cubes (1-2 of 20, in both arms) are bloated programs
(many dead loops) that pass because their training and test domains are tiny (n in 1..8 gives 8 distinct inputs for sum_of_cubes);
they are chance solutions, not evidence for or against the options.

Reading: the options close the two gaps they were built for on the two problems that motivated them (reach run: sum_of_squares 10/10 at
P = 0.3, count_odds 1/10), but at the registered P = 0.1 the same mechanism does **not** carry over to count_multiples_of_three, the
closest sibling of count_odds. The reach on count_odds was already low (1/10 at 0.3); the form needed there is one among several
dozen term forms times the comparator and the key, so at 0.1 it is rarer still. This is a fourth negative on transfer (sections
132-135 were three), and a more specific one: a piece tuned to one problem's exact form does not reach a sibling that needs a
different constant. A fix would be a narrower, higher-yield form (predicate `x % c == 0` or `!= 0` with c drawn directly, instead of the
whole term set), which is again a design made after seeing results.

Decisions: both options stay opt-in; no promotion. Paper-worthiness: unchanged.

## 138. `--count-mod`: the narrow form `arr[i] % c ==/!= 0` (opt-in), registered before running

Why: section 137's `--count-term` drew the test from the whole term set and did not reach count_multiples_of_three at P = 0.1. `--count-mod P`
(0 = off, no RNG draw) makes a single-loop `CountIf` test `arr[i] % c == 0` or `!= 0`, c drawn directly in 2..9 (one in 16 forms, instead of
one in several dozen times a comparator and a key). The c range was chosen after noticing that `maxConst` is 3 (so 4 and 5 would be
unreachable with it); the siblings below use 2, 3, 4 and 5, all inside the range, which is a coincidence I am flagging, not hiding.

Designed after seeing count_odds and count_multiples_of_three, so those two are **reach** problems, not tests. The tests are three siblings
written before the runs (`psb1n_`, vector of 1..8 items in [-20, 20]): count_evens (x % 2 == 0), count_not_multiple_of_four (x % 4 != 0),
count_mod_five_is_two (x % 5 == 2: a remainder of 2, not of 0, which `--count-mod` cannot express: a deliberate out-of-form control). They are
modular-count siblings by design, so a gain would show that the mechanism covers its family, **not** that it generalizes.

Protocol: BASE (current defaults) vs MOD (`--count-mod 0.1`), matched seeds 76000-76019, 300 s, 60M executions, 200 training and 2,000 unseen
cases; five problems (count_odds, count_multiples_of_three, count_evens, count_not_multiple_of_four, count_mod_five_is_two). If the
reach is real, a 77-problem regression battery follows (10 seeds, rule of sections 99/131); if not, the option is dropped.

Predictions:
1. Reach: count_odds MOD >= BASE + 3 and count_multiples_of_three MOD >= BASE + 3 (the form is now one draw in 16 instead of one in dozens).
2. Siblings in form: count_evens and count_not_multiple_of_four MOD >= BASE + 3.
3. Control: count_mod_five_is_two |MOD - BASE| <= 2 (the form cannot express `% 5 == 2`).
4. Absolute rates stay below 15/20 anywhere (the rest of the loop around the predicate still has to be found).
5. No harm: MOD >= BASE - 2 on every problem.

### 138 (results). `--count-mod 0.1`: BASE 0/100, MOD 63/100 on the modular-count family; control at 0/20

Runs: seeds 76000-76019, 300 s, `data/count_mod_{base,new}_20261022`; solved = 2,000/2,000.

| problem | BASE | MOD |
|---|---|---|
| count_odds (reach) | 0/20 | 18/20 |
| count_multiples_of_three (reach) | 0/20 | 18/20 |
| count_evens (sibling) | 0/20 | 18/20 |
| count_not_multiple_of_four (sibling) | 0/20 | 9/20 |
| count_mod_five_is_two (control, form not expressible) | 0/20 | 0/20 |

Against the predictions: (1) held (18 vs 0 twice), (2) held (18 and 9 vs 0), (3) held (0 vs 0), (5) held (no harm anywhere). (4) **failed**: I
expected rates below 15/20 and three problems reached 18/20; the rest of the loop around the predicate is easy once the predicate is drawn
directly. The solved programs read are the clean ones (`while (v2 < v0) { if (arr[v2] % 2 == 0) v1 = v1 + 1; ... }`, `% 4 != 0`). The lower
rate on count_not_multiple_of_four (9/20) is consistent with c = 4 being one of 8 draws against 2 and 3 being drawn as often.

Reading: the narrow form works where the wide one (section 137: 1/10 at P = 0.3, 0/20 at 0.1) did not, so the lesson of 137 was right: the draw
space, not the idea, was the problem. The control confirms the mechanism is the form: `x % 5 == 2` stays 0/20. What this is **not**: a
generalization result. All four solved problems are one family (count the items whose remainder is 0 or non-0), chosen as siblings on
purpose, and two of them motivated the option; the control is the only out-of-form problem and it does not move, exactly as designed. The
claim is "one cheap predicate form turns a 0/20 family into 15-18/20 at no cost on these five problems"; the cost elsewhere is not yet measured
(section 137's options dropped majority_value 5 -> 3 on the battery). Next: the 77-problem regression battery, registered below.

Battery prediction (registered before the run, `scripts/run_count_mod_joint_battery.sh`, seeds 60100-60109, base vs `--count-mod 0.1`): the
rule of sections 99/131 holds (no per-problem drop of 2 or more, aggregate not worse than base - 2). If it holds, `--count-mod 0.1` is promoted
to default on the grounds of sections 130/131 (a measured gain on a reachable family at no measured cost); if a drop of 2 or more appears it
stays opt-in and the drop is reported, with a 30-seed repeat of that problem.

### 138 (battery and promotion). Battery 758 vs 755, max drop 1: `--count-mod 0.1` is now default

`scripts/run_count_mod_joint_battery.sh`, 77 problems, seeds 60100-60109 (`data/count_mod_joint_battery_20261022`): base 755, `--count-mod
0.1` 758. Changed problems: count_leaders 9 -> 10, elements_twice 8 -> 10, max_gap_sorted 9 -> 10, mode 9 -> 10 (gains of 1-2), and
majority_value 5 -> 4, values_twice 10 -> 9 (drops of 1). The rule of sections 99/131 holds (no per-problem drop of 2 or more; aggregate not worse).
Note this base (755) is below the base of section 137's battery (760) with the same seeds: the runs are wall-time limited, so a battery has
about +-3 of run-to-run noise on the aggregate, and majority_value (5, 3, 4 across the three batteries) is the noisy one; the +3 here is within that
noise and is **not** claimed as a gain. The gain claimed is the 0/60 -> 45/60 of the modular-count family, with zero measured cost.

Decision: promoted to default at 0.1 (`--count-mod 0` restores the previous grammar). The suite test file still passes with the default flipped
(254 tests, run after a fresh `make test`). The four section 137 options (`--count-term`, `--square-term`) stay opt-in.
Correction: the family counts are 0/80 -> 63/80 over four problems (control 0/20 separate); an earlier line of this section said 63/100 and the INDEX 45/60, both wrong.

## 139. PSB2 integer subset with 100 seeds (current defaults), predictions registered before running

Why: `docs/claims.md` row 1 rests on 30 seeds; the literature uses 100. Same seven problems as section 126, same protocol (200 training
cases, 2,000 unseen, 900 s, 60M executions, success = every case passes), the **current defaults** with nothing tuned per problem (the six
pieces of sections 127-131 and `--count-mod 0.1`; `scripts/run_psb2.sh` with `SEEDS=100 SEED_BASE=80000`), fresh seeds 80000-80099 (not
those of section 126/131, 70000-70029). Output `data/psb2_100_20261022`.

Predictions from the 30-seed rates (section 131: gcd 30, fuel_cost 28, basement 30, find_pair 30, luhn 28, paired_digits 30, coin_sums 2):
gcd, basement, find_pair, paired_digits >= 95/100; fuel_cost and luhn between 85 and 99; coin_sums <= 15/100; total >= 600/700. The 30-seed
figures have a wide interval (fuel_cost 28/30 is 93%, 95% interval about 78-99%), so a fuel_cost or luhn result as low as 80 would be within
what those data allowed and would not be a failure of the method, only a correction of the headline.

### 139 (results). PSB2 integer subset, 100 seeds: 595/700

Runs: seeds 80000-80099, 900 s, current defaults, `data/psb2_100_20261022` (finished 04:11 after about 3.5 h); solved = 2,000/2,000 unseen cases.

| problem | solved /100 | 95% Wilson interval |
|---|---|---|
| gcd | 100 | 96-100% |
| basement | 100 | 96-100% |
| find_pair | 100 | 96-100% |
| paired_digits | 100 | 96-100% |
| fuel_cost | 91 | 84-95% |
| luhn | 90 | 83-94% |
| coin_sums | 14 | 9-22% |
| total | 595/700 | 82-88% |

Against the predictions: gcd, basement, find_pair, paired_digits >= 95: held (100). fuel_cost and luhn in 85-99: held (91, 90). coin_sums <= 15:
held, barely (14, against 2/30 at 70000-70029: the 30-seed figure understated it; 14% is inside the 30-seed interval's upper range).
Total >= 600/700: **failed** (595); the 600 was set from the 30-seed totals, where fuel_cost and luhn were 28/30 and coin_sums 2/30, and
the 100-seed rates of fuel_cost and luhn (91, 90) are a little below 93%. The three headline numbers of section 131 move as follows: fuel_cost
28/30 -> 91/100, luhn 28/30 -> 90/100, coin_sums 2/30 -> 14/100. The statement "six of seven problems at >= 93%" in `docs/claims.md` row 1 is
therefore **wrong at 100 seeds**: four are at 100%, two at 90-91%, one at 14%. The honest sentence is "four problems solved in all 100 runs, two at
about 90%, one at 14%". Row 1 of the claims table is corrected accordingly.

## 140. Why fuel_cost (9 failures of 100) and luhn (10 of 100) fail: diagnosis, and two experiments registered before running

Evidence from the 100-seed run (section 139, `data/psb2_100_20261022`):
- The failures never come close: best training scores are 48-80/200 for fuel_cost and 25-63/200 for luhn, against 200 for a solution; they use the
  whole budget (900 s; two fuel_cost runs ended earlier on the 60M-execution cap).
- The solved runs have a long tail: fuel_cost median 250 s, p90 747 s, max 877 s; luhn median 273 s, p90 730 s, max 851 s (gcd 6/23/43 s, basement
  4/11/18 s). A tenth of the runs solves in the last quarter of the budget, so some failures may be the same process cut off.
- The best programs of two failed fuel_cost seeds (80035, 80042, rerun at 90 s) both hold the shape `if (v6 % 2 == 0) { v3 = v3 / 3 + c; if (v3 > K)
  v3 = v3 - K }` inside a fold: the **positional-sum block** of section 130 (`--parity-term`, designed for luhn) with a term-expr term. It is a
  plateau attractor at ~50/200 for fuel_cost, where the parity condition is wrong.

Hypotheses and registered predictions:
- H1 (attractor): the failures of fuel_cost come from the parity-term block taking the beam. Experiment E1: fuel_cost, 100 seeds 80000-80099, 900 s,
  all defaults except `--parity-term 0`. Prediction: >= 95/100 (a rise of at least 4 over the 91 of section 139). Cost to luhn is not measured by E1
  (parity-term is the luhn piece, section 130), and the option stays default regardless; this only tests the cause.
- H2 (time): the luhn failures are the slow tail of the same process. Experiment E2: the ten failed luhn seeds (80004, 80014, 80019, 80032, 80053,
  80057, 80061, 80066, 80071, 80084), defaults, `--wall-seconds 1800` (and the execution cap raised to 120M). Prediction: at most 3 of 10 solve
  (the scores of 25-63/200 look like plateaus, not slow approaches). If 5 or more solve, the failures are mostly budget.

### 140 (results). Both predictions failed: the luhn failures are mostly budget; removing parity-term made fuel_cost worse

**E2** (`data/e2_luhn_1800_20261022`, the ten failed luhn seeds, `--wall-seconds 1800`, 120M executions): **8 of 10 solved** (prediction: at most 3).
Solve times 564, 675, 884, 915, 943, 999, 1033, 1103 s; seeds 80053 and 80066 did not. Two of the eight (564 s, 675 s) and one at 884 s solved
**inside** the original 900 s budget, where the same seeds had failed in section 139: the runs are wall-time limited and the machine load differed
(10 runs here against 8 in the batch, and other jobs), so a run's search depth at 900 s is not reproducible to the second. Consequences: (i) the
luhn failures of section 139 are mostly budget, not plateaus (the best partial score of 25-63/200 did not predict that); (ii) a 100-seed rate has
run-to-run noise from timing on top of binomial noise, which this table of section 139 does not include; (iii) the 90/100 is a statement about a 900 s
budget on this machine, and at 1800 s the rate would be about 98%.

**E1** (`data/e1_fuel_noparity_20261022`, fuel_cost, seeds 80000-80099, `--parity-term 0`): **81/100** against 91/100 with the default (prediction:
>= 95). Paired by seed: 76 solved in both, 15 only with the default, 5 only without, 4 in neither (exact McNemar p = 0.041). So the parity-term block
does not cause the fuel_cost failures; if anything it *helps* them (it is the piece that supplies `x / 3` style terms under a position
condition, and the solved programs of the default run may use it). The plateau shape seen in the failed seeds (section 140 diagnosis) was
a visited state, not the cause: removing it hurts. H1 is false, and it was a plausible-looking diagnosis from two examples, the usual way to go
wrong. The remaining fuel_cost failures are likely the same slow tail as luhn's (not tested: the nine failed fuel_cost seeds were not rerun at 1800 s).

Decisions: no change of defaults. For the paper: report the 900 s rates with the solve-time distribution (median 250 s, p90 747 s) and an
explicit budget-sensitivity statement; consider a success-versus-time curve from the existing logs.

## 141. fuel_cost and luhn at 1800 s: does the success-versus-time curve keep rising? (predictions registered before running)

Why: in section 139 the curves of fuel_cost (91/100) and luhn (90/100) are still rising at the 900 s budget (figure `paper/figures/success_vs_time`), and
E2 of section 140 solved 8 of 10 failed luhn seeds at 1800 s. Question: how much of the missing 9-10% is budget?

Runs: the same seeds 80000-80099, `scripts/run_psb2.sh` with `PROBLEMS="psb2_fuel_cost psb2_luhn" WALL=1800 MAXEXEC=120000000`, all defaults
(so the only changes from section 139 are the wall budget, doubled, and the execution cap, doubled, to keep wall time the binding limit; the two
fuel_cost runs of section 139 that stopped at the 60M cap, seeds 80097 and 80099, had stopped early). Output `data/psb2_1800_20261022`. The PSB2
cap is 60M executions, so this is a budget-sensitivity study, not a PSB2-protocol result, and is reported as such.

Predictions: fuel_cost >= 96/100 and luhn >= 95/100 at 1800 s (extrapolating E2's 8/10 and the 91/90 at 900 s, and allowing that two seeds failed in
E2 at 1800 s); the curve at 900 s of this run is within +-5 runs of section 139's (the timing noise of wall-limited runs); at least 8 of the 19 failed
runs of section 139 (9 fuel_cost, 10 luhn) solve.

### 141 (results)
