Run finished 2026-10-22 12:09 (200 runs, driver `PSB2_DONE`). Solved = 2,000/2,000 unseen cases. Analysis script: scratchpad `s141.py` (counts from `seed_*.json`, times from `seed_*.log`).

| problem | 900 s (sec. 139) | this run, solved within 900 s | this run, 1800 s | prediction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| fuel_cost | 91 | 90 | **94** | >= 96: **failed** |
| luhn | 90 | 91 | **98** | >= 95: **met** |

- Curve at 900 s within +-5 of section 139 (90 vs 91, 91 vs 90): **met**; wall-time noise is about one run.
- Failed runs of section 139 that solve now: fuel_cost 3 of 9 (80033, 80042, 80064), luhn 8 of 10 (all but 80053, 80066): 11 of 19 >= 8: **met**. No run solved in section 139 failed here.
- Three of four predictions met. The miss is fuel_cost: 94, not 96.
- Solve-time medians (solved runs): fuel_cost 264 s (p90 789), luhn 302 s (p90 853). Solves after 900 s: fuel_cost 4 (900-986 s), luhn 7 (927-1357 s).
- Remaining failures: luhn 80053 and 80066 ran the full 1800 s (budget, or truly out of reach). The six fuel_cost failures (80035, 80043, 80074, 80091, 80097, 80099) stopped at 1225, 1177, 1173, 1069, 743 and 544 s, **before** the wall budget, so they hit the 120M execution cap (or exhausted the search), not time. More wall time would not help them; a higher cap might (not tested).
- Reading: luhn's missing 10% was almost entirely budget (98/100 at 2x). fuel_cost's was about half budget (+3) and half something else (six runs end early), consistent with the parity-term finding of section 140 that fuel_cost depends on a specific shape. This is a budget-sensitivity study (cap 120M, wall 1800 s), outside the PSB2 protocol (cap 60M), and is not comparable to the published PushGP numbers.

### 141 (erratum, number sweep for the paper)

Two p-values in sections 131 and 133/134 were one-sided (or approximated) and were quoted as plain Fisher p: 2/20 vs 0/20 (middle_character) is
two-sided p = 0.49 (one-sided 0.24), and 29/30 vs 24/30 (elements_twice) is two-sided p = 0.10 (quoted 0.09). The conclusions do not change (noise
in the first, an indication in the second). The paper uses the two-sided values.

## 142. Paper revision, phase 2: out-of-sample ablation and stop reasons -- registered before running

Origin: external reviews of the paper (four independent critiques) agreed that the ablation of section 130 (34/210 -> 178/210) is in-sample (seeds 70000-70029,
the seeds on which the pieces and probabilities were developed) and that the PSB2 rates should be judged against the execution cap, not wall time.

### 142a (derived from existing logs, no new run): stop reasons and executions

`scripts/psb2_run_table.py` builds a per-run CSV (solved, seconds, scored, executions = scored x 200, stop reason) from `data/psb2_100_20261022` and
`data/psb2_1800_20261022`; the CSVs are in `paper/data/`. Findings that correct section 141r:
- The six fuel_cost failures of the 1800 s run did **not** hit the 120M cap: they scored 375,923 candidates (75M executions) and ended with "No fully correct
  program found within the given size/level limits", i.e. they **exhausted the program-size limit (150)**. The best score had sat at 48/200 since size 70. So these runs end by
  search exhaustion; more wall time or a higher cap would not help them, and a larger size limit is the untested lever.
- At 900 s, luhn's 10 failures all ended on the wall limit; fuel_cost's 9 split 7 wall, 2 execution cap; coin_sums' 86 failures: 85 on the 60M cap, 1 on the wall.
- All solved runs in the 1800 s rerun used at most 60.0M executions (fuel_cost max 59,989,000; luhn max 56,441,400). So the 94 and 98 are what the 60M cap
  alone would allow if wall time did not bind: the 1800 s rerun is much closer to the PSB2 criterion than section 141 claimed (the cap was doubled, but not needed by solved runs).

### 142b (new run): the six pieces off, 100 seeds

Seeds 80000-80099, `scripts/run_psb2.sh` with `EXTRA="--term-expr 0 --prefix-cross 0 --parity-term 0 --pair-find 0 --sum-if 0 --radix 0"`, 900 s, 60M cap, SEEDS=100 SEED_BASE=80000,
all else default (count-mod stays at its default). Output `data/psb2_pieces_off_100_20261022`. The on-arm is section 139 on the same seeds. Question: how much of 595/700 is due to the pieces on
seeds that were not used to develop them (RQ2).

Predictions: gcd >= 98/100; paired_digits 5-25; fuel_cost, basement, find_pair and luhn each <= 3; coin_sums 0; total 100-140 of 700 (30-seed baseline 34/210 = 16%, so ~113 expected).
The prediction fails if any problem except gcd and paired_digits exceeds 5, which would mean the base search is stronger than the 30-seed ablation suggested.

### 142b (results)

Run finished 2026-10-23 14:08 (700 runs, driver `PSB2_DONE`). Per-run table `paper/data/psb2_pieces_off_900s_runs.csv`. Same seeds as section 139 (on-arm).

| problem | six pieces on (sec. 139) | six pieces off | prediction (off) |
|---|---|---|---|
| gcd | 100 | 100 | >= 98: met |
| fuel_cost | 91 | 0 | <= 3: met |
| basement | 100 | 0 | <= 3: met |
| find_pair | 100 | 0 | <= 3: met |
| luhn | 90 | 0 | <= 3: met |
| paired_digits | 100 | 13 | 5-25: met |
| coin_sums | 14 | 0 | 0: met |
| total | 595 | **113** | 100-140: met |

- All seven predictions met. The out-of-sample effect (595 vs 113 of 700) matches the in-sample one (178 vs 34 of 210): the seeds are not the reason.
- No seed was solved with the pieces off and not with them on (zero discordant pairs in the other direction); exact McNemar p < 1e-4 for every problem except gcd (no difference).
- Reading: the pieces account for essentially all of the difference from the base search on these problems. Since four of the six were designed from the failure of the very problem they unlock, this
  measures how decisive the hand-designed bias is, not that the search finds the structure (RQ2). It does not separate "the idea of the piece" from "the piece is the answer".

## 143. Paper phase 2: simple baselines -- random search without selection feedback, registered before running

Question (RQ2 and the title): how much of the result is the beam search and how much the generators? Section 142b removed the six pieces from the beam search; this removes the beam's feedback.
New flag `--random-search` (`SearchConfig::randomSearch`): at each size only fresh random programs are scored (no growth or mutation of survivors, no tail enumeration, no crossover); the
beam only records the best of the step. The generators (and, in the "on" arm, the six pieces and count-mod at their defaults) are unchanged. Not run: an exhaustive bottom-up enumerator (the language is
too large to enumerate to the sizes at which these programs appear, 50-150 nodes), so "random search with pieces off" is the closest no-generator baseline.

Runs: `scripts/run_psb2.sh`, seeds 80000-80029 (30, the first 30 of the 100-seed arms of sections 139 and 142b so the comparison is paired), `--candidates 2000 --tail-enum 0 --random-search`
(2,000 fresh candidates x 150 sizes x 200 cases = 60M, the PSB2 cap), `WALL=3000` so that the execution cap, not the clock, binds. Arms: `psb2_random_on_20261023` (default pieces) and
`psb2_random_off_20261023` (`--term-expr 0 --prefix-cross 0 --parity-term 0 --pair-find 0 --sum-if 0 --radix 0`). Note: an Android emulator was using about five cores during these runs, hence the cap-bound design.

Predictions (solved of 30): random/on: gcd >= 25, basement <= 20, find_pair <= 20, fuel_cost <= 10, luhn <= 3, paired_digits <= 5, coin_sums 0; random/off: gcd >= 20, every other problem 0,
total <= 40 of 210. Expectation overall: random/on well below the beam (which has 30-seed on-arm ~ 28-30 for six problems), i.e. the beam's selection matters; random/off no better than the beam with pieces off (34 of 210).
A prediction fails in the other direction if random/on matches the beam on any problem except gcd, which would mean the selection feedback adds little there.

### 143 (results)

Runs finished 2026-10-23 17:13 (420 runs, driver `PSB2_DONE` in both). All 210 runs of each arm ended on solve or by exhausting the size limit (the 60M cap bound, not the clock). Per-run tables
`paper/data/psb2_random_on_runs.csv` and `psb2_random_off_runs.csv`. Solved of 30, seeds 80000-80029 (beam columns are the first 30 seeds of sections 139 and 142b):

| problem | beam, pieces on | beam, pieces off | random, pieces on | random, pieces off |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| gcd | 30 | 30 | 30 | 30 |
| fuel_cost | 30 | 0 | 30 | 0 |
| basement | 30 | 0 | 30 | 0 |
| find_pair | 30 | 0 | 30 | 0 |
| luhn | 27 | 0 | **30** | 0 |
| paired_digits | 30 | 7 | 30 | 0 |
| coin_sums | 6 | 0 | **7** | 0 |
| total | 183 | 37 | **187** | 30 |

Predictions: random/on gcd >= 25 **met**; basement <= 20, find_pair <= 20, fuel_cost <= 10, luhn <= 3, paired_digits <= 5, coin_sums 0 **all failed** (30, 30, 30, 30, 30, 7); the general expectation
"random/on well below the beam" **failed**. Random/off: gcd >= 20 met, others 0 met, total <= 40 met (30).
- Random search with the pieces on matches the beam (187 vs 183 of 210). Paired differences are not significant (luhn 0 vs 3 discordant, exact p = 0.25; coin_sums 1 vs 2, p = 1).
  It also needs fewer executions per solved run: median 9.2M vs 14.0M (fuel_cost), 5.6M vs 7.9M (luhn), 2.0M vs 2.3M (paired_digits), 13.6M vs 22.8M (coin_sums); 0.40M vs 0.30M for gcd/basement/find_pair (a floor of 200 x 2,000 fresh candidates per step vs the beam's).
- The solved fuel_cost program of random/on (seed 80000) is the plain sum of `arr[i]/3 - 2`: a single draw of the term-expression block. With the pieces on, the solutions are single-block draws, and the beam's selection
  adds nothing measurable on these seven problems. The pieces are what the search needs; with them off, neither the beam (37) nor random search (30, gcd alone) gets anywhere.
- Scope of the claim: this is for the seven integer PSB2 problems at 30 seeds. "Random" means fresh programs from the same generators (not uniform over ASTs). Section 102 (selection sort indispensable) and the
  skeleton ablations remain the evidence that selection matters on sorting/search problems of the suite; this run does not touch them.

## 144. Paper phase 2: fuel_cost with a larger program-size limit, registered before running

Section 142a showed that the six fuel_cost failures of the 1800 s rerun (seeds 80035, 80043, 80074, 80091, 80097, 80099) exhausted the size limit of 150 with the best score stuck at 48/200 since size 70.
Run: these six seeds, defaults, `--max-program-size 300`, `--wall-seconds 1800`, `--max-executions 120000000`, output `data/fuel_cost_size300_20261024`.
Prediction: at most 1 of 6 solves (a plateau since size 70 points to a missing structure, not missing room); if 3 or more solve, the failures were a size-limit effect and the paper's statement that
the remaining lever is the size limit holds in the strong form.

### 144 (results)

Run finished 2026-10-24 (6 runs, `--max-program-size 300`, wall 1800 s, cap 120M). Per-run table `paper/data/fuel_cost_size300_runs.csv`. **All six solve** (2,000/2,000 unseen cases), at 1,315-1,545 s and 78.7M-107.1M executions.
Prediction (at most 1 of 6): **failed**. The six failures of the 1800 s rerun were a size-limit effect, not a structural gap: the plateau at 48/200 since size 70 was not final. But every one of the six needed
more than the 60M executions of the PSB2 cap (78.7M-107.1M), so under the PSB2 criterion they remain failures: fuel_cost at 100 seeds would be 94 + 6 = 100 only with a size limit of 300 and about 1.3-1.8x the PSB2 cap and 1,300-1,550 s.
The solved programs are small (28-33 nodes in five runs), so the larger limit is not for room but for how the size schedule proceeds (not examined here).

## 145. Frozen held-out test: four problems not used in any design or transfer test -- registered before implementing

Why: the four external reviews of the paper agree that the PSB2 results are in-sample with respect to the design of the six pieces, and that the transfer tests of sections 132-135 have power only for
large effects. This is the held-out test planned as phase 3.1. Code is frozen at commit d61e125 (generators, probabilities and defaults as in sections 139-144); the only code added afterwards is
the four problem definitions (new `Psb2Problem::Kind` values and one generator), which do not touch the search.

Problems (chosen from the official suites before any run of them; none was used before):
- PSB2 square_digits (official dataset, `data/psb2/square-digits`): arr = {n, 0 x 14}; the leading cells of the output are the digits of the concatenated squares of the digits of n (the official output is a string of digits).
- PSB2 bowling (official dataset): arr = the string as codes ('-' 45, '/' 47, 'X' 88, digits 0-9 as values, as the loader already encodes digit strings); arr[0] = the score.
- PSB2 camel_case (official dataset): arr = the string as ASCII; the leading cells of the output are the characters of the converted string (empty strings dropped, as for twitter).
- PSB1 median (generator written by me from the PSB1 description: three integers in [-100, 100]; the PSB1 data are generated by code, not downloadable): arr = {a, b, c}; arr[0] = the median.
Not available as held-out: the ten PSB1-style problems (sections 134-135) and smallest4 (section 137) were used already; the other PSB1 problems need strings or floats.

Runs (all 30 seeds, 80000-80029, 900 s, 60M cap, 200 training and 2,000 unseen cases, `scripts/run_psb2.sh`): beam with the six pieces on (defaults), beam with the six pieces off, and random search with the pieces on.
That is 360 runs. Output `data/heldout_*_20261025`.

Predictions (solved of 30): (1) no transfer of the pieces: |on - off| <= 3 on every problem (a difference of 8 or more on any problem would be evidence of transfer); (2) bowling 0 and camel_case 0 in the three arms
(a frame state machine and a conditional rewrite are not any piece); (3) square_digits <= 3 in every arm; (4) median >= 10 in the beam arms (a nested-if chain over three values is reachable by the base grammar);
(5) random search with the pieces on is not better than the beam on median (by section 143 it equals the beam where pieces carry the result; here no piece applies, so selection should matter): random-on <= beam-on - 5 on median.
Reading rule: if (1) holds, the pieces are specific to the problems they were built for and the PSB2 numbers do not predict performance on new problems; if (4)/(5) hold, selection matters when no piece applies.

### 145 (results)

Runs finished 2026-10-24 (360 runs, 120 per arm, all exit 0). Per-run tables `paper/data/heldout_{beam_on,beam_off,random_on}_runs.csv` (`scripts/psb2_run_table.py` now also reads the `psb1_*` directory).
Solved of 30 (2,000/2,000 unseen cases):

| problem | beam, pieces on | beam, pieces off | random, pieces on |
|---|---|---|---|
| median (PSB1) | 30 | 30 | 0 |
| square_digits | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| bowling | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| camel_case | 0 | 0 | 0 |

Predictions: (1) |on - off| <= 3 everywhere: **met** (difference 0 on all four). (2) bowling and camel_case 0 in all arms: **met**. (3) square_digits <= 3: **met** (0). (4) median >= 10 in the beam arms: **met** (30, 30).
(5) random-on <= beam-on - 5 on median: **met** (0 against 30). Reading rule: the pieces do not transfer (nothing for them to do on these problems), and selection matters when no piece applies.
Median needed a median of 11.6 thousand scored candidates in the beam arms (both identical, so no piece is involved).

Caveats found in the logs. (a) Every random-search run on every problem stopped as "exhausted" with exactly 298,001 candidates scored: the random control runs out of new candidates per size level (2,000 per level up to the size limit)
and never reaches the execution cap or the wall limit, so "0 of 30" means "no hit in about 298 thousand samples of the generators", about 26 times the scored candidates the beam needed on median, not "no hit at 60M executions".
(b) The camel_case trivial program (size 1, the identity) scores 66/200 on training because strings without a hyphen are unchanged; all three arms sit at best score 66/200, so partial score gives no gradient there.
(c) The median data generator is mine (PSB1 supplies code, not files). (d) 0 against 0 on three problems cannot separate arms (power analysis of section 143).
Beam stop reasons: bowling wall 30/30 (pieces on and off); square_digits wall 29 + cap 1 (on), wall 30 (off); camel_case cap 17 + wall 13 (on), cap 28 + wall 2 (off).

## 146. Paper phase 3.2: PushGP baseline rerun is not feasible on this machine (measured, not run)

Installed Leiningen (`~/bin/lein`) and the PSB2 reference implementation (`thelmuth/Clojush`, tag `psb2-v1.0`, commit 8f7d2d7, in `~/tools/clojush-psb2`, outside the repository). Its problem files carry the PSB2 settings
(population 1000, 300 generations, lexicase, UMAD 0.09, genome up to 250, step limit 2000). It generates its own training and test cases from in-code domains, not from the Zenodo files used here, and `:random-seed` needs a Mersenne-Twister
seed object (a plain integer fails), so seeds cannot be matched to ours.
Measurement (fuel_cost, one run each, 2026-10-24, i5-8250U, 8 threads, about 7 cores busy): default JVM 251 s and 277 s per generation (generations 1-2; five processed in 22 min); JVM `-Xmx6g -Xms2g -XX:+UseParallelGC` 190 s and 187 s per generation.
At 200 thousand executions per generation that is about 1,000 executions per second on all threads; our fuel_cost runs do a median of 64 thousand per second on one thread. A non-solving run would take 16-22 h; 210 runs (30 seeds x 7 problems) would take months.
Both pilot runs were killed before finishing. No result of the baseline is claimed. Decision: keep the published PushGP figures, state the measured cost and its limits in the paper (one problem, a few early generations, an untuned reference implementation).
Our own cost, from `paper/data/psb2_900s_runs.csv`, solved runs: median seconds basement 3.9, find_pair 3.9, gcd 6.3, paired_digits 38.7, coin_sums 200.7, fuel_cost 250.2, luhn 272.9; the slowest solved run 877 s.

Review note (2026-10-24, paper pass): prediction (1) was nearly trivial to meet, since every held-out problem is at floor (0 of 30) or ceiling (30 of 30) in both beam arms; the held-out test therefore shows that the pieces add no reach on problems of other structure,
not that they have no moderate effect. The paper says so (abstract, section on the held-out test, discussion, conclusion).

## 147. Paper phase 3: leave-one-out of the six pieces on the final seeds, registered before running

Why: two reviews of the paper ask for the contribution of each piece with the others fixed at their final setting; so far the individual contributions were measured stepwise on the development seeds (section 126-131) and only the six pieces together
on the final seeds (section 142b). This is an attribution experiment, not a search for improvements.
Design: code as in section 145 (head 7ee7597, the search is unchanged since d61e125). In each arm one piece is set to 0 and the other five stay at their defaults. 30 seeds (80000-80029), 900 s, 60M cap, `scripts/run_psb2.sh`.
Problems per arm (the problem that motivated the piece, plus the bystander where an interaction is plausible): term-expr 0: fuel_cost, luhn; prefix-cross 0: basement; pair-find 0: find_pair, luhn; parity-term 0: luhn, fuel_cost; sum-if 0: paired_digits; radix 0: paired_digits.
That is 270 runs, output `data/leave_one_out_20261025/<piece>`. Reference (all pieces on, same seeds, from `paper/data/psb2_900s_runs.csv`, head 35cf3a5 whose default path is the same): fuel_cost 30, luhn 27, basement 30, find_pair 30, paired_digits 30 of 30.
Not run: coin_sums and gcd (no piece applies: 14 of 100 and 100 of 100 with and without) and the full 6 x 7 matrix (about 4,200 runs).
Predictions (solved of 30): (1) term-expr 0: fuel_cost <= 3 (all six off gave 0 of 100) and luhn within 3 of 27; (2) prefix-cross 0: basement <= 3; (3) pair-find 0: find_pair <= 3 and luhn within 3 of 27;
(4) parity-term 0: luhn <= 5 and fuel_cost between 22 and 30 (section 140: 81 against 91 of 100); (5) sum-if 0: paired_digits <= 10 and radix 0: paired_digits <= 10 (both are needed when the six are off: 13 of 100);
(6) no bystander cell (luhn under term-expr 0 and pair-find 0, fuel_cost under parity-term 0) falls more than 5 below the reference, apart from those in (4).
Reading rule: a piece is indispensable for its problem if its arm falls to <= 3; if one of sum-if/radix alone stays >= 25 the two are redundant for paired_digits; a bystander drop of 6 or more is an interaction, not covered by the stepwise attribution.
Power: 30 runs per cell detect only large paired differences; moderate effects of a single piece are not excluded by a non-significant cell.

## 147r. Leave-one-out of the six pieces: four pieces indispensable, radix redundant for paired_digits; eight of nine predictions met

Run 2026-10-25 as registered (270 runs, 30 seeds 80000-80029, 900 s, cap 60M, head 7ee7597). Solved of 30 (reference with all six on, same seeds, in parentheses); median seconds of solved runs after:
fuel_cost, term-expr 0: 1 (30), 683 s for the one solve. luhn, term-expr 0: 26 (27), 185 s. basement, prefix-cross 0: 0 (30). find_pair, pair-find 0: 0 (30). luhn, pair-find 0: 26 (27), 245 s. luhn, parity-term 0: 0 (27). fuel_cost, parity-term 0: 28 (30), 215 s. paired_digits, sum-if 0: 2 (30), 587 s. paired_digits, radix 0: **30** (30), 35 s.
Predictions: (1) met (1; 26). (2) met (0). (3) met (0; 26). (4) met (0; 28). (5) **half failed**: sum-if 0 gave 2 (<= 10, met) but radix 0 gave 30, not <= 10. By the reading rule, radix alone stays >= 25, so radix is redundant for paired_digits when sum-if is on; sum-if is indispensable there. (6) met: no bystander cell fell more than 2 below the reference (luhn 26 against 27; fuel_cost under parity-term 28 against 30); no interaction seen.
Reading: term-expr (Fuel Cost), prefix-cross (Basement), pair-find (Find Pair), parity-term (Luhn) and sum-if (Paired Digits) are each indispensable for the problem that motivated them. Radix is not needed for Paired Digits at this setting; this run does not show what radix is for (it was added in a stepwise sequence on the development seeds). Luhn needs parity-term only: term-expr 0 and pair-find 0 cost one run each.
Limits: 30 runs per cell detect only large effects; bystander differences of 1 to 2 are within noise. Coin Sums, GCD and the full 6 x 7 matrix were not run, so interactions between pieces on the other problems are not excluded. Per-run tables: `paper/data/leave_one_out_<piece>_runs.csv`.

### 147s. Clock check for the random control (Table 3): the three Luhn seeds the beam lost at 900 s are solved with a 3000 s clock

Seeds 80004, 80014, 80019 (beam, all failed on the 900 s wall in the main run; random arm had WALL=3000 and one random Luhn solve took 1035 s). Rerun alone, three processes, WALL=3000: solved at 392, 447 and 412 s with 39-48M executions, below the 60M cap. So beam Luhn is 30 of 30 on these seeds and the 27 against 30 was the clock (eight parallel runs make each slower). Table 3 keeps the 900 s figures; the paper states this. Table: `paper/data/beam_luhn_wall3000_runs.csv`.

### 147t. Audit after an external review (2026-10-25): Cut Vector, Median data, test choice, table layout

Cut Vector: `data/psb2/cut-vector/cut-vector-random.json` has 125,491 cases, 125,000 with an input of length 1 and 491 of length 2; `output1` is always the first element alone, and our reference (cut position, ties to the smallest k) agrees with all 125,491 cases. So the degeneracy is a property of the dataset file, not of our encoding; the paper now gives these counts. Median: the PSB1 authors do publish data (`thelmuth/program-synthesis-benchmark-datasets`, `datasets/median`, edge and random cases in CSV/JSON/EDN, gzipped; the edge file is 56 bytes); the paper claimed the opposite and is corrected: our own generator was used, and the result on the official files is unknown (not run). Paired Digits input is a digit string in the dataset (read as digits by `Psb2Problem`), so "used as they are" was wrong and the paper now says every PSB2 problem uses a mapping. Tests: Middle Character 2 against 0 of 20 on matched seeds gives McNemar p=0.5 (Fisher 0.49 as printed); Elements Twice not recomputed. Layout: Tables 1 and 5 overflowed the column; fixed (compact columns). Wording: "indispensable" replaced by "critical" with the 2-of-30 definition; "pieces off" stated not to mean no prior knowledge (base grammar, combinator, skeletons and count-mod stay on).

## 148. Median on the official PSB1 data files, registered before running

Why: the paper said PSB1 supplies no data files and used our own generator for Median (`scripts/psb1_median_prepare.py`); the files exist (`thelmuth/program-synthesis-benchmark-datasets`, `datasets/median`, section 147t). Median is the one place where the beam beats random search (30/30 against 0/30, section 145), so the result should not rest on our generator. Question: does the section 145 result hold on the official files?
Data: `median-random.json.gz` (1,000,000 lines, inputs in [-100, 100], one line `{"input1":..,"input2":..,"input3":..,"output1":..}`) and `median-edge.json.gz` (empty: the official edge file has no cases). Unpacked as `data/psb1_official/median-psb1/median-psb1-{edge,random}.json` (the first 50,000 random lines are read; with an empty edge file the loader prints a "not found" error and carries on with 200 random training cases and 2,000 random test cases; the Median code path and the reference are unchanged). Run with `ALGOSEARCH_PSB2_DIR=data/psb1_official` (`DATA=` in `scripts/run_psb2.sh`), problem `psb1_median_heldout`.
Arms as in section 145: beam with the six pieces on, beam with them off (`--term-expr 0 --prefix-cross 0 --parity-term 0 --pair-find 0 --sum-if 0 --radix 0`), random search with them on (`--random-search --candidates 2000 --tail-enum 0`); 30 seeds 80000-80029, 900 s, 60M cap; 90 runs; head c6f41e0 (search unchanged since d61e125). Output `data/median_official_20261029/<arm>`.
Predictions (solved of 30): (1) beam on >= 27; (2) beam off >= 27 and |on - off| <= 3; (3) random <= 3. Reading rule: (1) and (3) together mean the section 145 result is not an artefact of our generator; random >= 10 would mean it was; beam on <= 20 would mean our generator was easier than the official data. Differences from our generator: it had 10 edge cases and 5,000 random lines in [-100, 100]; the official file has no edge cases.
Power: as in section 145, a result at 30/30 or 0/30 cannot show moderate effects.

### 148r. Median on the official PSB1 files: same result as on our generator; all three predictions met

Runs 2026-10-29 (90 runs, 30 seeds 80000-80029, 900 s, 60M cap, head c6f41e0). Solved of 30: beam pieces on 30 (median 11,588 scored candidates, as on our generator), pieces off 29 (one run stopped as "exhausted", size limit), random search 0 (every run exhausted with exactly 298,001 candidates scored). Predictions: (1) beam on >= 27: met. (2) beam off >= 27 and |on - off| <= 3: met (29, difference 1). (3) random <= 3: met (0). Reading: the section 145 Median result is not an artefact of our data generator. Same limits as section 145 (30/30 and 0/30 cannot show moderate effects; random 0/30 means no hit in about 298 thousand samples). Per-run tables `paper/data/median_official_{beam_on,beam_off,random_on}_runs.csv`; data under `data/median_official_20261029` (not committed).

### 149. Learning-curve experiment: pool of 30 new problems, draw and predictions, registered before any problem is implemented

Question (user, 2026-10-31): is there a number of problems after which the hand-built library starts to generalise? Observed so far: pieces designed on the PSB2 integer subset do not transfer to 17 earlier problems or to the 4 held-out ones (sections 130-145), but the library as a whole (skeletons, combinator, pieces) was built over the 77-problem suite and has never been tested on problems chosen in advance by a rule. Design: a learning curve over batches of 10 fresh problems; the number that matters is the solve rate on a batch measured with the library frozen *before the batch is looked at*.

**Pool (30).** Chosen by the assistant for known, short reference solutions (needed to design generic pieces afterwards) and moderate difficulty, from areas the suite covers little (the 77 suite problems are mostly array statistics): number theory and digits, bit and arithmetic loops, array-to-scalar with a key, in-place array transforms, flattened matrices, small dynamic programming. None duplicates a registered problem (checked against the `--problem` list). Encodings follow section 3.2 of the paper (`v0` = n or key, `arr` input, output = scalar in `arr[0]` or the final `arr`).
Scalar from number(s): digit_sum, reverse_number, digital_root, isqrt, lcm, count_divisors, is_perfect_number, triangular_index (smallest k with k(k+1)/2 >= n), popcount, factorial_trailing_zeros, is_power_of_two, sum_multiples_3_or_5 (below n), palindrome_number, binomial, josephus.
Array to scalar: binary_to_decimal (bits array), dot_product_halves, alternating_sum, count_pairs_with_sum (key), range_max_min, kth_smallest (key), balanced_parentheses (+1/-1 array), max_non_adjacent_sum.
Array to array (in place): rotate_left_by_k, prefix_sums, cumulative_max, adjacent_differences, swap_adjacent_pairs, matrix_transpose (n x n flattened, `v0` = n), matrix_diagonal_sum (scalar, same input).
Harder than the rest and kept on purpose: josephus, kth_smallest, matrix_transpose, binomial.
Caveat: the author of the pool knows the library, so the pool is not blind to it; the draw below is.

**Draw.** `random.Random(20261031).shuffle(pool)` in Python 3 on the list in the order above; batches are consecutive tens.
Batch 1: sum_multiples_3_or_5, binomial, digit_sum, cumulative_max, popcount, alternating_sum, is_power_of_two, balanced_parentheses, swap_adjacent_pairs, count_pairs_with_sum.
Batch 2: matrix_transpose, binary_to_decimal, range_max_min, adjacent_differences, lcm, max_non_adjacent_sum, isqrt, count_divisors, kth_smallest, josephus.
Batch 3: matrix_diagonal_sum, is_perfect_number, reverse_number, dot_product_halves, digital_root, palindrome_number, rotate_left_by_k, prefix_sums, triangular_index, factorial_trailing_zeros.
Only batch 1 is implemented and run now. Batches 2 and 3 are implemented only after the library change that follows batch 1 (to avoid designing for them).

**Protocol for each batch.** Library frozen at the commit recorded before the run (batch 1: head 0426acd, search code unchanged since d61e125). 30 seeds per problem (80000-80029 reused is fine, the problems are new), 900 s, cap 60M executions, 200 training cases, 2,000 test cases, PSB2 criterion. Arms: beam with all defaults (main); random search with the same generators (control); pieces-off arm only for batch 1. No pilot and no selection of problems by difficulty: floor and ceiling results are reported as they come. Reference solutions and instance generators are written and unit-tested before any search run, and not changed after seeing results.

**Metrics.** M1 = problems solved in at least 1 of 30 seeds; M2 = problems solved in at least 15 of 30. Per problem: solved count and median candidates.

**Predictions for batch 1 (beam, defaults), registered now:** (1) M1 between 3 and 6 of 10 (the historical transfer was null on 21 problems, but the suite is large and many of these are one-loop scalar problems); (2) M2 <= 5; (3) random control M1 within 1 of beam M1; (4) pieces-off arm M1 within 1 of beam M1; (5) the solved problems are mostly the scalar loops (digit_sum, popcount, sum_multiples_3_or_5, alternating_sum, is_power_of_two); balanced_parentheses, binomial and count_pairs_with_sum are not solved in a majority of seeds.
Reading rule: M1 >= 8 of 10 means the library already covers this kind of problem (generalises); M1 <= 2 means it does not. In between is reported as partial, with the unsolved ones classified as missing piece / budget / unsound (checked against a known solution).
Curve rule: after batch 1, failures are read and library changes are made and frozen; batch 2 is then run once. Evidence of a rising curve = M1(batch 2) >= M1(batch 1) + 2 with no problem of batch 2 looked at beforehand; M1 within +-1 = flat. Every new piece is logged with the problems it solves (reuse count) and any piece solving only the problem it was written for is flagged. Power: 10 problems per batch, so only differences of 2 or more are claimed. A batch-3 run closes the curve (three points); with three points the claim is "rising / flat / falling", not a rate.
Limits recorded in advance: (a) the author chose the pool and designs the pieces, so a rise means "this author's pieces cover this pool", not "the search generalises" in the ML sense; (b) batches are random draws from one pool, so batch difficulty varies; (c) pieces written for batch 1 may help batch 2 by shared family, which is what the curve measures but also what a clever draw could exaggerate.

### 149 (batch 1 run launch)

Problems implemented as `FreshProblem` (`src/problems/FreshProblem.*`, names `fresh_<name>`, 259 tests pass), ranges as in the header comment of the class, fixed before any search run (a 60 s smoke run of `fresh_digit_sum` only checked that the program runs). Library frozen at head ff3bde7 (the search code is unchanged since d61e125; only problem files, main.cpp registry and tests were added). Runs: `scripts/run_psb2.sh` with `PROBLEMS` = the ten batch-1 names, seeds 80000-80029, 900 s, 60M cap, three arms in sequence under one detached driver (`data/learning_curve_b1_20261031/{beam_on,random_on,beam_off}`): beam defaults; random (`EXTRA="--candidates 2000 --tail-enum 0 --random-search"`); six pieces off (`EXTRA="--term-expr 0 --prefix-cross 0 --parity-term 0 --pair-find 0 --sum-if 0 --radix 0"`, count-mod stays on).

### 149r. Batch 1 results: M1 = 3 of 10 (beam), M2 = 0; random 0; pieces off 3

Runs finished 2026-11-01 08:14 (900 runs, 300 per arm, head ff3bde7 for the problems; search code unchanged since d61e125). Per-run tables `paper/data/lc_b1_{beam_on,random_on,beam_off}_runs.csv` (`scripts/psb2_run_table.py` now also reads `fresh_*`); data under `data/learning_curve_b1_20261031` (not committed). Solved of 30 (PSB2 criterion: 200 training and 2,000 unseen cases):

| problem | beam on | random | pieces off |
|---|---|---|---|
| cumulative_max | 8 | 0 | 12 |
| is_power_of_two | 3 | 0 | 6 |
| swap_adjacent_pairs | 2 | 0 | 4 |
| sum_multiples_3_or_5, binomial, digit_sum, popcount, alternating_sum, balanced_parentheses, count_pairs_with_sum | 0 | 0 | 0 |

M1 (solved in at least 1 of 30): beam on 3, random 0, pieces off 3. M2 (at least 15 of 30): 0 in every arm.
Predictions: (1) M1 in 3-6: met, at the lower edge (3). (2) M2 <= 5: met (0). (3) random M1 within 1 of beam: FAILED (0 against 3; here the beam beats random, as for Median and unlike the seven PSB2 problems). (4) pieces-off M1 within 1 of beam: met (3 against 3; per problem the off arm solved more runs on all three, 12/6/4 against 8/3/2, so the six pieces give no gain here, as the section 130-145 transfer results said). (5) half failed: balanced_parentheses, binomial and count_pairs_with_sum are not solved in a majority (met), but the solved problems were cumulative_max, is_power_of_two and swap_adjacent_pairs; the scalar loops I expected (digit_sum, popcount, sum_multiples_3_or_5, alternating_sum) were NOT solved in any of 30 seeds.
Reading rule (section 149): M1 = 3 is in the "partial" band (2 < M1 < 8), not the "does not generalise" band (M1 <= 2), but at its edge; the three solved problems are solved in 8, 3 and 2 of 30 runs, so the library covers little of this pool.
Other facts: all unsolved scalar problems end at the execution cap (or wall) with a best training score far below 200 (alternating_sum 59/200 at size 33, popcount 57/200 at size 12, digit_sum 19/200, sum_multiples_3_or_5 5/200). Four balanced_parentheses runs (seeds 80016, 80020, 80022, 80025) and two is_power_of_two runs (80007, 80015) passed all 200 training cases but failed 7-32 of 2,000 test cases (1968-1993 passed): counted as unsolved under the PSB2 criterion, and a sign that 200 cases do not pin those problems down. Random search stops as "exhausted" at 298,001 candidates for every problem, as in section 143.
Next (not yet done): classify each unsolved problem as missing piece / budget / unsound, by checking whether a known solution of at most the size limit exists in the grammar and which construct is absent, before any library change. Batch 2 stays unimplemented until the library change is made and frozen.

### 149c. Batch 1: classification of the seven unsolved problems (before any piece is written)

Method: (a) for each problem a hand-written solution in the Mini-C AST (test `fresh_unsolved_batch_1_problems_have_known_solutions_within_the_grammar`, 260 tests pass) is run on the 200 training and 2,000 test cases: all seven pass every case, with sizes 23-41 nodes against the 150 limit, so none is "unsound" (reference wrong) or "size limit". (b) For the construct absent from the generators I read the closest existing generator (`genScalarLoopBlock`, `genPositionalSumBlock`, `genRadixBlock`, the combinator's conditional sum, prefix-crossing and pair relations) and the best training score reached (best over the 30 beam-on runs, of 200 exact cases). Arithmetic (Mul/Div/Mod/shifts) is always available to the free grammar, so no operator is missing. Not tested: a 10x larger budget; "missing piece" below is a structural reading (no generator produces the shape and the best scores plateau far from 200), not a measured claim that more budget would not help.

| problem | known solution (size) | best training score, median of seeds | class | construct absent / nearest piece |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| digit_sum | digit loop `while x > 0 { acc += x % 10; x = x / 10 }` (23) | 14 (max 26) | missing piece | a loop that peels digits of one value: `UntilZero` in the scalar-loop piece exists only for the pair form (gcd); radix is a fixed 1-3 step decomposition into cells, not a loop |
| popcount | same loop with base 2 (23) | 51 (max 67) | missing piece | the same construct as digit_sum with another base: one piece would cover both |
| sum_multiples_3_or_5 | counted loop, test `i % 3 == 0` else `i % 5 == 0`, add i (37) | 12 (max 24) | missing piece | counted loop up to arr[0] with a test on the counter by a constant modulus (and an "or"); the scalar-loop Test form tests `X % i == 0` (counter as divisor), and the combinator's conditional sum tests array items, not the counter |
| alternating_sum | parity branch with + and - (32) | 62 (max 91) | missing piece, near an existing one | the positional-sum piece applies a term on one parity only and its term set (x+c, x-c, x/c, x%c, x*c, ...) has no negation; one sign term would reach it |
| balanced_parentheses | running sum, flag if it goes negative, final test for 0 (35) | 196 (max 200) | missing piece AND under-determined training | prefix-crossing exists (basement) but outputs an index, not a flag combined with a final-sum test. Four beam-on runs reached 200/200 training cases and failed 7-32 of 2,000 test cases, so wrong programs also fit the 200 cases |
| binomial | `c = c * (n - k + i) / i` over i = 1..k (32) | 121 (max 134) | missing piece, specific | the scalar-loop Fold applies one operation per step and one division after the loop; no generator divides inside the loop. Score 121/200 is partly the n = k and k = 0 cases (result 1) |
| count_pairs_with_sum | nested loop over i < j, test `arr[i] + arr[j] == arr[0]` (41) | 170 (max 179) | missing piece | pair-find returns the first pair, not a count; nested relations compare arr[i] with arr[j], not their sum with the key; items start at index 1 and the key sits in cell 0. Scores near 170/200 are the many cases with count 0, not progress |

Reading. Zero problems are budget-only, size-limited or unsound references. Six are missing pieces (one nearly covered, alternating_sum), one (balanced_parentheses) is a missing piece plus a training set too small to rule out wrong programs. Reuse across the 30-problem pool, which matters for the curve: the digit-peeling loop covers digit_sum and popcount (batch 1) and reverse_number, digital_root, palindrome_number and factorial_trailing_zeros (batch 3, by the same shape with another body), and the counter-with-modulus test covers sum_multiples_3_or_5 and, with a different accumulator, is_perfect_number and count_divisors (a different test) in batches 2-3; binomial, count_pairs_with_sum and balanced_parentheses are one-problem shapes. A piece for the first two families would be generic; the other three would be problem-specific and are flagged as such in advance.
Note for the curve rule (section 149): any piece written now is designed after seeing batch 1, so batch 1 is contaminated; the reading of a rise is on batches 2 and 3 only, and the batch-3 problems named above share shapes with the batch-1 failures (a deliberately weak test of generalisation: batch 3 will partly measure reuse, which is stated here before it is looked at).

### 150. Three generator pieces after batch 1 (digit loop, counter with modulus test, parity-signed sum); batch 2 predictions registered before it is implemented or run

Why: section 149c. Pieces (generic forms, written from the batch-1 failures only; the combinator takes them with the same pattern as radix, section 131):
- `--digit-loop P` (default 0.03): `x = arr[0]; acc = 0; while (x > 0) { acc = acc + x % c; x = x / c }; arr[0] = acc`, base c drawn in 2..10. Covers digit_sum (c = 10) and popcount (c = 2). Deliberately NOT included: variants other than the digit sum (reverse, count of digits, carry sums): they would be designed for batch-3 names I already know.
- `--counter-mod P` (default 0.03): `acc = 0; i = start (0 or 1); while (i < arr[0] [or i < arr[0] + 1]) { if (i % c1 == 0) acc += t [else if (i % c2 == 0) acc += t]; i++ }; arr[0] = acc`, c1 (and c2, in half of the blocks) in 2..9, t = i (70%) or 1 (30%). Covers sum_multiples_3_or_5.
- `--signed-parity P` (default 0.03): `acc = 0; i = 0; while (i < n) { if (i % 2 == p) acc += arr[i] else acc -= arr[i] }; arr[0] = acc`, p in {0, 1}. Covers alternating_sum.
Not addressed on purpose (one-problem shapes, section 149c): binomial, count_pairs_with_sum, balanced_parentheses.
Code: `genDigitLoopBlock`, `genCounterModBlock`, `genSignedParityBlock` in `ProgramGenerator.cpp`, taken inside `genCombinatorBlock` after radix; with all three at 0 the generator makes no extra RNG draw (the test `section_150_pieces_are_byte_identical_when_off_and_change_programs_when_on`; one older generator test now sets the three to 0 to isolate its own piece, as for the earlier pieces). 261 tests pass.
Sanity (not a result; batch 1 is contaminated): `fresh_digit_sum`, `fresh_popcount`, `fresh_sum_multiples_3_or_5`, `fresh_alternating_sum`, 3 seeds each, 300 s: 12 of 12 solved, in 2 to 19 s.
Cost check before freezing: `scripts/run_digit_pieces_joint_battery.sh` (the 77-problem suite of section 138, 10 matched seeds per problem, base = the three at 0, PIECE = 0.03). Promotion rule as before: no per-problem drop of 2 or more, aggregate not worse. If a problem drops, the offending piece's probability is lowered (or the piece removed) and the battery rerun on the affected problems; the final library is the commit recorded in the batch-2 run entry.

**Batch 2 predictions, registered now (library = the three pieces at 0.03 if the battery passes; problems not yet implemented):**
Batch 2 = matrix_transpose, binary_to_decimal, range_max_min, adjacent_differences, lcm, max_non_adjacent_sum, isqrt, count_divisors, kth_smallest, josephus. Encodings fixed now, before implementing: matrix_transpose arr = n*n items (n in 2..3) in [-20, 20] with `v0` = n*n, transposed in place; binary_to_decimal arr = 1..8 bits, most significant first, arr[0] = value; range_max_min arr = 1..8 items in [-20, 20], arr[0] = max - min; adjacent_differences arr = 1..8 items in [-20, 20], in place `a_i := a_i - a_(i-1)` for i >= 1 (a_0 unchanged); lcm arr = {a, b}, 1..30, arr[0] = lcm; max_non_adjacent_sum arr = 1..8 items in [0, 20], arr[0] = max sum of non-adjacent items; isqrt arr = {n}, n in 0..200, arr[0] = floor(sqrt n); count_divisors arr = {n}, n in 1..100, arr[0] = number of divisors; kth_smallest arr = {k, a_1 .. a_m}, m in 1..8 items in [-20, 20], k in 1..m, arr[0] = the k-th smallest (counting duplicates); josephus arr = {n, k}, n in 1..12, k in 1..5, arr[0] = survivor index (1-based) of the Josephus problem.
(1) M1 (at least 1 of 30) between 2 and 5 of 10. (2) M2 (at least 15 of 30) <= 3. (3) count_divisors is solved in at least 1 seed (the scalar-loop Test form `X % i == 0` already exists). (4) josephus, max_non_adjacent_sum, kth_smallest, matrix_transpose and binary_to_decimal are solved in 0 of 30. (5) random control M1 <= 1; pieces-off arm is not run for batch 2 (only the new three pieces differ, tested by the battery).
Curve rule (from section 149): M1(batch 2) >= M1(batch 1) + 2, i.e. >= 5, is a rise; within +-1, i.e. 2 to 4, flat. My prediction is flat (M1 around 2-4). Note: none of the batch-2 problems has a digit loop, a counter-with-modulus test or a parity-signed sum, so the three new pieces are not expected to matter here; a rise would have to come from earlier parts of the library.

### 150b. Cost battery of the three pieces: aggregate 756 against 760, two problems drop by 2; isolation started

`scripts/run_digit_pieces_joint_battery.sh`, 77 suite problems, 10 matched seeds (60100-60109), data `data/digit_pieces_joint_battery_20261101` (not committed). Solved runs (a genuinely generalising solution kept by the independent check): base 760, PIECE 756. Per problem: no problem gains 2 or more; two drop by 2: least_frequent_value 10 -> 8, majority_value 4 -> 2; every other problem is equal. The promotion rule (no per-problem drop of 2 or more) is therefore NOT met as the battery stands. Per the plan in section 150, the offending piece is isolated before anything is changed: `scripts/run_digit_pieces_isolation.sh`, the two problems, arms base / digit-loop alone / counter-mod alone / signed-parity alone / all three, 30 matched seeds (60100-60129), 180 s. The rule for the outcome is fixed now: a piece whose lone arm loses 3 or more runs against base on either problem at 30 seeds has its probability halved (0.015) and the isolation is repeated for it; if the drop persists it is removed. If no lone arm drops by 3 or more and "all" does not either, the 10-seed drops are read as noise and the pieces stay at 0.03.

### 150c. Isolation of the cost: signed-parity and digit-loop each lose 3 or more on one problem; both halved to 0.015 and rerun

Isolation run (30 seeds 60100-60129, 180 s, data `data/digit_pieces_isolation_20261101`, not committed). Solved of 30: least_frequent_value base 30, digit-loop alone 29, counter-mod alone 29, signed-parity alone 27, all three 28; majority_value base 11, digit-loop alone 7, counter-mod alone 10, signed-parity alone 10, all three 10. By the rule of section 150b: signed-parity (-3 on least_frequent_value) and digit-loop (-4 on majority_value) lose 3 or more and are halved to 0.015 and rerun alone (`scripts/run_digit_pieces_isolation2.sh`, arms digit015 and signed015, same seeds, base reused); counter-mod (-1 and -1) stays at 0.03. All three together lose 2 and 1, so the 10-seed drops of section 150b were partly noise, but the rule is applied as written. If a halved piece still loses 3 or more it is removed; if not, it stays at 0.015.

### 150d. Isolation of the halved pieces: both pass; library frozen for batch 2 at head e118572

Second isolation (30 seeds 60100-60129, 180 s; data `data/digit_pieces_isolation2_20261101`, not committed). Solved of 30: digit-loop at 0.015: least_frequent_value 28 (base 30), majority_value 11 (base 11); signed-parity at 0.015: 30 and 12. Neither loses 3 or more, so by the rule of section 150b both stay at 0.015; counter-mod stays at 0.03. Final defaults: `--digit-loop 0.015 --counter-mod 0.03 --signed-parity 0.015`. The cost of the three together was 2 and 1 runs on the two problems at 30 seeds (section 150c) and aggregate 756 against 760 over the 77-problem suite at 10 seeds (section 150b); the rule's letter was met only after the halving, and the evidence that the residual drops are real is weak (28 of 30 against 30 of 30 is not significant). Sanity at the final defaults (batch 1 problems, not a result): digit_sum, popcount, sum_multiples_3_or_5, alternating_sum 5 of 5 seeds each at 300 s. Tests: 263 pass (the stream-sensitive combinator test helper sets the three new biases to 0, as in section 150's generator test).
Library frozen at head e118572 for batch 2 (search code changes since d61e125: only section 150's three blocks). Batch 2 runs: `scripts/run_psb2.sh` with PROBLEMS = the ten batch-2 names, seeds 80000-80029, 900 s, 60M cap, arms beam (defaults) and random (`--candidates 2000 --tail-enum 0 --random-search`), data `data/learning_curve_b2_20261101/{beam_on,random_on}`; no pieces-off arm (section 150).

### 150r. Batch 2 of the learning curve: beam solves 6 of 10 (M1 = 6 against 3 in batch 1), a rise by the rule; predictions 1, 4, 5 failed

Run: seeds 80000-80029, 900 s, 60M cap, library frozen at head e118572 (section 150d); data `data/learning_curve_b2_20261101/{beam_on,random_on}` (not committed), tables `paper/data/lc_b2_{beam_on,random_on}_runs.csv`. Solved of 30 (beam / random): count_divisors 30 / 30, range_max_min 30 / 30, kth_smallest 19 / 0, isqrt 11 / 0, binary_to_decimal 3 / 0, max_non_adjacent_sum 2 / 0; adjacent_differences, josephus, lcm, matrix_transpose 0 / 0. M1 beam 6, random 2; M2 beam 3 (count_divisors, range_max_min, kth_smallest), random 2.
Curve rule (section 149): M1(batch 2) = 6 >= M1(batch 1) + 2 = 5, so the curve rises (3 -> 6). M2 goes 0 -> 3.
Predictions (registered in section 150): (1) M1 between 2 and 5: FAILED (6). (2) M2 <= 3: met (3, at the bound). (3) count_divisors in at least one seed: met (30 of 30). (4) josephus, max_non_adjacent_sum, kth_smallest, matrix_transpose, binary_to_decimal all 0 of 30: FAILED for three of five (kth_smallest 19, binary_to_decimal 3, max_non_adjacent_sum 2); josephus and matrix_transpose met. (5) random M1 <= 1: FAILED (2), but both are the same two problems the beam solves in all 30 seeds, so random adds nothing beyond them. My forecast was a flat curve; the data say rise.
Caveats. The author chose the pool and designs the pieces, so the rise reads "this author's pieces cover this pool better than batch 1", not general transfer. Where the rise comes from is not yet known: none of the three section 150 pieces was meant for these problems, so it comes from earlier pieces (kth_smallest, isqrt, range_max_min, count_divisors share forms with the 77-problem suite), which is reuse, not new coverage; batch 1 was weaker partly because its failures needed the missing loop forms. The random arm solves only the two problems that are reachable by tiny programs. Batch 3 shares forms with batch 1 failures and so measures partly reuse. Not tested: ten times the budget on the unsolved ones.
Next: read the four zero problems (adjacent_differences, josephus, lcm, matrix_transpose) as in section 149c before any library change.

### 150s. Batch 2: the four unsolved problems have known solutions within the grammar (test, not search)

Test `fresh_unsolved_batch_2_problems_have_known_solutions_within_the_grammar` (tests/fresh_problems_tests.cpp): hand-written ASTs for adjacent_differences (backward loop, `arr[i] = arr[i] - arr[i-1]`), lcm (remainder gcd then `arr[0] / g * arr[1]`), josephus (`s = (s + k) % i` for i = 2..n) and matrix_transpose (n by a counting loop, swap for j > i) pass all 200 training and 2,000 test cases, each of size at most 150 (the exact sizes were not recorded). So none of the four is a wrong reference or a grammar/size limit; they are missing-piece or search-reach failures. This is a reachability check, not a diagnosis: the programs the search ended with were not printed (the logs hold only partial scores: lcm plateaus at 123-130 of 200 at size 22 in every seed; josephus about 107 of 200; adjacent_differences 28; matrix_transpose 84-96), so which piece is missing for each is a hypothesis (lcm: gcd done, divide-and-multiply not completed; josephus: modulus by the loop variable; adjacent_differences: backward in-place loop; matrix_transpose: flat index with swap). 264 tests pass. No library change made: batch 2 stays as run.

### 151. Batch 3 of the learning curve: encodings and predictions registered before implementing or running it

Decision (section 150s, accepted): the library stays frozen at head e118572 (the batch 2 library). No piece was added after reading the batch 2 failures. Batch 3 is the ten problems left by the draw of section 149: matrix_diagonal_sum, is_perfect_number, reverse_number, dot_product_halves, digital_root, palindrome_number, rotate_left_by_k, prefix_sums, triangular_index, factorial_trailing_zeros.
Encodings, fixed now (same convention: `arr` read, scalar answer at arr[0] or array result in place; 200 training and 2,000 unseen test cases): matrix_diagonal_sum: arr = n*n items (n in 2..3) in [-20, 20], arr[0] = the sum of items i*n+i. is_perfect_number: arr = {n}, n in 1..500, three in ten draws are one of 6, 28, 496, the rest uniform; arr[0] = 1 if the sum of proper divisors equals n (n = 1 gives 0), else 0. reverse_number: arr = {n}, n in 1..9999; arr[0] = the decimal digits reversed (1200 gives 21). dot_product_halves: arr = 2m items (m in 1..4) in [-9, 9]; arr[0] = sum over i < m of arr[i]*arr[m+i]. digital_root: arr = {n}, n in 1..99999; arr[0] = the digital root. palindrome_number: arr = {n}, n in 1..9999, half the draws palindromes; arr[0] = 1 if the digits read the same reversed, else 0. rotate_left_by_k: arr = {k, a_0 .. a_(m-1)}, m in 2..7, k in 0..m-1, items in [-20, 20]; arr[0] = k stays, items rotate left by k in place. prefix_sums: arr = 1..8 items in [-20, 20]; in place a_i := a_0 + ... + a_i. triangular_index: arr = {n}, n in 1..100; arr[0] = the smallest k with k(k+1)/2 >= n. factorial_trailing_zeros: arr = {n}, n in 1..200; arr[0] = the number of trailing zeros of n!.
Protocol as batches 1 and 2: seeds 80000-80029, 900 s, 60M cap, arms beam (defaults) and random (`--candidates 2000 --tail-enum 0 --random-search`); no pieces-off arm.
Predictions (written before any code for these problems exists):
1. Batch 3 M1 (problems solved in at least one seed) between 4 and 7, point guess 5; by the curve rule that is flat against 6 (a rise needs 8 or more, a fall 4 or fewer by the plus-or-minus-1 band, so a value of 4 would count as a fall).
2. prefix_sums solved in at least one seed (it has the shape of cumulative_max, batch 1 in 8 of 30).
3. digital_root solved in at least 25 of 30 (the closed form (n-1) % 9 + 1 is a short expression; not a use of the digit-loop piece).
4. triangular_index solved in at least one seed (the shape of isqrt, 11 of 30).
5. reverse_number, palindrome_number, matrix_diagonal_sum and rotate_left_by_k all 0 of 30 (no piece builds reversal; matrix_transpose was 0; swap_adjacent_pairs was 2 of 30).
6. Random arm M1 at most 3.
7. M2 (problems solved in at least 15 of 30 seeds) at most 3.
Reading rules: the curve is described by M1 over batches 1, 2, 3 = 3, 6, ?; a third rise would be 8 or more. Caveats stated in advance: batch 3 shares forms with the unsolved batch 1 problems (digit loops, a counter with a modulus test), so a rise partly measures the three section 150 pieces on forms they were built for, which is reuse, not transfer; the author chose the pool and designs the pieces; digital_root and prefix_sums are close to problems already in the library.

### 151r. Batch 3 of the learning curve: beam solves 3 of 10 (M1 3, 6, 3 over the three batches); the curve is not monotone; predictions 1, 2, 3 and part of 5 failed

Run: seeds 80000-80029, 900 s, 60M cap, library frozen at e118572 (the batch 2 library); data `data/learning_curve_b3_20261101/{beam_on,random_on}` (not committed), tables `paper/data/lc_b3_{beam_on,random_on}_runs.csv`. Solved of 30 (beam / random): is_perfect_number 1 / 0, matrix_diagonal_sum 1 / 0, triangular_index 1 / 0; the other seven 0 / 0 (digital_root, dot_product_halves, factorial_trailing_zeros, palindrome_number, prefix_sums, reverse_number, rotate_left_by_k). M1 beam 3, random 0; M2 0 in both. Each solved problem is a single seed of 30.
Curve: M1 over batches 1, 2, 3 = 3, 6, 3; M2 = 0, 3, 0. By the rule stated in section 151 (rise 8 or more, flat 5 to 7, fall 4 or fewer; the text of prediction 1 was inconsistent about the fall band, which I read as the band around 6 of batch 2) batch 3 is a fall against batch 2 and equal to batch 1. So the number of problems solved does not grow with the number of batches: the library written for the 77-problem suite plus the section 150 pieces solves about three of ten fresh problems on average, and batch 2 was the easy batch.
Predictions: (1) M1 between 4 and 7: FAILED (3, below the range). (2) prefix_sums in at least one seed: FAILED (0 of 30), although cumulative_max (same loop shape, an accumulator over the prefix) was 8 of 30 in batch 1. (3) digital_root in at least 25 of 30: FAILED (0 of 30); the closed form (n - 1) % 9 + 1 passes the known-solution test, so it is within the grammar, and it was not reached in 27,000 sampled runs. (4) triangular_index in at least one seed: met (1 of 30; isqrt had 11 of 30). (5) reverse_number, palindrome_number, matrix_diagonal_sum, rotate_left_by_k all 0: held for three, FAILED for matrix_diagonal_sum (1 of 30). (6) random M1 at most 3: met (0). (7) M2 at most 3: met (0). Scored: 3 of 7 predictions held outright (4, 6, 7), 4 failed or half failed. Two of the three wrong ones overestimated how close a problem was to a solved one (prefix_sums, digital_root).
Reading. With the pieces of this author and the three batches fixed by a draw, the fraction solved by the beam is 3, 6, 3 out of 10: no upward trend, and the sample is three points of ten problems, so the differences are at the level of what one problem swap changes (a problem solved in 2 of 30 seeds is counted like one solved in 30 of 30 by M1; M2 is stricter and also goes 0, 3, 0). Batch 3 was meant to share forms with the unsolved batch 1 problems, and the section 150 pieces were built for exactly those forms (digit loop, counter with modulus test, signed parity); yet reverse_number and palindrome_number (digit loops that build a number rather than sum digits) are 0 of 30, the sum-type forms the pieces target do not appear in batch 3, and the pieces were deliberately not given variants. This is a hypothesis for why the reuse effect is not visible, not a measurement. The beam beats random again (3 against 0 here; 6 against 2 in batch 2; 3 against 0 in batch 1), always by problems that random never solves and the beam solves in few seeds, which fits the Median result of the paper but does not extend it. Over the 30 problems: beam M1 12 (3 + 6 + 3), random M1 2 (both in batch 2).
Caveats: the author chose the pool and the pieces; three single-seed solves are near the noise floor; the budget was 900 s and 60M executions, not tested at ten times that; the batches are not matched in difficulty and cannot be, so the shape of the curve over batches says little about a trend. What the three batches do show is a cost: with a fixed hand-built library the unaided search solves 12 of 30 fresh easy problems in at least one of 30 seeds, but only 3 of 30 in at least half of the seeds (count_divisors, range_max_min, kth_smallest).

### 152. Diagnosis after the learning curve: literals are limited to 0..3; test with `--max-const`; predictions registered before the run

Context (user decision after sections 149-151r): three paths, in order: (1) cheap diagnosis of why short programs are not reached, (2) diversity in selection, (3) a learned library; no new lines. This section is step 1.
Two corrections to what was said after section 151r. (a) The `errors` figure in the progress lines counts program-by-case executions per size step (the counter is reset each step), so the roughly 195 thousand errors of a step are about 38% of that step's roughly 506 thousand executions (2,530 candidates times 200 cases), not 65% of candidates as I said; the share of candidates that fail on every case is not measured. The cause is not diagnosed. (b) FINDINGS.md section 4 lists population diversity, niching and crossover as closed negatives for finding new algorithms (sections 5, 20, 21, 28); the behavioural-duplicate diagnostic of section 35 exists; per-case selection (lexicase) is not in the record. Step 2 must start from that, not from scratch.
Finding in code: `GenConfig::maxConst = 3` (src/search/ProgramGenerator.hpp:27) and no CLI flag changed it, so a generated literal is in 0..3. Constants 5 (factorial_trailing_zeros), 9 (digital_root), 10 (reverse_number, palindrome_number) can then be reached only as arithmetic of smaller ones, or inside the section 150 digit-loop piece. Hypothesis: the literal range explains digital_root (closed form `(n - 1) % 9 + 1`, 0 of 30) and is a candidate cause for factorial_trailing_zeros and the digit-building problems.
Test: new flag `--max-const N` (default 3, byte-identical). Arms: base = the batch 2 and 3 runs already made (seeds 80000-80019, 900 s, 60M cap, same flags); new = the same plus `--max-const 10`. Problems: digital_root, factorial_trailing_zeros, reverse_number, palindrome_number, prefix_sums (a control not driven by constants), and as cost controls isqrt and count_divisors (batch 2).
Predictions (before the run): (1) digital_root at least 15 of 20 with `--max-const 10` (base 0). (2) factorial_trailing_zeros at least 1 of 20 (base 0). (3) reverse_number and palindrome_number at most 2 of 20 each (a constant 10 helps but the digit-building loop is still not a piece). (4) prefix_sums stays 0 of 20; if it moves, the noise is larger than assumed. (5) cost: count_divisors at least 18 of 20 and isqrt not more than 3 below its base on the same 20 seeds. A pass on (1) means the literal range is a reach limit, and the next question is how to offer constants without diluting 0..3 (a battery would follow before any default change; promotion rule of FINDINGS.md applies).

### 152r. `--max-const 10` does not solve the short problems; the search stops on a plateau just below the answer (prediction 1 failed)

Run: `--max-const 10`, seeds 80000-80019, 900 s, 60M cap, other flags as batches 2 and 3; data `data/max_const_diag_20261102/mc10` (not committed), table `paper/data/mc10_diag_runs.csv`. Solved of 20, base (batches 2 and 3, same seeds) against max-const 10: digital_root 0 / 1, factorial_trailing_zeros 0 / 0, reverse_number 0 / 0, palindrome_number 0 / 0, prefix_sums 0 / 1, isqrt 6 / 10, count_divisors 20 / 20.
Predictions: (1) digital_root at least 15 of 20: FAILED (1). (2) factorial_trailing_zeros at least 1: FAILED (0). (3) reverse_number and palindrome_number at most 2: held (0, 0). (4) prefix_sums stays 0: FAILED (1 of 20, so one solve in 20 seeds is within the noise of this setting). (5) cost controls: held (count_divisors 20, isqrt 10 against 6).
Conclusion: the literal range 0..3 is not what blocks digital_root, so the hypothesis of section 152 is rejected as the main cause. It is not irrelevant: the best partial score of digital_root rises from 49 of 200 at the base (a 200-case training set; one hand run, seed 80000, 14-size cap) to 177-179 of 200 with max-const 10 in every seed, which is the score of `n % 9` (it is wrong exactly for the multiples of 9, about 11% of cases). The beam then stays at that plateau: the repair that turns 0 into 9 (a conditional, or `(n - 1) % 9 + 1`) is not found in 60M executions, and 20,000 fresh candidates per size for 200 s (seed 80000, one run, size cap 14) leave it at 177. So budget in this range is not the limit either (a single run, not a test). The same pattern holds in other unsolved problems: lcm sits at 123-130 of 200 in every seed (batch 2), and digital_root now at 177. Reading: for these problems the stop is a plateau where the last step is not a neighbour of a high-ranked program, which is what per-case selection (step 2 of the plan of section 152) is meant to address: the failing cases (here n divisible by 9) are the same in every near-solution, and a program that solves just them would be ranked low by a sum of cases.
Open and not diagnosed: why prefix_sums stays at 23-58 of 200; what the share of candidates that fail on every case is; the log field `best size` reaches 34-42 for a run with `--max-program-size 14`, so it is not the same size as the generation target (I read the size of the n % 9 program from this field in the first pass and was wrong; no claim rests on it).
Next (step 2 of the plan): per-case selection, with the check against the closed negatives of FINDINGS.md section 4 first (they concern diversity, niching and crossover, not per-case selection).

### 153. Step 2 of the plan: per-case (lexicase) selection; design and predictions registered before the run

Check against the closed negatives (FINDINGS.md section 4; sections 5, 21, 28, 35): they cover random immigrants (5: beam diversity up 3.5x, no effect on min/max/sum), quota strategies and crossover on contains_ordered (21), the diversity battery (28) and the measure of behavioural redundancy (35: 70-98% of scored candidates behave like one already seen). Shape and behaviour niches exist in the code (`--shape-niche-rate`, `--behavior-niche-rate`). None of them select by per-case filtering. The behaviour niche scans the ranking in score order and takes the first unseen right/wrong pattern, so a program that solves only the few cases everyone else fails (the multiples of 9 of digital_root) sits far down the ranking and is not reached. This test is therefore new, but the record says that widening what the beam keeps has not found new shapes, so the prior for a gain is low.
Implementation: `--lexicase-rate R` (default 0, byte-identical: the output of fresh_count_divisors seed 80000 has the same checksum as the batch 2 log, and 267 tests pass). R * beamWidth slots are filled by lexicase draws from the candidates left after the elite core: cases in random order, keep only the candidates that solve the case when any does, stop at one candidate, no replacement; the rest of the beam is the top by rank as before. It needs the per-case correctness vector, which is already computed for the niche.
Arms (all with the library frozen at e118572, flags as batches 2 and 3, seeds 80000-80019, 900 s, 60M cap): lexicase R = 0.25 against the base runs already made (same seeds). Problems: the 14 problems that batches 2 and 3 left at 0 or one seed of 30 (lcm, josephus, adjacent_differences, matrix_transpose, digital_root, prefix_sums, factorial_trailing_zeros, reverse_number, palindrome_number, dot_product_halves, rotate_left_by_k, is_perfect_number, matrix_diagonal_sum, triangular_index) and three controls (kth_smallest, isqrt, count_divisors). One more arm for the mechanism: digital_root with `--max-const 10` (base 1 of 20 in section 152r).
Predictions: (1) digital_root with max-const 10 and lexicase: at least 5 of 20 solved (base 1). (2) Over the 14 problems, the number solved in at least one of the 20 seeds rises by at least 2 against the base (base is counted from the existing tables). (3) lcm, josephus and matrix_transpose stay at 0 of 20 (their gap is a missing step, not an unrewarded case). (4) cost: no control loses 3 or more solved runs (kth_smallest, isqrt, count_divisors) on the same seeds. Promotion would need the 77-problem battery by the rule of FINDINGS.md (no problem falls by 2 or more, aggregate not worse); a pass on (1) and (2) is the condition to run it.

### 153r. Lexicase (R = 0.25), first 20 seeds: registered predictions 1 and 2 failed; a possible small gain on problems already partly solved; confirmation on new seeds registered

Run: seeds 80000-80019, 900 s, 60M cap, library at e118572 plus `--lexicase-rate 0.25`; data `data/lexicase_ab_20261102/{lex25,lex25_mc10}` (not committed), tables `paper/data/lexicase_lex25_runs.csv`, `lexicase_lex25_mc10_runs.csv`. Solved of 20, base (batches 2 and 3, same seeds) / lexicase: adjacent_differences 0 / 0, count_divisors 20 / 20, digital_root 0 / 0, dot_product_halves 0 / 0, factorial_trailing_zeros 0 / 0, is_perfect_number 1 / 3, isqrt 6 / 11, josephus 0 / 0, kth_smallest 11 / 15, lcm 0 / 0, matrix_diagonal_sum 1 / 3, matrix_transpose 0 / 0, palindrome_number 0 / 0, prefix_sums 0 / 3, reverse_number 0 / 0, rotate_left_by_k 0 / 0, triangular_index 1 / 0; digital_root with `--max-const 10` and lexicase 0 / 20 (base 1 / 20).
Predictions: (1) digital_root with max-const 10 and lexicase at least 5 of 20: FAILED (0). The mechanism I proposed for the plateau (an unrewarded subset of cases) did not rescue the case I built it for. (2) at least 2 more problems solved in some seed among the 14 hard ones: FAILED (3 problems with a solve in the base, 3 with lexicase; prefix_sums gained, triangular_index lost). (3) lcm, josephus, matrix_transpose stay 0: held. (4) cost: no control loses 3 or more: held (isqrt 6 to 11, kth_smallest 11 to 15, count_divisors 20 to 20).
Reading. By the condition registered in section 153 (predictions 1 and 2 passing) the 77-problem battery is not run. What the data do show: total solved runs over the six problems that had any solve (is_perfect_number, isqrt, kth_smallest, matrix_diagonal_sum, triangular_index, count_divisors) are 40 of 120 for the base and 55 of 120 with lexicase (Fisher exact two-sided p = 0.064; these six were not chosen by gain, but only because they were not at zero in both arms, so the p is optimistic). The gain is in problems the beam already solves in some seeds (isqrt, kth_smallest), not in the plateau cases. Single-problem differences are not significant (isqrt 11 against 6, p = 0.20; kth_smallest 15 against 11, p = 0.32; prefix_sums 3 against 0, p = 0.23). So: not a way to reach new problems; possibly a small gain in reliability on reachable ones, to be confirmed.
Confirmation (registered now, before the run): new seeds 80030-80059 (not used in any selection), both arms (base, lexicase 0.25), 900 s, 60M cap, problems isqrt, kth_smallest, prefix_sums, is_perfect_number, matrix_diagonal_sum, triangular_index. Prediction: if the effect is real, lexicase solves at least 8 more runs than the base summed over the six problems, and no problem loses 3 or more; if the sum is below 8 the gain on the first 20 seeds is taken as noise and lexicase is closed as a negative for reaching new problems (and as not shown for reliability).

### 153c. Lexicase confirmation on new seeds: +8 solved runs, at the registered threshold, not significant; opt-in, not promoted

Run: seeds 80030-80059, 900 s, 60M cap, both arms on the same new seeds; data `data/lexicase_confirm_20261102/{base,lex25}` (not committed), tables `paper/data/lexicase_confirm_{base,lex25}_runs.csv`. Solved of 30, base / lexicase: is_perfect_number 1 / 3, isqrt 10 / 11, kth_smallest 14 / 18, matrix_diagonal_sum 0 / 0, prefix_sums 2 / 1, triangular_index 0 / 2. Sum 27 / 35, difference +8.
Prediction of section 153r (at least 8 more solved runs summed over the six problems, and no problem loses 3 or more): met, exactly at the threshold (+8; the worst problem loses 1). By the rule that I registered the effect is not taken as noise. Honest reading: Fisher exact two-sided p = 0.33 for 35 against 27 of 180, so on these seeds alone the difference is within chance; pooled with the first 20 seeds (70 against 47 of 300 on the same six problems, p = 0.02, optimistic because the first 20 seeds are where the six were picked) it is suggestive. The base itself moves between sets of seeds (prefix_sums 0 of 20, then 2 of 30; matrix_diagonal_sum 1 of 20, then 0 of 30), which is the level of noise to keep in mind.
Decision. Lexicase raises, at most, the reliability on problems the beam already reaches (kth_smallest 11 of 20 to 15 of 20, then 14 of 30 to 18 of 30; isqrt 6 to 11, then 10 to 11). It does not reach any problem that was at zero (digital_root, lcm, josephus, matrix_transpose, and the others stay at 0), and it did not fix the plateau for which it was designed (digital_root with max-const 10: 0 of 20). For the goal of discovering problems not written by hand it is therefore closed: a null result for reach, a small reliability gain. It stays opt-in (`--lexicase-rate`, default 0) and is not promoted: promotion would need the 77-problem battery by the rule of FINDINGS.md, and the gain does not justify that now. Step 2 of the plan is done. Step 3 (a learned library, evaluated leave-batch-out) is next.

### 154. Step 3 of the plan, analysis before building: a library mined from solved programs does not contain what the unsolved problems need

Script `scripts/mine_fragments.py`. Corpus: the solved programs printed in the logs of the 77-problem battery (base arm, `data/digit_pieces_joint_battery_20261101`) and of the three learning-curve batches: 90 problems, 6,697 programs, 993 distinct statement fragments after renaming variables by first occurrence (982 with constants other than 0 and 1 abstracted). Support of a fragment = the number of distinct problems whose solved programs contain it; for a target problem its own programs are excluded (leave-one-problem-out, so a fragment from the target's own batch mates does count). Targets: my hand-written known solutions of the nine problems unsolved in batches 2 and 3 for which a short solution exists (digital_root, prefix_sums, lcm, josephus, adjacent_differences, factorial_trailing_zeros, reverse_number, dot_product_halves, matrix_transpose; the same ASTs pass the tests of sections 150s and 151) and, as a calibration, the shortest solved program of each solved fresh problem.
Results. (a) Fragments of size 4 or more: covered by at least one other problem in 12 of 35 for the unsolved targets (34%) and 59 of 112 for the solved ones (53%). The covered ones are the loop counters and the result store `arr[0] = vK` (support 85). (b) The non-trivial simple statements of the known solutions of the unsolved problems (the ones that carry the answer: `arr[0] = (arr[0] - 1) % 9 + 1`, `v = (v + k) % i`, `arr[0] = arr[0] / g * arr[1]`, `v = v * 10 + w % 10`, `arr[i] = arr[i] - arr[i-1]`, the flat-index swap, the dot-product accumulation): 3 of 14 have support above 0, and those three are the trivial store `arr[0] = vK`; the 11 others have support 0 in all 90 problems. (c) Leaf-abstracted expression shapes (variables and constants replaced by holes) of size 5 or more: support 0 for digital_root, prefix_sums, lcm, josephus, reverse_number, dot_product_halves; adjacent_differences 9 problems; matrix_transpose 1.
Reading. The pieces that block the unsolved problems have never appeared, even as a shape, in any program the search has solved; a library mined from solved programs would not contain them, so it cannot make these problems reachable. What it could add is recombination of what is already there, which is what the generators and the beam do. This is a predicted negative: the mining library (step 3 as defined: subtrees of solved programs used as new pieces, evaluated leave-batch-out) is not built. Limits of this analysis: my known solutions are one solution per problem (digital_root has another through repeated digit sums, which the digit-loop piece could contain; the others are less ambiguous); the corpus is the solved programs of this system, which come mostly from hand-built pieces; coverage is a necessary condition for reach, not a sufficient one (a covered fragment may still not be composed into the whole program).
What this leaves open (for a decision, not started): the blocker is not a missing frequent subtree but a missing composition: a loop skeleton whose update expression is a small arithmetic expression that no earlier problem used. The record (FINDINGS.md sections 63-69) says that the shape of a skeleton is what the search does not assemble, while holes inside a given shape are filled by enumeration.

### 155. Evaluation of a loop skeleton with an enumerated update expression (counting estimate, nothing built)

Question (user): would a generic piece "skeleton whose update expression is enumerated" make the unsolved problems reachable. Existing relatives: `--term-expr` (section 127; a uniform draw from about 100 forms `x op c1 op c2` inside the Sum loop, fuel_cost 0 to 29 of 30), `--tail-enum` (enumerated tails), and the record that holes inside a given shape are filled by enumeration but the shape is not assembled (sections 63-69). A generic version would draw the update, and possibly the final store, from all expressions of up to n binary ops over the leaves in scope.
Counting estimate (`scripts/hole_space_estimate.py`; five arithmetic operators, literals 0..3, the holes of my known solutions, small discrete choices for initial constants and comparator; assumes the right skeleton is given): joint fill space and the ratio budget / space for 300 thousand scored candidates: factorial_trailing_zeros 1.9e5 (1.0), prefix_sums 8.8e4 (1.0), adjacent_differences 1.8e5 (1.0), digital_root 8.2e5 (0.37), reverse_number 2.1e7 (0.015), lcm 1.8e7 (0.017), josephus 7.2e7 (0.004), dot_product_halves 8.3e9 (0.0). With a cheap staged evaluation (a candidate rejected after 3 of the 200 cases, which by section 35 discards 94-99% of candidates) a candidate costs about 100 times less: reverse_number and lcm become 1.0, josephus 0.41, dot_product_halves 0.004.
What this says. (1) For four of eight problems the fill space is small enough (under one million) that, if the skeleton is produced, enumeration finds the fill within the existing budget even without staging; for three more it needs staged evaluation; dot_product_halves is out of reach in this form. (2) Sampled without staging, the piece cannot reach the larger ones: 300 thousand candidates against 1e7 to 1e8 fills. That is the reason the term-expression piece worked only because its form set is about 100 and sits at one site. (3) The estimate leaves out the factor that decides it: how often the search produces the right skeleton (for prefix_sums, an in-place loop over the indices with an element store; for josephus, a loop from 2 to n with a recurrence on a scalar). That is not measured. Prefix_sums, with a fill space of 8.8e4 and still 0 to 3 of 20, suggests the skeleton, not the fill, is the blocker there, or that my leaf count (2 + 4) understates the array-element leaves.
Verdict: not built. A generic piece of this kind needs a staged evaluation inside generation (a new engine path), and its payoff is bounded by an unmeasured skeleton frequency. Cheapest decisive step, if the line is continued: instrument a run to count the fresh candidates that contain the skeleton (a loop over the indices with an array store; a counting loop with a scalar recurrence), without changing the search. If that frequency times the fill-space ratio is below one for the batch problems, the piece cannot work; if it is above, build the staged version.

### 156. Skeleton frequency among fresh candidates (the measurement section 155 left open; nothing in the search changed)
