Round trip (`c_roundtrip.py`, 2000 inputs): ctype-style `((unsigned)c|32)-'a' < 26`, constant and variable shifts, an unsigned popcount loop, mixed int/unsigned compares and `?:`, an inlined `uint32_t` rotate, an array sum with an `unsigned` length and index: all OK.
Census predictions (same sources and directories as 192/193): musl 13 → 21–35 functions importing on their own (65%: ctype's `(unsigned)c - 'a' < 26` family, bit functions, but most `unsigned` code also uses `long`/`size_t`/pointers or calls `isdigit` from another file); Lua 2 → 2–4 (75%).
Next, registered with its results: superopt (timed, defaults, 3 seeds × 60 s) on what newly imports.
Superopt part, registered after the census and before the run. The 18 distinct non-stub functions that newly import (`hexval` appears in both inet_pton.c and regcomp.c) are copied verbatim to `tests/c_samples_musl_lua/musl_*.c`. The ones using `uint32_t` get the `#include <stdint.h>` their file reaches through other headers. Round trip, 2000 inputs: 18/18 OK. rol / ror are defined only for k in 1..31, so they compare 460 in-domain inputs (`--min-compared 0`). Script fix: `linkable()` drops `inline` with `static`, since a plain C99 `inline` definition emits no symbol (checkint, swapc, zeroinfnan failed to link without it).
Run: `SAMPLES="<the 18 files>" scripts/run_superopt_battery.sh data/superopt_unsigned_20261115 "10 11 12" 60 1 4`.
Predictions:
- all 54 outputs equivalent, and the 15 unary ones at level `exhaustive` or the reference (95%);
- no result < 0.95 on the measurable samples (90%);
- the six one-compare ctype functions (isalpha, isdigit, isgraph, islower, isprint, isupper) do not gain (85%: one `sub` plus one unsigned `cmp` already);
- setup_salt, a 24-step bit loop, gains on ≥ 2 seeds (50%);
- 1–4 of the 18 gain (60%).

### 194 (first run, invalid): a stale binary, and one wrong result

`data/superopt_unsigned_20261115/` (kept as evidence). Two problems:
- **Stale binary.** The `prefixFunctions` edit (unsigned return types) was made after the last `make` of `./algosearch`; `make test` rebuilt only the test binary. Every function returning `uint32_t`/`unsigned` failed to compile in the timer (the original and the candidate were defined twice in one unit), so 7 of the 18 samples have no output (21 runs): ascii_to_bin, lcg31, rol, ror, setup_salt, swapc, temper. The front end in the binary was current; the 11 int-returning samples ran as intended.
- **A wrong result.** checkint seed 11 returned a program that the battery's external check refutes (1 mismatch in 2000 inputs: 1245017448 → 1, original 2). It moves both exponent bounds by one (`e < 128` for `e < 0x7f`, `e > 149` for `e > 0x7f + 23`), which is wrong exactly when e = 127 or e = 150. That is 2 of 256 exponents, and further mantissa conditions apply, so few random inputs reach it. Its level was `tested`, not `exhaustive`. The exhaustive gate required a reference that cannot fail, and checkint's `1 << (0x7f + 23 - e)` has a variable shift count, which the static check counts as possibly failing (it cannot fail, given the guards above it).

Fix: the gate now runs for every unary scalar function. The compiled harness lists the first 4096 mismatching arguments. When the reference may fail, each mismatch is checked against the Mini-C reference: an argument the reference defines refutes the candidate. Where the reference fails (undefined in C), the compiled values are arbitrary and the mismatch does not count. More than 4096 mismatches refute it. A run that crashes or times out (e.g. a division the reference never reaches on its domain) stays `tested`. Test extended (the list of mismatches; unsigned signatures in one unit); 310/310. Rerun of all 18 samples below, same command, into `data/superopt_unsigned2_20261115/`, predictions unchanged.

### 194 (results): `unsigned` opens 19 musl functions; 7 of 18 gain on random ints, 4 hold on realistic workloads

Census (`data/c_census_unsigned_20261115/`): musl 13 → 32 / 2060, Lua 2 → 2 / 1125. New in musl: the ctype one-liners (isalpha, iscntrl, isdigit, isgraph, islower, isprint, isspace, isupper) plus hexval (×2), crypt's ascii_to_bin, setup_salt, rol, ror, mo_lookup's swapc, powf's checkint and zeroinfnan, random's lcg31, rand_r's temper. Integer suffixes drop from 217 to 156 (the `u` ones). Predictions: musl 21–35 — **held** (32). Lua 2–4 — **held** (2).
Superopt rerun (`data/superopt_unsigned2_20261115/`, fixed binary and gate): 54/54 equivalent. The battery's check prints NOT_EQUIVALENT for rol and ror only because of its 50% guard: all 6 runs returned the original byte for byte, and `--min-compared 0` gives OK. The exhaustive gate refuted 10 timed winners: checkint 3 + 3 (seeds 10, 12), all through the new in-domain mismatch path, so checkint returns the reference; zeroinfnan 1 + 3. setup_salt's check (24-step loop × 2^32) exceeds the 60 s limit, so its level is `tested`. Its 3 outputs, compared natively outside superopt with no limit (107–139 s each), have 0 mismatches.
Gains, 190 rule, inline, on superopt's own workload (uniform ints with 30% small): ascii_to_bin 1.15 / 1.18 / 1.18, hexval 1.13 / 1.43 (2 seeds), isalpha 3.13 / 1.83 / 2.84, iscntrl 2.74 / 1.21 / 2.71, isspace 2.36 / 2.11 / 2.19, zeroinfnan 1.55 / 1.38 (2 seeds), setup_salt 1.24 / 1.29 / 1.31 (ref 15 ns). All others return the reference, within 0.99–1.00. The external self-controls (inline) are all within 0.99–1.01.
Predictions: all equivalent — **held**; unary ones `exhaustive` or the reference — **failed** for setup_salt (gate timeout; proved outside). No result < 0.95 — **held** (lowest 0.990). The six one-compare ctype functions do not gain — **failed** (isalpha; the other five hold). setup_salt gains on ≥ 2 seeds — **held** (3). 1–4 of 18 gain — **failed** (7).
Workload check, registered in conversation before measuring (`c_speedup.py --inline --inputs-file`, new option): the ctype gains add an early exit (`if (c <= 32) return 0`, `if (c < 0) return 0`, `if (c >> 6) return 0`). That exit is almost always taken on uniform ints, which are almost never characters. So they were re-timed on bytes 0..255, the README's text, hex digits (hexval) and the crypt alphabet `./0-9A-Za-z` (ascii_to_bin), with self-controls 0.96–1.02 (`workloads.tsv`). Prediction: the ctype gains fall to ≤ 1.05 or regress (70%).
- isalpha: seeds 10 / 11 keep 1.69 / 1.83 (bytes) and 1.77 / 1.83 (text); seed 12 1.05 / 1.09.
- iscntrl: 1.61 / 1.21 / 1.61 (bytes), 1.60 / 1.21 / 1.61 (text).
- isspace: only seed 10 holds, 1.85 / 2.28; seeds 11 and 12 regress to 0.80–0.99.
- hexval regresses on hex digits, 0.23 / 0.58 (its uniform-int gain is an artifact).
- ascii_to_bin on its alphabet: 1.08 / 1.09 / 1.12.

Prediction — **failed** for isalpha and iscntrl, **held** for isspace (by the 2-seed rule) and hexval. Gains that survive realistic workloads: isalpha, iscntrl (2-seed rule), and setup_salt and zeroinfnan, whose natural inputs are any 32-bit pattern.
What the survivors are:
- isalpha seed 11 is branch-free: `((c|32) + (-2147483645 - 100)) <= -2147483623`, the unsigned range check rewritten as a signed one. Its compiled code differs from the original's by one instruction in the whole timing loop, `setl` for `setbe` (and `add` for `sub`). On this CPU (i5-8250U, Skylake family) `setbe` reads two flags and costs 2 µops, `setl` 1 µop. Instruction selection, specific to Intel; not an algorithm.
- setup_salt: the 24-step loop unrolled ×4, with `obit` (the descending bit) as the loop condition, so the counter `i` is gone. Induction-variable elimination plus unrolling: the first real-code loop rewrite.
- iscntrl, zeroinfnan: an early exit plus a cheaper flag test, workload-dependent by construction.

Reading: `unsigned` is the gate that pays: 19 functions, 18 distinct, real code that does work (bit loops, hash tempering, float classification). The exhaustive gate, extended to references that may fail, caught 6 wrong checkint winners, the same kind that slipped through as `tested` in the first run. Uniform random ints are the wrong workload for character functions: half of the apparent gains (hexval, isspace 2 seeds, ascii_to_bin) vanish on character inputs. The superoptimizer should accept a workload from the user and time on it (open). Unsigned `/` and `%` are next in this direction.

### 195 (registered): superopt on a user-given workload

`superopt --workload FILE` (one input per line, as for `c-import --eval`). The in-domain inputs replace the random ones in the compiled timing (up to 512) and in the model cost (an even sample of 64 / 24). The first 64 also join the tests. Correctness is checked as before, on every input: grid, boundaries, CEGIS, and the exhaustive gate for unary functions. A workload on which the reference never completes is an error. The battery takes `EXTRA="--workload ..."` and `EVAL_INPUTS=FILE` (external measurements on that file); `summary.tsv` gains `user_workload`. Test `superopt_user_workload_keeps_in_domain_inputs_as_tests`; 311/311.
Workloads (`scripts/make_char_workloads.py`, committed in `tests/c_samples_musl_lua/workloads/`): each kind has a search file and a held-out eval file of 1024 inputs from the same distribution. text = the bytes of README.md (search) / docs/superopt.md (eval); hex = uniform over 0-9a-fA-F; b64 = uniform over `./0-9A-Za-z`.
Design: the 10 character samples of 194, the 8 ctype functions on text, hexval on hex, ascii_to_bin on b64. Arm WL: `--workload <kind>_search.txt`, seeds 10–12, 60 s, `--time-top 4`. Arm RAND: the 194 outputs (`data/superopt_unsigned2_20261115/`, same seeds, random timing workload). Both arms are measured inline on `<kind>_eval.txt`, with self-controls on the same file.
Predictions:
- WL has no result < 0.95 on the held-out workload (85%); RAND has ≥ 3 (90%: isspace seeds 11–12 and hexval seeds 11–12 regressed on similar inputs in 194);
- WL gains (190 rule, held-out) on isalpha and iscntrl (60%);
- WL hexval and isspace gain on ≤ 1 seed each (65%);
- WL total gaining samples ≥ RAND's on the held-out workload (70%).

### 195 (results): with the user's workload, the regressions are gone and isspace gains 2.3–2.5× on held-out text

Raw: `data/superopt_workload_{text,hex,b64}_20261115/` (arm WL; external inline measurements and self-controls on the held-out `*_eval.txt`), `data/superopt_workload_rand_heldout_20261115/rand_heldout.tsv` (arm RAND: the 194 outputs re-timed inline on the same held-out files, serially after the battery). Self-controls on the held-out files: 0.96–1.05, wider than on random ints because the inputs are sub-ns character tests. 30/30 WL outputs equivalent; every winner is level `exhaustive` (no gate rejection); all 1024 workload inputs are in-domain.
Held-out inline speedups, seeds 10 / 11 / 12, WL vs RAND:
- isalpha: 1.86 / 1.81 / 1.87 vs 1.77 / 1.83 / 1.11;
- iscntrl: 1.27 / 1.55 / 1.36 vs 1.61 / 1.23 / 1.62;
- isspace: 1.11 / 2.48 / 2.33 vs 2.16 / 0.97 / 0.78;
- hexval: reference ×3 (1.05 / 1.02 / 1.09) vs 0.97 / 0.23 / 0.61;
- ascii_to_bin: 1.14 / 1.14 / 1.08 vs 1.19 / 1.12 / 1.16;
- isdigit, isgraph, islower, isprint, isupper: the reference in both arms, 0.94–1.09.

Gains (190 rule): WL 4 samples (isalpha, iscntrl, isspace, ascii_to_bin), RAND 3 (isalpha, iscntrl, ascii_to_bin). Results < 0.95: WL 1 (isgraph seed 12, 0.943, a returned reference, so the original timed against itself: noise, like 190b's), RAND 3 (isspace 0.78, hexval 0.23 and 0.61).
Predictions: WL none < 0.95 — **failed** as written, by one returned reference at 0.943, which is noise (no candidate below 1.02). RAND ≥ 3 — **held** (3). WL gains on isalpha and iscntrl — **held**. WL hexval and isspace gain on ≤ 1 seed each — **held** for hexval (0), **failed** for isspace (3). WL gaining samples ≥ RAND's — **held** (4 vs 3).
isspace WL seed 11: `if (c >= 33) return 0;` and then the original's two tests, with the range test as a signed compare (`setl` for `setbe`, as isalpha in 194). On text, about 80% of the bytes are above 32, so the branch is mostly taken and cheap. RAND's isspace exits on `c >> 6` or `c < 0`, which suits uniform ints and loses on text.
Reading: timing on the user's inputs does what it should. The candidates that regressed on characters (hexval, isspace ×2) are not chosen. The one sample whose gain depended on the workload (isspace) now gets a program tuned to text and gains on held-out text. The gains that do not depend on the workload (isalpha's `setl`, ascii_to_bin) stay. The result is tied to the workload by design: isspace WL's early exit would cost on whitespace-heavy input. `--workload` should be described that way to the user.

### 196 (registered): unsigned `/` and `%`

Lowered with signed Mini-C division only (Hacker's Delight 9-3), the quotient in a temporary, the remainder `a - q*b`:
- constant power-of-two divisor: a logical shift, or a mask for the remainder;
- constant divisor ≥ 2^31: q = (a ≥u d);
- other constant divisor: `a / d` when a ≥ 0, else `q = ((a >>> 1) / d) << 1`, plus 1 when `a - q*d ≥u d`;
- variable divisor: the same, with the ≥ 2^31 case as a branch;
- a zero divisor still fails at run time, as in C.

Test `c_import_unsigned_division_and_remainder_match_native` (7 functions × 529 argument pairs against native unsigned C++, a zero divisor must fail); 312/312. Round trip (2000 inputs): `x / y + x % y`, a digit-count loop with `x /= 10`, all constant-divisor kinds mixed with int, and an array function with `% 13u`: all OK.
Census prediction, stated in the conversation before the rerun (the 194 census lists only one function whose first gate is an unsigned `/` or `%`, musl's casemap): musl 32 → 32–33, Lua 2 → 2 (80%). The superopt part follows only if a non-stub function opens.

### 196 (results): correct, but it opens no new real function

Census (`data/c_census_udiv_20261115/`): musl 32 / 2060, Lua 2 / 1125, the same functions as in 194. casemap gets past the division and stops at its next gate, indexing a global table (`indexing a non-parameter`). Prediction: musl 32–33, Lua 2 — **held** (32, 2). No superopt run (registered condition: none opened).
Reading: in this code, unsigned division and remainder appear inside functions that also use `size_t`/`long`, pointers or global tables. Removing them as a gate was cheap and is tested, but by itself it opens nothing. Census ranking for what would: types (pointers, `char`, `long`, `size_t`: 541 musl), string literals (267), unknown definition heads (185), integer `L` suffixes (156).

### 197 (registered): `char` and `short` scalars

Types `char` (signed, as on gcc/x86), `signed`/`unsigned char`, `short`, `unsigned short` and the <stdint.h> names of 8, 16 and 32 bits. A value narrower than 32 bits promotes to int in every expression, so expressions keep their 194 types (int or unsigned). Storage converts: assignments, compound assignments and `++`, initializers, parameters (the prologue converts each argument, as the caller does in C), results, helper arguments and results, and casts. The conversion is `x & mask` for unsigned and `(x << (32 - n)) >> (32 - n)` for signed, folded for constants. Still rejected: `char *` and other pointers (only `int *a, int n`), a narrow array length, and `long`.

The ABI keeps the C spelling of each type, and the emitter writes the original signature (`<stdint.h>` names become built-in ones). `c-import` prints `narrow=1` for such signatures. A caller in another unit that declares `int f(int)` would read undefined upper result bits, so `c_roundtrip.py` / `c_speedup.py` then compile the target as `<new>_typed` behind an int-signature wrapper. `CompiledTimer` (one unit) renames the new return types. Test `c_import_char_and_short_truncate_on_storage_and_promote_in_expressions` (7 functions × 784 argument pairs against native C++); 313/313. Round trip: a char → char function, an `unsigned char` helper, an array hash into `uint8_t`, `int16_t`/`int8_t` arithmetic: all OK; superopt runs on two of them (8 s, timed) return the original with self-controls 0.98–1.03.
Census predictions (same sources and directories): musl 32 → 36–55 (60%: most `char` is `char *`, which stays rejected); Lua 2 → 2–5 (70%). Superopt on what newly imports follows, registered with its results, with a character workload for character functions (195).
Superopt part, registered after the census and before the run: the one new function, musl's crypt `ascii_is_unsafe(unsigned char ch)` (`!ch || ch == '\n' || ch == ':'`), copied verbatim to `tests/c_samples_musl_lua/`; round trip OK. Its inputs are salt characters, so the run uses the crypt-alphabet workload: `SAMPLES=tests/c_samples_musl_lua/musl_ascii_is_unsafe.c EVAL_INPUTS=tests/c_samples_musl_lua/workloads/b64_eval.txt scripts/run_superopt_battery.sh data/superopt_char8_20261115 "10 11 12" 60 1 4 "--workload tests/c_samples_musl_lua/workloads/b64_search.txt"`. Predictions: all 3 equivalent with level `exhaustive` or the reference (95%); gain (190 rule, held-out) on ≥ 2 seeds (35%: three compares on a byte, but none of the alphabet is unsafe, so an early exit on `ch > ':'` could pay, as for isspace on text).

### 197 (results): `char` opens one function; its run exposes winners tuned to one input sequence

Census (`data/c_census_char8_20261115/`): musl 32 → 33 / 2060 (+ascii_is_unsafe), Lua 2 → 2. Of 693 functions whose first gate changed, the ones that used `char` now stop at `char *`. As a parameter that is `the array parameter must be int *` (158). As a return type it is `expected an identifier` (128, e.g. `char *crypt(...)`). The `char` gate stood in front of the pointer gate. Predictions: musl 36–55 — **failed** (33). Lua 2–5 — **held** (2).
Superopt (`data/superopt_char8_20261115/`): 3/3 equivalent. Seeds 10 and 12 return the original. Seed 11 returns `if (ch & 1) return 0; v = ch & 254; return v == 0 | v == 10 | v == 58;`, level `exhaustive`, which measured 1.27× in the loop. External inline on the held-out b64 file: **0.60×**. Re-measured by hand (self-controls 0.95–1.07):
- on `b64_search.txt` (the search's inputs): 1.55 / 1.53 inline;
- on `b64_eval.txt` (same distribution, other seed): 0.57 / 0.58 inline;
- call mode: 0.38 on both.

The candidate's branch on the low bit is cheap only on the input sequence the search timed it on. The search times hundreds of candidates on one fixed sequence of 512 inputs, repeated, and keeps the fastest, so it selects for luck with the predictor on that sequence. This is section 190's memorization, now through selection rather than repetition alone. The 195 results were measured on held-out files and stand, but nothing inside superopt guarded against this.
Predictions: equivalent, `exhaustive` or reference — **held**. Gain on ≥ 2 seeds — **failed** (none on held-out; one regression).
Fix (`Superoptimizer::run`): confirmation inputs. The user's workload is split into alternate inputs. The even ones are timed in the loop, the odd ones only in the final re-measurement of the top three (the noise threshold now covers self-controls on both sets; `confirm_self_control` is printed). Without a workload, the confirmation set is a second random set (another seed). 313/313.

### 197b (registered): the confirmation split, rerun

Run: ascii_is_unsafe (b64 workload) plus the four samples that gained in 195 (isalpha, iscntrl, isspace on text; ascii_to_bin on b64), seeds 10–12, 60 s, `--time-top 4`, external measurement on the held-out `*_eval.txt` as in 195 (`data/superopt_confirm_{text,b64}_20261115/`). Predictions:
- no result < 0.95 on held-out, other than returned references (85%);
- ascii_is_unsafe returns the reference or gains ≥ 1.10 on held-out on every seed (85%);
- isalpha, iscntrl, isspace and ascii_to_bin keep a 190-rule gain on held-out (70% each; isalpha's `setl` does not depend on the inputs, so 90% for it).

### 197b (results): with confirmation inputs, no lucky winner survives and the real gains stay

Raw: `data/superopt_confirm_{text,b64}_20261115/`. 15/15 equivalent. Confirmation self-controls are 0.997–1.005, and external self-controls (inline, held-out) are 0.98–1.03.
Held-out inline speedups, seeds 10 / 11 / 12 (in brackets, 195 without confirmation):
- isalpha: 1.88 / 1.84 / 2.00 (1.86 / 1.81 / 1.87);
- iscntrl: 1.48 / 1.22 / 1.32 (1.27 / 1.55 / 1.36);
- isspace: 1.11 / 3.71 / 2.78 (1.11 / 2.48 / 2.33);
- ascii_to_bin: 1.14 / 1.12 / 1.29 (1.14 / 1.14 / 1.08);
- ascii_is_unsafe: the original on all three, 1.01 / 1.02 / 0.97 (197: seed 11 returned the 0.60× candidate).

All winners are `exhaustive`. The speedup measured on the confirmation inputs (1.07–1.92) is lower than the held-out inline figures but agrees in direction on every run.
Predictions: none < 0.95 other than returned references — **held** (lowest 0.970, a reference). ascii_is_unsafe the reference or ≥ 1.10 — **held** (reference ×3). isalpha, iscntrl, isspace, ascii_to_bin keep a gain — **held** (all four, 3 seeds each).
Reading: timing a whole search on one input sequence selects for that sequence. Confirming the top three on inputs the search never timed removes the lucky winner (ascii_is_unsafe) at no cost to the four real gains, which are equal or larger here. Without `--workload`, the confirmation set is a second random set. Earlier random-workload results (190–194) were not confirmed this way, but their gains were re-measured externally on fresh random inputs (seed 1 of c_speedup.py, another sequence), which plays the same role.

### 198 (registered): how far the search is from the best version known, on the 27 accepted samples

Question: on the functions the front end already accepts, is the search near the best known program? If it is far, the next lever is the search, not coverage.
For each of the 27 samples in `tests/c_samples_musl_lua/`, one hand version, the best I know, written before any of them was timed:
- the unsigned range test as a signed compare after `^ INT_MIN` (`setl` for `setbe`; isalpha's 194 gain), in digit, isalpha, iscntrl, isdigit, isgraph, islower, isprint, isupper;
- bitmasks: isspace (`(0x80001F00 >> (c-1)) & 1` for c-1 < 32), ascii_is_unsafe (a 64-bit mask), days_in_month (a 2-bit-per-month 64-bit table);
- branchless: filterpc (`pc | -(pc < jmptarget)`), abs, ascii_to_bin (`sch - 46 - 7*(sch>='A') - 6*(sch>='a')`), isblank (`|`), isascii (`(unsigned)c < 128`);
- a 128-byte table: hexval;
- known algorithms: setup_salt as a 24-bit bit reversal (bswap + three swap steps), checkint with `ctz` (`(tz >= s) + (tz > s)`), is_leap as Neri–Schneider's `!(y & (y % 25 ? 3 : 15))`, swapc with `__builtin_bswap32`;
- lcg31, rol, ror, temper, toascii, zeroinfnan: I know nothing better, so the hand version is the original.

Each hand version agrees with the original natively on 20,000 random inputs (`c_roundtrip.py --candidate`, domain-filtered), and the 18 unary ones on all 2^32 arguments (0 mismatches).
Mini-C can express the signed-compare, branchless, isspace, is_leap and setup_salt versions. It cannot express the table, 64-bit and builtin ones (hexval, ascii_is_unsafe, days_in_month, checkint, swapc).

Measurement: one battery, run serially, `c_speedup.py --inline`, with the original against itself, the hand version and the search's three winners of its latest valid run (seeds 10–12) for each sample. Each sample uses the distribution its search ran on:
- text_eval for isalpha, iscntrl, isspace (197b);
- b64_eval for ascii_to_bin, ascii_is_unsafe (197b);
- hex_eval for hexval (195);
- random (seed 1) for the rest (192b, 193, 194 rerun).

Gap = hand / best search seed. The best known is the larger of hand and best seed.
Predictions:
- gap ≥ 1.10 on 4–8 of 27 (60%): setup_salt (a gap ≥ 2, 85%), hexval (70%), checkint (50%), and the signed-compare ctype functions on random, where the search returned the original (isdigit, islower, isupper, isgraph, isprint, digit: ≥ 3 of these 6 show a gap, 40%);
- the search beats the hand version by ≥ 1.10 somewhere (is_leap or zeroinfnan, 50%);
- no gap ≥ 1.10 on isalpha, iscntrl, isblank, ascii_to_bin, abs, isascii, toascii, filterpc, swapc, lcg31, rol, ror, temper (80%).

Decision rule:
- gaps mostly where Mini-C can express the hand version: the search is the bottleneck;
- gaps mostly in table/64-bit/builtin versions: the language is;
- few gaps: coverage (the 199 pilot) is the lever.

### 198 (deviation, registered before the rerun): 512 repeated inputs still let the predictor learn branchy code

The battery above ran as registered (`ceiling_512.tsv`). Self-controls were 0.84–1.14, and several branch-free hand versions measured far below the original (is_leap 0.51, checkint 0.40, filterpc 0.54, abs 0.64), so I re-measured is_leap with `--inputs 60000` (no input repeats within a pass):

| is_leap (inline) | 512 inputs | 60,000 inputs |
|---|---|---|
| original, ns/call | 1.92 | 5.84 |
| hand (Neri–Schneider) | 0.59× | **1.58×** |
| search seed 11 | 3.68× | **1.32×** |
| original vs itself | — | 0.99× |

On 512 random inputs, repeated thousands of times, the original's `%`-guarded branches are learnt by the predictor (1.9 ns vs 5.8 ns), and the ranking inverts. 190b moved from 24 to 512 inputs for this reason; 512 is not enough for branchy scalar code on random inputs. This touches every external inline measurement since 190b (fixed 512-input or 1024-line files) and the search's in-loop timer, which repeats 512 inputs too.
Rerun of the same battery: random samples on `--inputs 65536`, workload samples on held-out 65,536-input files (`make_char_workloads.py OUTDIR --large 65536`: text from `docs/findings/`, hex/b64 with another seed). Predictions as registered above, evaluated on the rerun; additional prediction: the self-controls tighten to 0.95–1.05 (70%).

### 198 (results): five gaps, three of them in Mini-C; the search's timer is part of the problem

**Corrected below (198, correction):** the six workload rows (isalpha, iscntrl, isspace, ascii_is_unsafe, ascii_to_bin, hexval) were measured on 512 inputs, not 65,536.

Raw: `data/superopt_ceiling_20261115/`:
- `ceiling_512.tsv`: the first battery;
- `ceiling_r{1,2,3}.tsv`: three repetitions on 65,536 inputs;
- `ceiling_median.tsv`: per-cell medians.

Script `scripts/run_ceiling_battery.sh`; hand versions in `tests/c_samples_musl_lua/hand/`. Median inline speedups over the original:

| sample | workload | self | hand | best search seed | hand / search | Mini-C can express the hand version |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| filterpc | random | 1.01 | **5.92** | 1.01 | **5.87** | yes (`pc \| -(pc < j)`) |
| setup_salt | random | 1.04 | **8.30** | 1.18 | **7.03** | yes (swap steps) |
| hexval | hex | 0.98 | **3.10** | 1.07 | **2.90** | no (table) |
| swapc | random | 1.06 | **1.62** | 1.10 | **1.47** | no (`bswap`) |
| is_leap | random | 1.00 | **1.74** | 1.32 | **1.32** | yes (`% 25`, `& 15`) |
| isspace | text | 1.01 | 1.02 | **5.23** | 0.19 | yes |
| iscntrl | text | 1.05 | 0.99 | **1.26** | 0.79 | yes |
| ascii_to_bin | b64 | 0.97 | 0.99 | **1.19** | 0.84 | yes |
| isalpha | text | 1.01 | 1.41 | 1.45 | 0.97 | yes |
| days_in_month | random | 1.01 | 0.63 | 1.11 | 0.57 | no (64-bit table) |
| checkint | random | 1.00 | 0.80 | 0.99 | 0.81 | no (`ctz`) |
| zeroinfnan | random | 0.99 | 0.99 (original) | 0.98; seeds 10/11: **0.18 / 0.20** | — | — |

Every other sample has hand and best search within 0.84–1.13 of the original. That is inside the noise of these sub-ns functions: self-controls range 0.69–1.24 per run and 0.85–1.13 as medians.

Findings:
- **The search returned code 5× slower than the original** (zeroinfnan seeds 10 and 11, 194 rerun; all three repetitions at 0.17–0.21). The winner adds a branch on `1 < 2*ix`, random on random inputs. On the 512 repeated inputs of the in-loop timer it measured 1.5×. On the 512-input external check it also measured 1.38–1.55×, and it was proved `exhaustive`. Correct and much slower: the confirmation split (197b) would not have caught it either, since its random confirmation set is also 512 inputs repeated.
- **The same memorization hides real gains.** filterpc's branch-free hand version is 0.54× on 512 inputs and 5.92× on 65,536. The search, timing on 512, sees the original's branch as cheap. It never had a reason to keep a branch-free candidate.
- **Gaps (hand / search ≥ 1.10): 5 of 27.** Mini-C can express three: filterpc (one edit, hidden by the timer), setup_salt (a bit reversal, several coordinated edits) and is_leap (`% 25`). It cannot express hexval (table) or swapc (`bswap`).
- **The search beats my hand versions on 4.** isspace on text is 5.2× with an early exit tuned to the workload. Then iscntrl, ascii_to_bin and days_in_month (branch order). Two of my "known algorithms" lose on random inputs: checkint's `ctz` and days_in_month's 64-bit table.

Predictions:
- gaps on 4–8 of 27 — **held** (5);
- setup_salt gap ≥ 2 — **held** (7.0);
- hexval — **held** (2.9);
- checkint — **failed** (hand 0.80);
- ≥ 3 of the 6 signed-compare ctype functions show a gap — **failed** (0: on random inputs the `setl` trick is within noise);
- the search beats the hand version on is_leap or zeroinfnan — **failed** as stated (it does on isspace, iscntrl, ascii_to_bin, days_in_month);
- no gap on the 13 listed — **failed** (filterpc, swapc);
- self-controls 0.95–1.05 — **failed** (medians 0.85–1.13).

Reading, by the registered rule: the gaps are mostly in programs Mini-C can express (3 of 5), so the search, not the language or coverage, is the first bottleneck. Part of it is the search's timer, not its moves. Timing on a short repeated input sequence lets the branch predictor learn it. That makes branchy candidates look cheap, which both accepts a 5× regression and hides a 5.9× gain. The rest is reach: a 24-bit reversal needs several coordinated edits.
Consequence: the gains measured since 190b on 512 repeated inputs are optimistic for branchy winners, and pessimistic for branch-free ones.

### 198 (correction): the six workload samples, re-measured on 65,536 inputs

The six workload rows above were measured on 512 inputs, repeated. Each workload sample got `--inputs-file *_eval_large.txt` without `--inputs`, so `c_speedup.py` took its default of 512 (`inputs 512` in `ceiling_r*.tsv`; 30 of 135 rows). `c_speedup.py` now defaults to 65,536 scalar or 16,384 array inputs, and resamples a shorter file in a random order. The six were re-measured three times on 65,536 inputs (`ceiling_w{1,2,3}.tsv`). Text is contiguous text from `docs/findings/`; hex and b64 are uniform.

| sample | workload | self | hand | search 10 / 11 / 12 | hand / best search |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| hexval | hex | 1.02 | **21.69** | 1.00 / 1.00 / 1.00 | **21.65** |
| isalpha | text | 1.06 | 1.91 | 1.91 / 1.86 / 1.90 | 1.00 |
| iscntrl | text | 0.98 | 1.21 | 1.47 / 1.25 / 1.23 | 0.82 |
| isspace | text | 1.02 | 0.36 | 1.12 / **0.39 / 0.41** | 0.32 |
| ascii_to_bin | b64 | 0.99 | 0.44 | 1.04 / 1.04 / 0.99 | 0.42 |
| ascii_is_unsafe | b64 | 1.00 | 0.97 | 1.00 / 1.01 / 1.01 | 0.96 |

Self-controls are 0.96–1.08 per run, tighter than on 512.
- isspace: the 197b winners of seeds 11 and 12 (early exit at `c >= 33`), 3.71× and 2.78× held-out on 1024 bytes, run at **0.39× and 0.41×** on 65,536 bytes of real text. Their gain was the predictor learning a 1024-byte sequence. Of 197b's four "real gains", isalpha (1.86–1.91×), iscntrl (1.23–1.47×) and isspace seed 10 (1.12×) stand. ascii_to_bin falls to 0.99–1.04×.
- hexval: the original's two range tests mispredict on uniform hex digits, and the table wins **21.7×**. That is the largest gap of the study, and not expressible in Mini-C.
- My bitmask isspace (0.36×) and branch-free ascii_to_bin (0.44×) lose: on these inputs the original's branches are predictable.

With the correction:
- gaps hand / search ≥ 1.10: 5 of 27 (filterpc, setup_salt, hexval, swapc, is_leap), three expressible in Mini-C, so the registered reading stands;
- the search beats the hand versions by ≥ 1.10 on iscntrl, isspace and days_in_month (branch order);
- the prediction "the search beats the hand version somewhere" holds on these three, not on ascii_to_bin.

Two more search results returned as gains were slower than the original: isspace seeds 11 and 12, plus zeroinfnan's two. Every one was measured, inside the search and outside, on a short repeated input sequence.

### 198b (registered): the 27 samples rerun with the 65,536-input timer

Change (commit 90cc143): the in-loop timer and its confirmation set are each one pass of 65,536 scalar inputs (16,384 arrays). A user workload's even and odd halves are resampled in a random order to that size.
Run: the 27 samples of 198, seeds 10–12 (matched with the runs 198 measured), 60 s, `--time-top 4`, and the same search workloads:
- `text_search.txt` for isalpha, iscntrl, isspace;
- `b64_search.txt` for ascii_is_unsafe, ascii_to_bin;
- `hex_search.txt` for hexval;
- random for the rest.

External measurement with the battery's `c_speedup.py`, now on 65,536 inputs: `EVAL_INPUTS` = the `*_eval_large.txt` files of `data/superopt_ceiling_20261115/`, or random. Output in `data/superopt_timer65k_20261115/`. Baseline: the old arm's medians in 198 (correction included).
Predictions:
- no search-made result below 0.95 externally, over all 81 runs (75%);
- in particular zeroinfnan and isspace have none (85%);
- filterpc gains (190 rule) on ≥ 2 seeds, now that the timer can see the branch-free form (45%);
- samples with a 190-rule gain: 5–9 (60%; old arm on 65,536: isalpha, iscntrl, is_leap, setup_salt);
- setup_salt's gap to the hand version stays ≥ 4 (80%: the bit reversal is a reach problem, not a timing one).

### 198b (results): no regression in 81 runs, filterpc 13.6x, ascii_is_unsafe 3.6x; 198's magnitudes depend on the session

Raw: `data/superopt_timer65k_20261115/{text,b64,hex,random}/` (searches, `summary.tsv`, `self_control.tsv`), plus two measurements in `data/superopt_ceiling_20261115/`:
- `ceiling_n{1,2,3}.tsv`: the new winners (`RUNS=data/superopt_timer65k_20261115 scripts/run_ceiling_battery.sh`);
- `ceiling_o{1,2,3}.tsv`: 198's old winners, re-measured in the same session.

Medians in `ceiling_on_median.tsv`. All 81 new winners verified; self-controls 0.99–1.03 in every run.

**Deviation: matched remeasure.** The baseline was to be 198's medians, but they cannot be compared across sessions. 198's own hand versions measure very differently today, with the self-control 1.00 in both sessions:

| hand version | 198 (yesterday) | today |
|---|---|---|
| filterpc | 5.92 | 11.76 |
| swapc | 1.62 | 4.34 |
| is_leap | 1.74 | 2.43 |
| setup_salt | 8.30 | 9.12 |

The original filterpc took 8.7 ns per call yesterday (empty-loop overhead 2.2 ns) and 5.3 ns today (overhead 0.8). The cause is not verified: the governor is `powersave`, and no other run was writing to data/ at the time. The self-control cannot catch this: it checks the measurement's symmetry, not the machine's state. So the comparison is old against new winners in one session (`o` vs `n`). The 198 conclusions hold in sign; their magnitudes are session-dependent.

Median inline speedups over the original, today. Rows where an arm has a seed ≥ 1.10 or < 0.95, or the hand version has a gap:

| sample | hand | old 10 / 11 / 12 | new 10 / 11 / 12 | hand / best new |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| filterpc | 11.76 | 1.00 / 1.00 / 1.00 | 1.00 / **13.55 / 13.54** | 0.87 |
| ascii_is_unsafe (b64) | 0.97 | 1.00 / 1.00 / 1.00 | **3.27 / 3.58 / 3.58** | 0.27 |
| zeroinfnan | 1.00 | **0.11 / 0.11** / 1.00 | 1.00 / 1.26 / 1.25 | 0.79 |
| isspace (text) | 0.35 | 1.10 / **0.37 / 0.41** | 1.10 / 1.10 / 1.10 | 0.32 |
| is_leap | 2.43 | 1.04 / 1.20 / 1.07 | 1.30 / 1.31 / 1.00 | **1.85** |
| isascii | 1.00 | 1.00 / 1.00 / 1.00 | 1.15 / 1.00 / 1.15 | 0.87 |
| iscntrl (text) | 1.19 | 1.46 / 1.19 / 1.19 | 1.46 / 1.19 / 1.46 | 0.82 |
| days_in_month | 0.77 | 1.00 / 1.83 / 1.83 | 1.83 / 1.29 / 1.83 | 0.42 |
| setup_salt | 9.12 | 1.26 / 1.30 / 1.32 | 1.30 / 1.32 / 1.26 | **6.90** |
| isalpha (text) | 1.80 | 1.81 / 1.81 / 1.81 | 1.81 / 1.81 / 1.80 | 0.99 |
| isblank | 1.00 | 1.19 / 1.18 / 1.18 | 1.18 / 1.18 / 1.19 | 0.84 |
| hexval (hex) | 21.89 | 1.00 / 1.00 / 1.00 | 1.00 / 1.00 / 1.00 | **21.87** |
| swapc | 4.34 | 1.00 / 1.00 / 1.00 | 1.00 / 1.00 / 1.00 | **4.34** |

Every other sample is 0.99–1.01 on every seed in both arms.

- **No regression.** The old arm has 4 results below 0.95 today: zeroinfnan 10/11 at 0.11 (9× slower) and isspace 11/12 at 0.37/0.41. The new arm has 0 of 81.
- **Gains the old timer hid:**
  - filterpc `arr[(pc >= j)] = -1; return arr[0];`: a store to an index chosen by the comparison, beating my hand version `pc | -(pc < j)` (11.76);
  - ascii_is_unsafe `(c == 58) + (c == 10) + (c == 0)`: branch-free, against the original's short-circuit chain, which mispredicts on uniform b64 bytes;
  - zeroinfnan, a branch-free comparison;
  - is_leap 1.30 on two seeds (`(y + 12) & 3`, then `& 12`);
  - isascii `(c & (127 - c)) == 0`.
- **Samples with a 190-rule gain** (≥ 1.10 on ≥ 2 seeds), today:
  - old arm: 4 (isalpha, isblank, days_in_month, setup_salt);
  - new arm: 11 (filterpc, ascii_is_unsafe, zeroinfnan, is_leap, isascii, iscntrl, days_in_month, setup_salt, isalpha, isblank, plus isspace at the threshold with 1.10 on all three seeds, run medians 1.099–1.106).
- **Gaps hand / search ≥ 1.10, today:** 4 of 27.
  - setup_salt 6.9 and is_leap 1.85: expressible in Mini-C;
  - hexval 21.9 (table) and swapc 4.3 (`bswap`): not expressible.
  - filterpc's gap is closed: the search now beats the hand version.

Predictions:
- no result < 0.95 in 81 runs — **held** (0; the old arm, re-measured, has 4);
- none on zeroinfnan or isspace — **held**;
- filterpc gains on ≥ 2 seeds — **held** (13.5× on 2);
- 5–9 samples with a gain — **failed** (11, above the range: the long timer lifted ascii_is_unsafe, zeroinfnan, is_leap, isascii and filterpc at once, and dropped nothing);
- setup_salt's gap stays ≥ 4 — **held** (6.9; its winners are the same 1.3× in both arms).

Reading:
- The 65,536-input timer is the change. It removes every slow result, and its gains come from seeing a mispredicted branch: a short repeated sequence does not show one, and long random inputs do.
- What is left of the ceiling is in two places. One is reach: setup_salt's bit reversal, and is_leap's `% 25`. The other is outside Mini-C: tables and `bswap`.
- Method: a gain ratio is only comparable within one session. From now on the baseline arm is re-measured in the same session as the new one.

### 199 (registered): string pilot, eight musl/ELF string functions on int arrays

Question: does the superoptimizer find measurable gains in byte-loop string code, the largest class in the census (266 musl functions take only `char *` plus scalars)? Samples (commit bf2bc27), hand-ported to `int f(int *a, int n)`:
- `tests/c_samples_strings_pilot/`: strlen, atoi, strcmp, strcasecmp, memcmp (two strings as halves), keyhash, sysv_hash, gnu_hash;
- round-trip and libc-checked.

Run: seeds 10–12, 60 s, `--time-top 4` (in-loop timer, 16,384 arrays per pass, 198b), `--workload` the matching `*_search.txt`:

| samples | workload |
|---|---|
| strlen, keyhash, sysv_hash, gnu_hash | words |
| atoi | numbers |
| strcmp, memcmp | pairs |
| strcasecmp | casepairs |

External measurement on `data/superopt_strings_pilot_20261115/*_eval_large.txt` (16,384 strings, from other text). Equivalence round trip on `*_eval.txt` (new `RT_INPUTS` in `run_superopt_battery.sh`: random arrays rarely hold a NUL). Baseline: the original, with the battery's self-controls in the same session. Output: `data/superopt_strings_199_20261116/`.
Predictions:
- all 24 winners pass the round trip (90%);
- no result below 0.95 inline (75%);
- 190-rule gains (inline ≥ 1.10 on ≥ 2 seeds) on 0–2 of 8 (65%: gcc compiles byte loops well, and the search's moves are expression-level);
- strcasecmp is among them (40%: the inlined `tolower` has branches the search can remove);
- strlen does not gain (85%);
- on ≥ 4 samples the model cost falls but the timer returns the original (60%; smoke test: strcmp 1600 → 1024, measured 1.00).

### 199 (deviation, registered before the rerun): the search accepted wrong string code

In the first run (`data/superopt_strings_199_20261116/`), strcasecmp's winners for seeds 11 and 12 fail the round trip: 104 and 71 of the 1,024 eval pairs, and 77 and 54 of the search workload itself. Both were reported `verified yes`, measured 1.49× and 1.36× inline, and the in-loop timer accepted them.

Cause: only the first 64 workload inputs are tests. The counterexample search (CEGIS) tries exhaustive small arrays, boundary inputs and 2,000 random arrays. Almost none of these holds a NUL inside the array, so the original reads past the end and the input is dropped as outside the domain. Real strings were barely checked.

Fix: the counterexample search also runs the whole in-domain user workload (`Superoptimizer.cpp`, the `counterexample` lambda). `make test` passes 313/313. Smoke test, strcasecmp seed 11 for 60 s: 6 counterexamples, a correct winner (1.15× in the loop), round trip OK on the eval set.

Scope: the scalar samples of 192–198 are not affected. Their random inputs are in the domain, and the unary workload samples were proved exhaustively.

Rerun: the same 24 searches with the fix, round trip on the 16,384-string `*_eval_large.txt`. Output: `data/superopt_strings_199b_20261116/`. The predictions above apply to the rerun. The first run is kept as the record of the hole.

### 199 (results): no valid gain on eight string functions; every strcasecmp winner is wrong

Raw: `data/superopt_strings_199b_20261116/` (the rerun with the workload counterexamples):
- `summary.tsv` and `self_control.tsv` per workload;
- `remeasure.tsv`: the six non-trivial correct winners, plus self, 3× inline in one session;
- `bytes{1,2}.txt`: a byte-level fuzz set, 20,000 NUL-terminated strings, or pairs, over bytes 1–255. The pairs share a prefix with one bit flipped (70%) or are random. Seed 7.

The first run, with the hole, is in `data/superopt_strings_199_20261116/`.

Winners that are not the original (the rest of the 24 return the original):

| sample | seed | inline in battery | remeasure median (3×) | eval round trip (16,384) | byte fuzz (20,000) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| strcasecmp | 10 | 1.55 | 1.67 | OK | **1,239 wrong** |
| strcasecmp | 11 | 0.96 | — | **15 wrong** | **588 wrong** |
| strcasecmp | 12 | 1.19 | — | **34 wrong** | **2,664 wrong** |
| strlen | 10 / 12 | 1.10 / 1.10 | 1.08 / 1.10 | OK | OK |
| memcmp | 10 | 1.09 | 1.10 | OK | OK |
| sysv_hash | 10 / 11 | 1.07 / 1.06 | 1.08 / 1.07 | OK | OK |

Self-controls in the remeasure: 0.92–1.11 per run (array timing on strings is noisier than scalars).

- **strcasecmp.**
  - Seed 10's loop compares `(a[i] | 32) == (b[i] | 32)`. That equals `tolower` only for letters. For `[` vs `{`, or `@` vs `` ` ``, it continues where the original stops: on `"[" vs "{"` it returns 0, the original −32.
  - Words and identifiers never put such a pair at the same position, so the program passes all 1,024 search pairs, the 6 workload counterexamples and 16,384 eval pairs.
  - Seeds 11 and 12 fail on held-out text too (`"FOUND"` vs `"For"`).
  - Its 1.67× is the speed of a different function.
- **The rest.** strlen (one loop unrolled once), memcmp and sysv_hash hold on the byte fuzz. All are within 1.06–1.10, at the edge of this timing's noise. None makes the 190 rule (≥ 1.10 on 2 seeds).
- **Cost model vs timer.** The model cost fell on 7 of 8 samples (not keyhash), e.g. strcmp 1600 → 920. The in-loop timer returned the original on all seeds for gnu_hash, atoi and strcmp, and on some seeds for strlen, sysv_hash and memcmp. The model's savings are mostly not real time on byte loops.

Predictions:
- all 24 winners pass the round trip — **failed** (2 fail on eval text; 3 are wrong on bytes);
- no result < 0.95 — **held** (lowest 0.96, itself a wrong program);
- 190-rule gains on 0–2 — **held** (0);
- strcasecmp among them — **failed** (its only gains are wrong programs);
- strlen does not gain — **held** (1.08 / 1.10, one seed at the threshold);
- the model cost falls but the timer returns the original on ≥ 4 samples — **held** (6 on some seed, 3 on all seeds).

Reading: the pilot's first result is about correctness, not speed. Verification by tests drawn from the user's workload is not enough for string code.
- The domain is wide: all bytes, any length.
- The workload covers a thin slice of it.
- Random arrays do not reach it, because without a NUL the input is outside the domain.

Fixing the hole (deviation above) removed wrong code on the workload itself, not wrong code outside it. Before string functions can be optimized, the counterexample search needs inputs that are in the domain but off the workload: mutations of workload inputs (bit flips, random bytes, truncation), or random NUL-terminated byte strings. The byte fuzz above caught all three in 20,000 inputs. Until then, a string winner is reported with `equivalence_level tested` and should be treated as unverified.

### 199c (registered): counterexamples off the workload for array functions

Change, for array functions with a `--workload`:
- **`mutateInput` (`Superoptimizer.cpp`).** It makes 1–3 edits to a workload input. Each edit is one of:
  - a random value in the workload's range;
  - one bit flipped;
  - an element set to another element with one bit flipped, or ±1 (near-equal aligned pairs);
  - a copy of another element;
  - a 0 (a shorter string);
  - a constant ±2 of the reference or the candidate.
  
  Inputs the reference rejects are dropped.
- **CEGIS** tries 3,000 mutations after the workload scan.
- **Final check.** A timed array winner must also pass 200,000 mutations, with a fixed seed. Otherwise it is rejected (`fuzz_rejected`) and the next one is tried. A winner that passes is reported as `equivalence_level fuzzed`.

`make test` passes 313/313. Smoke test, seen before registering: strcasecmp 60 s.
- seed 10: a 1.24× winner in the loop, `fuzzed`;
- seed 11: 3 winners refuted, the original returned;
- both pass 199's byte fuzz;
- `evaluated` 15–20% lower than in 199b.

Run: as 199b (8 samples, seeds 10–12, 60 s, `--time-top 4`, the same workloads; round trip and external timing on `*_eval_large.txt`). Output: `data/superopt_strings_199c_20261116/`. Afterwards every non-original winner also runs 199's independent byte fuzz (`data/superopt_strings_199b_20261116/bytes{1,2}.txt`, 20,000 inputs, not used by the search).
Predictions:
- no winner wrong on the eval round trip or the byte fuzz (85%);
- `fuzz_rejected` > 0 in at least 2 runs (80%; the smoke test had 3 in one);
- strcasecmp: a 190-rule gain (≥ 1.10 inline on ≥ 2 seeds) with correct code (35%);
- 190-rule gains in total: 0–1 of 8 (70%);
- no result below 0.95 inline (75%);
- median `evaluated` per sample at least 75% of 199b's (70%).

### 199c (results): no wrong winner; strcasecmp has no correct gain within 60 s; strlen 1.10 at the threshold

Raw: `data/superopt_strings_199c_20261116/`:
- `summary.tsv` per workload, with the new `fuzz_rejected` column;
- `remeasure.tsv`: the non-original winners plus self, 3× inline in one quiet session (self 0.99–1.01).

Results:
- **Correctness.** Six winners are not the original: strlen 10 and 12, sysv_hash 10, 11 and 12, memcmp 11. All are `fuzzed`, and all six pass the round trip on 16,384 eval strings and 199's independent byte fuzz (20,000 inputs). None is wrong. The other 18 return the original.
- **strcasecmp.**
  - The final check rejected 3 timed winners in each of the 3 runs (`fuzz_rejected 3`), and the original was returned.
  - The cheap candidates (model cost 1,543–1,602 vs 2,772) are the `| 32` trick and its variants: the mutations in CEGIS do not catch all of them before timing, and the final check does.
  - In the smoke test, seed 10 found a correct 1.24× winner. In the battery it did not: the timed order varies between runs.
- **Gains, remeasure medians:**

  | winner | median |
  |---|---|
  | strlen 10 | 1.105 |
  | strlen 12 | 1.103 |
  | sysv_hash 10 | 1.100 |
  | sysv_hash 12 | 1.072 |
  | memcmp 11 | 1.079 |

  - strlen meets the 190 rule (≥ 1.10 on 2 seeds), but just. Its two programs are byte-identical to 199b's, which measured 1.08 / 1.10 in that session.
  - The strlen gain is the loop unrolled once, with a second NUL test. sysv_hash's winner is unrolled the same way, and it adds a dead `if (n < 3) return n`: it never runs in the domain, since a NUL must lie inside the array.
- **Inline noise in the battery.** One returned original measured 0.917 (strcmp 12) and another 1.205 (memcmp 10); array self-controls ran 0.95–1.04. Search-made winners: 1.048–1.105.
- **`evaluated`.** Median per sample, 199c / 199b: 0.83 (strcasecmp, the extra CEGIS work on many wrong candidates) to 1.05.

Predictions:
- no winner wrong — **held** (6/6 correct, byte fuzz and eval);
- `fuzz_rejected` > 0 in ≥ 2 runs — **held** (3 runs, all strcasecmp);
- strcasecmp gains with correct code — **failed** (original on all 3 seeds);
- 0–1 samples with a 190-rule gain — **held** (strlen, at the threshold);
- no result below 0.95 inline — **failed**, by the letter: strcmp seed 12 returned the original and measured 0.917, which is battery noise; no search-made winner went below 1.048;
- `evaluated` ≥ 75% of 199b — **held** (lowest 0.83).

Reading:
- The mutation fuzz closes the correctness hole of 199: every winner returned is right on bytes the search never saw. The price is strcasecmp's apparent 1.5×, which was wrong code.
- On these eight byte loops the search finds about 1.1× at most (unrolling). gcc already compiles the originals well. The model cost (e.g. strcmp 1600 → 920) predicts gains the timer does not confirm.
- The string pilot's answer: opening `const char *` is feasible (import, ABI, workloads, now verification), but this search does not yet find real speed in byte loops. What did produce large gains (198b) was branch-free rewriting of branchy scalar code.

### 200 (registered): do the branch-free gains of 198b generalize to unseen real code?

Census of six more code bases (`scripts/c_census.py`, `data/c_census_200_20261116/`):

| code base | functions | import on their own |
|---|---|---|
| zlib 1.3.1 | 457 | 0 |
| lz4 1.10.0 | 295 | 0 |
| SQLite 3.46.0 | 647 | 3 |
| Mbed TLS 3.6.2 | 2,308 | 3 |
| curl 8.11.0 | 810 | 3 |
| libpng 1.6.44 | 1,249 | 2 |
| **total** | **5,766** | **11 (0.19%)** |

What blocks the rest:
- "type not supported" (1,564): mostly structs and their pointers (`mbedtls_mpi`, `sqlite3_file`, `png_structp`), not integer typedefs (`u8` 12);
- string literals (1,599, "unexpected character `\"`");
- preprocessor directives (1,045).

Scalar integer functions are rare in real code. Of the 11, two are identity stubs. The other 9 are copied verbatim into `tests/c_samples_real2/` (licenses in `LICENSES.md`):
- with branches: curl `is_cr_or_lf`, Mbed TLS `hex_to_int` and `nibble_to_hex_digit`, SQLite `memsys3Roundup`;
- straight-line: curl `smb_swap16` / `smb_swap32`, Mbed TLS `aes_rot_word`, libpng `first_transform` / `is_combo`.

All 9 round-trip. Hand versions are in `hand/`, each round-trip-checked against the original on 20,000 inputs:
- `(c == '\r') | (c == '\n')`;
- a 256-entry table for `hex_to_int`;
- `i + '0' + (-(i >= 10) & 7)`;
- clamp-then-formula for `memsys3Roundup`;
- `__builtin_bswap16/32` for the swaps;
- the originals for the other three.

Workloads (`scripts/make_int_workloads.py`, new; search, eval and 65,536-input eval_large):
- is_cr_or_lf on text, and hex_to_int on hex digits (the 195 files);
- nibble_to_hex_digit on uniform 0–15;
- memsys3Roundup on log-uniform allocation sizes 1–65,536 (22% at most 12);
- random for the rest.

Run: seeds 10–12, 60 s, `--time-top 4`, `data/superopt_real2_200_20261116/`. Then three ceiling repetitions in the same session: `DIR=tests/c_samples_real2 LIST=… RUNS=…`; `run_ceiling_battery.sh` now takes `DIR` and `LIST`.
Predictions:
- every winner equivalent; all are unary, so exhaustive proofs (95%);
- no search result below 0.95 in the ceiling medians (80%);
- 190-rule gain (≥ 1.10 on ≥ 2 seeds) on is_cr_or_lf (40%: on text both compares are almost always false, so the short circuit predicts well);
- no search gain on hex_to_int (65%, as hexval in 198), with the hand table ≥ 5× (75%);
- no gain on nibble_to_hex_digit (60%: gcc likely emits a `cmov` already);
- memsys3Roundup gains (40%);
- none of the five straight-line functions gains (90%), and their hand versions are within 0.95–1.05 (70%: gcc recognizes bswap and rotate idioms);
- samples with a gain: 1–3 of 9 (55%).

### 200 (results): one real gain in unseen code (is_cr_or_lf); the int-array timer misstates char functions

Raw: `data/superopt_real2_200_20261116/`:
- `{text,hex,nibble,sizes,random}/summary.tsv`;
- `ceiling_c{1,2,3}.tsv` and `ceiling_median.tsv` (self, hand and the three winners, inline on 65,536 inputs, one session; self 0.99–1.01);
- `byte_caller_198b.txt`.

Correctness: three winners differ from the original. All are proved on all 2^32 arguments (`exhaustive`); the other 24 are the original.

| sample | workload | hand | search 10 / 11 / 12 | hand / best search |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| is_cr_or_lf | text | 1.00 | **1.41 / 2.19 / 1.41** | 0.46 |
| hex_to_int | hex | **53.76** | 1.00 / 1.00 / 1.00 | **53.8** (table) |
| memsys3Roundup | sizes | **3.97** | 1.00 / 1.44 / 1.00 | **2.75** |
| nibble_to_hex_digit | nibble | 0.87 | 1.00 / 1.00 / 1.00 | 0.87 |
| smb_swap16 / smb_swap32 / aes_rot_word / first_transform / is_combo | random | 1.00 / 1.00 / 0.99 / 1.00 / 1.00 | 1.00–1.01 | ≈ 1 |

Ceiling medians (inline on the int-array harness).
- **memsys3Roundup**, seed 11: `if (n <= 4) return 12;` (the formula already gives 12 for 5–12), so the branch is taken less often. My clamp-then-formula version (a `cmov`) is 3.97×. It can be written in Mini-C (the select move), so the gap is reach.
- **The straight-line functions:** gcc already recognizes the bswap and rotate idioms (hand `__builtin_bswap` = 1.00).

**is_cr_or_lf, and a measurement artifact.** Seed 10 is `(c == 13) | (c == 10)`, the same as my hand version, and compiles to the same function. Yet it measured 1.41× and the hand version 1.00×, on all three repetitions (0.56 vs 0.79 ns).
- The cause is the timing loop. The harness passes each `char` argument from an `int` array. For the original and the hand version, gcc inlines a load plus 8-bit compares (`cmp $0xd,%cl`). For the search's version, which spells out the sign extension, it emits `movsbl` and 32-bit compares, which run faster in that loop.
- A real caller passes bytes from a buffer. New `scripts/c_byte_caller.py` inlines each version into a loop over a `const char *` buffer of the eval text. There:
  - seed 10 is **1.00×** and the hand version 1.00×;
  - seed 12 is 1.25×;
  - seed 11 (`if (c >= 14) return 0;` first, then the two compares) is **1.53–1.65×**: on text almost every byte is ≥ 14, so the early exit predicts well.
  
  The gain is real and tuned to the workload, the same kind as 197b's isspace.

**The same check on 198b's character winners** (byte caller vs the int-array ceiling of 198b, same eval files):

| sample | int harness: search 10 / 11 / 12; hand | byte caller: search; hand |
|---|---|---|
| isalpha | 1.81 / 1.81 / 1.80; 1.80 | 1.10 / 1.08 / 1.10; 1.08 |
| iscntrl | 1.46 / 1.19 / 1.46; 1.19 | 1.38 / 1.22 / 1.39; 1.22 |
| isspace | 1.10 / 1.10 / 1.10; 0.35 | 1.06 / 1.06 / 1.06; 0.53 |
| ascii_is_unsafe | 3.27 / 3.58 / 3.58; 0.97 | 2.16 / 2.15 / 2.15; 0.94 |
| ascii_to_bin | 1.04 / 1.00 / 1.00; 0.42 | 1.00 / 1.26 / 1.29; 1.22 |
| hexval (table) | 1.00; 21.9 | 1.00; 8.8 |

- The ranking mostly survives: ascii_is_unsafe and iscntrl are real gains.
- The magnitudes do not: isalpha's 1.8× is mostly the harness.
- In one case the direction flips: ascii_to_bin gains 1.26–1.29× only in the byte caller, and my hand version goes from 0.42× to 1.22×.

For char-typed functions, "faster" depends on how the caller feeds the argument. The search's in-loop timer, like `c_speedup.py`, uses the int-array context.

Predictions:
- every winner equivalent, proved — **held** (3/3 `exhaustive`);
- no search result < 0.95 — **held** (lowest 1.00);
- is_cr_or_lf gains — **held** (2 seeds in both contexts);
- no gain on hex_to_int — **held**; its table ≥ 5× — **held** (53.8×; 16.6× in the byte caller);
- no gain on nibble_to_hex_digit — **held**;
- memsys3Roundup gains — **failed** (1.44 on one seed only);
- no straight-line function gains, and their hand versions within 0.95–1.05 — **held**;
- 1–3 samples with a gain — **held** (1).

Reading:
- Real code offers few whole scalar functions: 9 of 5,766 functions here, 4 of them branchy. The search's results on them repeat 198b's pattern. One branchy function gains through a branch on the input's typical values (is_cr_or_lf, 1.5–1.65×). One gap is reachable in Mini-C (memsys3Roundup, 2.75). Tables (hex_to_int) stay out of reach. Straight-line code is already what gcc emits.
- Two consequences for the next steps:
  - Whole functions are too rare a unit. The next unit is scalar regions (an expression or branch inside a larger function, e.g. a ctype test inside a parsing loop).
  - The timing context matters as much as the input distribution. Character functions should be timed in a byte-buffer caller, inside the search as well as outside it.

## 201. (registered) Byte-buffer timing context inside the search for byte-input functions

Change: `algosearch superopt --timing-context byte`. When the function is unary and every timing and confirmation input fits in a byte, the in-loop timer and the confirmation timer read each argument from a byte buffer (`unsigned char`, or `signed char` if a value is negative): `acc += fn(h_u8[i])`. The default `int` context reads it from an int array (`h_data[h_off[i]]`), as before. This is the context of `scripts/c_byte_caller.py` (log 200). The output reports `timing_context int|uchar|schar`. The default stays `int` until this test.

Smoke test (is_cr_or_lf, seed 10, 10 s): `timing_context uchar`, self-controls 1.000 / 1.000, winner `exhaustive`.

Samples: the 9 byte-workload unary samples of 198b and 200, each with its own search workload:
- musl isalpha, iscntrl, isspace (text);
- musl ascii_is_unsafe, ascii_to_bin (b64);
- musl hexval (hex);
- curl is_cr_or_lf (text), mbedtls hex_to_int (hex) and nibble_to_hex_digit (nibble), from `tests/c_samples_real2/workloads/`.

Arms: `int` against `byte`. Seeds 10–12, 60 s, `--time-top 4`. Both arms run in this session, alternating by workload group, with `run_superopt_battery.sh` (round trip on random inputs, the int-context `c_speedup.py` on the eval file).
Evaluation: `c_byte_caller.py` on the `*_eval_large.txt` file of the workload (65,536 bytes of other text or uniform characters). Each sample is compiled into one program that holds:
- the original, plus the original again as the self-control;
- the hand version;
- int winners 10–12 and byte winners 10–12.

That program runs 3 times. Per winner, the median of the 3 repetitions is the speedup. Gain = ≥ 1.10 on ≥ 2 seeds (190 rule, measured in the byte caller). Output: `data/superopt_bytectx_201_20261116/`.

Predictions:
1. All 54 winners equivalent (round trip) and verified (95%).
2. Byte arm: no winner < 0.95 in the byte caller (80%).
3. Samples with a gain: byte arm ≥ int arm (75%), and byte strictly more on at least one sample (40%).
4. Per sample, the byte arm's median over seeds is ≥ the int arm's − 0.05 on ≥ 8 of 9 samples (70%).
5. ascii_to_bin: byte arm gains (≥ 1.10 on ≥ 2 seeds) (55%; int-arm winners 11 and 12 of 198b were 1.26–1.29 in the byte caller, by accident).
6. isalpha: no arm gains (≥ 1.10 on ≥ 2 seeds) (55%; 1.08–1.10 in 200).
7. The in-loop `measured_speedup` predicts the byte-caller speedup better in the byte arm: the median of |log(measured / byte caller)| over non-reference winners is smaller than the int arm's (75%).
8. Self-controls in the byte context are within 0.97–1.03 (85%).

Reading rule: if 3 and 7 hold, `byte` becomes the default for byte-input unary functions. If 7 holds but 3 does not, it becomes the default for honest measurement only, since it does not find more. If 7 fails, the byte context does not track the realistic caller, and it stays opt-in.

### 201 (results): the byte context does not find more and mis-times hex_to_int; the byte caller had a position bias, so 200's re-time is corrected

Raw: `data/superopt_bytectx_201_20261116/`:
- `{int,byte}/<group>/`: searches, `summary.tsv`, `self_control.tsv`;
- `byte_caller_r{1,2,3}.txt`: the registered evaluation, invalid (see below);
- `byte_caller_a{1,2,3}.txt`: the evaluation with the aligned caller;
- `byte_caller_median.tsv`: medians.

All 54 winners verified, round trip EQUIVALENT, levels `exhaustive` or `reference`. `timing_context` was `uchar` in all 27 byte runs. The byte arm's in-loop self-controls are 0.998–1.003.

**Deviation: the evaluator was biased.** In the registered evaluation (`byte_caller_r*`), the original's second copy measured 0.94× against the first in ascii_is_unsafe and 0.78× in ascii_to_bin. Four copies of ascii_to_bin in one `c_byte_caller.py` program measured 1.00 / 1.00 / 1.22 / 1.27, with identical code at different addresses. `c_byte_caller.py` compiled without alignment. It now uses `-falign-functions=64 -falign-loops=64`, as the in-loop timer does. With alignment, 6 copies of ascii_to_bin are within 0.99–1.01, and 6 copies each of is_cr_or_lf and ascii_is_unsafe are within 0.996–1.001. The evaluation was rerun the same way, three times, with a second copy of the original added last as a positional control (`byte_caller_a*`; both controls 1.00 on every sample). The rerun is the result.

Medians of 3 in the aligned byte caller:

| sample | hand | int 10 / 11 / 12 | byte 10 / 11 / 12 | in-loop: int / byte |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| isalpha | 1.36 | 1.36 / 1.36 / 1.36 | 1.36 / 1.36 / 1.36 | 1.42 / 1.36 |
| iscntrl | 1.22 | 1.39 / 1.19 / 1.40 | 1.22 / 1.39 / 1.22 | 1.26, 1.12, 1.26 / 1.22, 1.39, 1.22 |
| isspace | 0.53 | 1.06 ×3 | 1.06 ×3 | 1.06 / 1.06 |
| ascii_is_unsafe | 1.00 | 2.28 / 2.27 / 2.28 | 2.27 / 2.27 / 1.85 | 2.54–2.57 / 2.21–2.74 |
| ascii_to_bin | 0.71 | 1.00 / 1.00 (ref) / 1.00 (ref) | 1.05 ×3 | 1.04 / 1.20 |
| hexval | 8.65 | ref ×3 | 0.99–1.00 (ref) | — |
| is_cr_or_lf | 1.00 | 1.61 / 1.64 / 1.00 | 1.00 (ref) / 1.64 / 1.24 | 1.43, 1.32, 1.24 / —, 1.41, 1.14 |
| hex_to_int | 16.55 | ref ×3 | **0.88 ×3** | — / **1.15** |
| nibble_to_hex_digit | 0.40 | ref ×3 | ref ×3 | — |

Gains, by the 190 rule in the byte caller: **4 samples in each arm**, the same 4: isalpha, iscntrl, ascii_is_unsafe and is_cr_or_lf.

hex_to_int: the byte arm returned the same 59-node program on all 3 seeds, an if-chain with `&` and `==` in place of `&&`. The in-loop byte timer measured it at 1.15×, and an external rebuild of that harness on the eval file reproduces 1.148× (self 1.000). The aligned byte caller measures 0.88×, the int context 0.98×. Two byte-buffer callers differ by 30% on the same code and inputs. The differences between them are a global buffer and bound with an outer repeat loop, against a pointer and length called through a function pointer. For branchy code near 1 ns per byte, that is enough to flip the sign.

Where the in-loop number tracks the byte caller, it does so in both arms (isalpha, isspace, and iscntrl to the second decimal in the byte arm). The byte arm loses iscntrl and is_cr_or_lf on seeds 10 and 12 by picking a weaker variant, and it measured those variants correctly. That is the search's seed-to-seed spread, not mis-timing. is_cr_or_lf byte seed 10 returned the reference. Its emitted reference measured 1.00× in the byte context against 1.24× in the int context. That is the 200 artifact, removed as intended.

Predictions:
1. 54 winners equivalent and verified — **held**.
2. Byte arm: no winner < 0.95 — **failed** (hex_to_int 0.88 on 3 seeds; the int arm has none).
3. Samples with a gain: byte ≥ int — **held** (4 = 4); byte strictly more — **failed**.
4. Byte median ≥ int median − 0.05 on ≥ 8/9 — **failed** (6/9: iscntrl 1.22 vs 1.39, is_cr_or_lf 1.24 vs 1.61, hex_to_int 0.88 vs 1.00).
5. ascii_to_bin gains in the byte arm — **failed** (1.05).
6. isalpha: no arm gains — **failed** (1.36 in both: 200's 1.10 was the position bias).
7. In-loop speedup closer to the byte caller in the byte arm — **failed** (median |log| 0.112 against 0.077 for the int arm).
8. Byte self-controls within 0.97–1.03 — **held**.

3 of 8 held.

Reading, by the registered rule (7 fails): the byte context stays opt-in, and the default stays `int`. The byte buffer removes one artifact (is_cr_or_lf's emitted reference 1.24×). But a byte-buffer timer is not the realistic caller: a second byte-buffer caller disagrees with it by up to 30% on branchy code. The search exploits whichever context it is timed in. Speedups below about 1.2× on byte functions are therefore within the spread between callers. The gains that hold in every context measured here (int harness, both byte callers) are isalpha 1.36×, iscntrl 1.39×, ascii_is_unsafe 2.3× and is_cr_or_lf 1.6×. A winner timed in the int context as well as the byte context, and accepted only if both are above the threshold, would have rejected hex_to_int (0.98 in int) and ascii_to_bin (1.01–1.03).

### 200 (correction): the byte-caller re-time had a position bias

The 200 byte-caller numbers were measured without alignment. Aligned, on the same samples and the 201 int-arm winners:
- isalpha is 1.36× (not 1.10×);
- ascii_to_bin's int-arm winners are 1.00× (not 1.26–1.29×), and the hand version 0.71× (not 1.22×);
- ascii_is_unsafe is 2.28×, iscntrl 1.39×, is_cr_or_lf 1.61–1.64×, hexval's table 8.65×.

The 200 claim "ascii_to_bin gains only in the byte caller" is withdrawn. "isalpha's 1.8× is mostly the harness" becomes "1.4–1.8× in the int harnesses, 1.36× in a byte caller". The claim that identical assembly measured 1.41× against 1.00× still stands: it compared contexts, not positions.

## 202. (registered) Cross-context confirmation: a winner must gain both in a byte buffer and from an int array

Change: `superopt --cross-context-confirm` (opt-in). It applies to unary functions whose timing and confirmation inputs all fit in a byte (the 201 rule).
- The final confirmation times each candidate winner (top 3) on the confirmation inputs in the search's context, and also in the other one: byte buffer if the search times in `int`, int array if it times in `byte`. Each gets 3 × 21 trials, median.
- A winner whose other-context speedup is not above the noise threshold is rejected (`cross_context_rejected`). Otherwise its speedup is the smaller of the two.
- The noise threshold is that of the search's context. The other context's self-control is reported (`cross_context_self_control`), as is the returned winner's speedup there (`cross_context_speedup`).

Smoke test: hex_to_int, `--timing-context byte`, seed 10, 20 s. The three timed winners (1.035 in byte) measured 1.008 in int; all were rejected and the reference returned.

Samples, seeds, budget and workloads as in 201 (9 byte samples, seeds 10–12, 60 s, `--time-top 4`). Arms, run in this session in rotating order per workload group:
- A: `int` (the default);
- B: `int --cross-context-confirm`;
- C: `byte --cross-context-confirm`.

Evaluation per winner, in two external contexts:
- byte: the aligned `c_byte_caller.py`, with self-controls first and last, 3 repetitions, median;
- int: the battery's `c_speedup.py --inline` on the eval workload.

Robust gain: min(byte, int) ≥ 1.10 on ≥ 2 seeds. Regression: either context < 0.95. Output: `data/superopt_crossctx_202_20261116/`.

Predictions:
1. All 81 winners verified and round trip EQUIVALENT (95%).
2. B and C: no regression (80%); A: none (70%; the 201 int arm had none).
3. B and C each keep the 4 robust gains of 201 (isalpha, iscntrl, ascii_is_unsafe, is_cr_or_lf) (65%).
4. C does not return the 201 hex_to_int program (0.88 / 0.98) on any seed (85%).
5. ascii_to_bin: no robust gain in any arm (75%).
6. `cross_context_rejected` > 0 in ≥ 3 of the 54 B and C runs (70%).

Reading rule:
- If B or C has no regression and as many robust gains as A, `--cross-context-confirm` becomes the default for byte-input unary functions. Its timing context becomes the default with it: `byte` if C has more robust gains than B, `int` otherwise.
- If both lose a robust gain of A, it stays opt-in.

### 202 (results): cross-context confirmation removes no regression and loses is_cr_or_lf; the in-loop byte timer misjudges in both directions

Raw: `data/superopt_crossctx_202_20261116/{A,B,C}/<group>/` (searches, `summary.tsv`); `byte_caller_a{1,2,3}.txt` (aligned byte caller, original first and last). All 81 winners verified, round trip EQUIVALENT. Self-controls: both byte-caller copies 1.00–1.02; the other context's in-loop self-control 0.997–1.004.

Robust gain (min of byte caller and `c_speedup.py --inline` ≥ 1.10 on ≥ 2 seeds):

| sample | A int | B int+cross | C byte+cross |
|---|---|---|---|
| isalpha | yes (1.36 / 1.81 ×3) | yes (×3) | yes (2 seeds; seed 12 see below) |
| iscntrl | yes (1.39, 1.19, 1.39) | yes (1.39, 1.19, 1.19) | yes (1.22 / 1.19 ×3) |
| ascii_is_unsafe | yes (2.27, 2.27, 1.82) | yes (same) | yes (2.27, 2.27, 2.30) |
| is_cr_or_lf | **yes** (1.61, 1.49 / 1.82, 1.93) | **no** (one seed: 1.64) | **no** (one seed: 1.49) |
| the other 5 | no | no | no |

Regressions (either context < 0.95): none in any arm. `cross_context_rejected` > 0 in 9 of the 54 B and C runs:
- ascii_to_bin (1 B, 3 C);
- hex_to_int (3 C): the 201 program was rejected each time, and the reference returned;
- is_cr_or_lf (2 B): the int winners, rejected because the in-loop byte timer saw no gain.

**is_cr_or_lf: the in-loop byte timer misses a robust gain.** A's seed-10 winner measures 1.61× in the byte caller and 1.82× from an int array. The rebuilt in-loop byte harness gives it **1.00×** (1.42× in the in-loop int harness). B's seed-11 winner: in-loop byte 1.035×, byte caller 1.64×. In 201 the same timer measured hex_to_int at 1.15× against 0.88× in the byte caller. So it is wrong in both directions, by 30–60%. The original compiles to the same branch-free `sete`/`or` loop in the harness and in the caller (1.18 ns per byte in both). The candidate is what differs: 0.73 ns per byte in the caller, 1.18 in the harness. Cross-context confirmation inherits this error. It removed hex_to_int, but A in the int context never returned hex_to_int in the first place. It also removed a real gain.

**Bug found (pre-existing): a confirmed gain of the re-emitted reference was discarded.** When the timed winner is the reference program itself, `algosearch superopt` printed `returned original` and wrote the original source, although the emitted reference had been confirmed faster (`measured_speedup` 1.36, level `exhaustive`). Cases:
- 202: C isalpha seed 12 (1.36× in the loop, written as the original: 1.00 outside);
- 202: B isspace seed 10 (1.06×);
- 198b: zeroinfnan seed 10.

Fixed in `CImportTool.cpp`: the original is written only when no winner was confirmed (`equivalence_level reference`). Neither 202 case changes a robust-gain verdict.

Predictions:
1. 81 equivalent and verified — **held**.
2. No regression in B and C, nor in A — **held**.
3. B and C keep the 4 robust gains — **failed** (both lose is_cr_or_lf).
4. C does not return the 201 hex_to_int program — **held** (rejected on 3/3 seeds).
5. ascii_to_bin: no robust gain anywhere — **held**.
6. `cross_context_rejected` > 0 in ≥ 3 runs — **held** (9).

5 of 6 held.

Reading, by the registered rule (both cross arms lose a robust gain of A): `--cross-context-confirm` stays opt-in. The default stays `int` without cross confirmation. Over 201 and 202 it has no regression in 54 runs and the most robust gains (4 of 4). The byte harness inside the loop is the weak link: a byte buffer is the realistic caller, but this harness's version of it does not predict another byte-buffer caller. A gate that consults it rejects real gains as readily as false ones. Closed for now: the timing context is not where the search loses gains on these samples. The remaining gaps (hexval and hex_to_int tables, and the 198b reach gaps) are about what the search can construct.

## 203. (registered) Reach: a branch-merge move and a divisibility-split move

The three gaps the search could reach in Mini-C (198b, 200): memsys3Roundup (hand 3.97×, search 1.44× on one seed), is_leap (hand 2.43×, search 1.30×) and setup_salt (hand 9.1×, search 1.3×). Change: `superopt --reach-moves` (opt-in). It adds two phase 3c moves; the five old moves and the reach moves are drawn uniformly, so each old move drops from 1/5 to 1/7 of the algorithmic edits.
- **Branch merge (5):** `if (c) R = K; else R = E(x)` → `if (c) x = a; R = E(x)`, with `a` one of K or a constant of c ± 1. R is a variable or `arr[k]`, with an optional `return` after it in both branches. Tests reject a wrong `a`. memsys3Roundup: `n <= 12 ? 12 : ((n+11)&~7)-4` becomes the hand version's clamp `if (n <= 12) n = 12;`, and the existing select move can then make it branch-free.
- **Divisibility split (6), exact:** `x % (2^p·b) == 0` → `(x % 2^p == 0) & (x % b == 0)`, and `!= 0` → `|`, for odd b > 1. is_leap: `y % 400` → `y % 16`, `y % 25`. `y % 100` → `y % 4`, `y % 25`, whose `% 4` term is then redundant under the `y % 4 == 0` guard (removable by the existing hoist edit). The existing strength reduction turns `% 16` into `& 15`.
- **Structural timing (registered after a smoke test, before the run):** the model cost does not see mispredictions. Here the merged clamp (3.97× by hand, emitted from Mini-C) costs more than the beam's cost-324 equivalents and drops out of the beam of 40 before anything times it. Two 60 s smokes found the clamp without being able to time it (best 1.10×, 306,413 evaluated). So `--reach-moves` also times one correct mutant of a phase 3c move per generation, taken from the fresh candidates before the beam is cut. With it, memsys3Roundup smokes, seeds 13 and 14: 2.59× and 2.57× in the loop, `exhaustive`; is_leap seed 13: reference. The arm therefore tests the moves and this timing together.
- Unit tests: the merge reaches an exact clamp on memsys3Roundup; the split is exact on 17 inputs, signs and extremes included, and refuses an odd modulus. Without `--reach-moves` the search is unchanged: same draws, no extra timing.

**setup_salt is out of scope** (measured before the run, `c_speedup.py --inline`, 65,536 random inputs, same session):

| version | speedup |
|---|---|
| hand (bswap + 3 swap stages) | 9.13× |
| search 198b, 4× unrolled | 1.32× |
| shift-in loop `r = r << 1 | x & 1` | 1.30× |
| branch-free loop body | 0.97× |

The original's `if` does not mispredict: gcc already compiles it branch-free. Its cost is the 24-step serial chain. The gap therefore needs the bit-reversal closed form: a move that recognizes a bit permutation and builds a swap network. That is an idiom, not a local edit, and is not attempted here.

Samples: all 36 (27 musl/Lua + 9 real2), seeds 10–12, 60 s, `--time-top 4`, timing context `int`, with the workloads of 198b and 200:
- musl: text, b64, hex;
- real2: text, hex, nibble, sizes;
- random for the rest.

Arms: control (defaults) vs `--reach-moves`, alternating order per group in this session. Measurement: the battery's `c_speedup.py --inline` on the `*_eval_large.txt` files, or on 65,536 random inputs, plus the battery's self-controls. Gain: 190 rule (inline ≥ 1.10 on ≥ 2 seeds). Output: `data/superopt_reach_203_20261117/`.

Predictions:
1. All 216 winners verified and EQUIVALENT (95%).
2. memsys3Roundup: reach ≥ 2.0× on ≥ 2 seeds (60%); control above 1.5× on at most 1 seed (70%).
3. is_leap: reach ≥ 1.5× on ≥ 2 seeds (35%).
4. Samples with a gain: reach ≥ control − 1 (75%).
5. No winner below 0.95 in either arm (75%).
6. setup_salt within 1.2–1.45× in both arms (85%).

Reading rule: `--reach-moves` becomes the default if 2 or 3 holds (a gap closed), and 4 and 5 hold. If no gap closes, the moves stay opt-in, and the gaps are a matter of search effort (budget, selection), not missing moves.

### 203 (results): memsys3Roundup closed (3.96× on 3/3 seeds), 12 samples gain against 9, no regression; is_leap not reached; `--reach-moves` becomes the default

Raw: `data/superopt_reach_203_20261117/{control,reach}/<group>/` (searches, `summary.tsv`, `self_control.tsv`). All 216 winners verified. External self-controls (inline): 0.996–1.007.

Round trip: 18 rows are NOT_EQUIVALENT, the same as in 198b, and all in both arms:
- rol and ror: the reference returned; the round trip drops > 50% of random inputs as undefined (shift counts);
- days_in_month: the domain is a third of random inputs. All 6 winners pass `c_roundtrip.py --min-compared 0` (506 compared, 0 mismatches).

No wrong winner.

Gains (190 rule, `c_speedup.py --inline` on 65,536 inputs, three seeds):

| sample | control 10 / 11 / 12 | reach 10 / 11 / 12 |
|---|---|---|
| **memsys3Roundup** | 1.00 / 1.44 / 1.00 | **3.96 / 3.97 / 3.96** (hand 3.97) |
| **swapc** | 1.00 ×3 | **4.07 / 6.45 / 6.75** (see below) |
| **filterpc** | 13.72 / 1.00 / 1.00 | 12.13 / 1.16 / 1.24 |
| days_in_month | 1.00 / 1.83 / 1.84 | 2.33 / 2.09 / 2.05 |
| is_leap | 1.31 / 1.00 / 1.00 | 1.29 / 1.00 / 1.00 |
| setup_salt | 1.31 / 1.26 / 1.32 | 1.26 / 1.25 / 1.31 |
| ascii_is_unsafe | 2.75 / 3.59 / 2.76 | 2.75 / 3.27 / 3.51 |
| is_cr_or_lf | 1.41 / 2.16 / 1.41 | 1.41 ×3 |

Samples with a gain:
- control: 9 (is_cr_or_lf, ascii_is_unsafe, days_in_month, isalpha, isblank, iscntrl, isspace, setup_salt, zeroinfnan);
- reach: 12 (the same 9 plus memsys3Roundup, swapc, filterpc).

No winner below 0.95 in either arm.

- **memsys3Roundup** is closed. All three reach winners are the hand version's clamp (`if (n <= 12) n = 12;`, then the one expression, a `cmov`), proved on all 2^32 arguments. The in-loop timer measured 2.55–2.59×. Without the structural timing the clamp was produced but never timed (registration smoke).
- **swapc: a context gain, not an algorithm.** The reach winners branch on `c` and compute the byte swap in a form gcc emits as `bswap`, with dead code after it. All three were checked against the original on all 2^32 `x` for 7 values of `c`: 0 mismatches. Compiled alone, the winner and the original are the same 3 instructions (`bswap`, `test`, `cmove`). Inlined into the measurement loop they differ: 4–6.7× in `c_speedup.py --inline`, 1.58–1.70× in the in-loop timer. The hand `__builtin_bswap32` version also measured 4.34× there (198b). So the gain is real in a loop that inlines the call, with a size that depends on that loop: the 200/201 lesson, on an int function.
- filterpc gains on 2 seeds (control: 1). days_in_month gains on 3 (control: 2).
- **is_leap is not reached.** No reach winner contains `% 25` or `% 16`: the split was applied but never kept. A single split leaves a version with more divisions under the same branches. It only pays after a removal and a strength reduction, and no intermediate step measures faster.
- Noise outlier: reach islower seed 10 returned the original unchanged, and its inline measurement was 1.33×. One inline measurement can be off by a third on a sub-ns function. The two-seed rule absorbs it.

Predictions:
1. 216 verified and EQUIVALENT — **held**, read as no wrong winner. The 18 round-trip failures are the known domain limits, the same as in 198b and the same in both arms; days_in_month passes with `--min-compared 0`.
2. memsys3Roundup reach ≥ 2.0× on ≥ 2 seeds — **held** (3/3); control above 1.5× on ≤ 1 seed — **held** (0).
3. is_leap ≥ 1.5× on ≥ 2 seeds — **failed**.
4. Gains reach ≥ control − 1 — **held** (12 vs 9).
5. No winner < 0.95 — **held**.
6. setup_salt within 1.2–1.45× in both arms — **held**.

5 of 6 held.

Reading, by the registered rule (2, 4 and 5 hold): **`--reach-moves` is the default** (`--no-reach-moves` restores 202). The structural timing did as much as the new moves. A rewrite the model cannot rank (branch → clamp) needs to be timed to be kept, and timing one structural mutant per generation is enough to find it on 3/3 seeds. The 198b gaps now stand at:
- memsys3Roundup closed;
- is_leap open (a multi-step rewrite with no improving intermediate);
- setup_salt open (an idiom);
- hexval and hex_to_int open (tables, not in Mini-C).
